### [1] [Knowledge-guided Disentanglement with Atomic Actions for Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26097)
*Tianci Wu,Siqi Cao,Guangming Zhu,Jiang Lu,Siyuan Wang,Longfei Zhang,Jincai Huang,Jun Sheng,Liang Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Knowledge-guided Disentanglement with Atomic Actions (KDA) decomposes actions into atomic actions via LLMs, injects this knowledge into video features with a Knowledge Injection Module (KIM), and disentangles it with a Knowledge Disentanglement Module (KDM) using a KD Loss, achieving state-of-the-art results and strong generality across methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex scenes with multiple concurrent fine-grained actions are hard to model with holistic representations; there is a need for explicit, spatial-temporal semantic guidance to improve disentanglement and recognition.

Method: Use Large Language Models to decompose action labels into atomic actions. A Knowledge Injection Module integrates atomic action knowledge into video features; a Knowledge Disentanglement Module further disentangles atomic action knowledge to provide semantic guidance for action disentanglement; a Knowledge Disentanglement Loss encourages clearer separation of knowledge components; the approach is modular and transferable to other methods.

Result: Empirical results show improved feature discriminability and state-of-the-art performance on multi-label action recognition benchmarks; KIM and KDM demonstrate strong generality and can be integrated into other methods.

Conclusion: KDA provides finer-grained semantic guidance for action recognition through atomic-action knowledge and explicit disentanglement, yielding robust performance gains and broad applicability.

Abstract: Action recognition in complex scenes often involves multiple concurrent fine-grained actions, making it challenging to model internal action structures. Most existing methods rely on holistic representations, which are insufficient for capturing subtle interactions and fine-grained semantics. While recent prompt-based approaches introduce disentanglement, they lack explicit semantic guidance, and methods based solely on visual or structured cues remain coarse-grained. In this paper, we propose Knowledge-guided Disentanglement with Atomic Actions (KDA), which leverages fine-grained semantic knowledge to enhance action representations and enable more precise disentanglement. Specifically, we use Large Language Models (LLMs) to decompose action labels into atomic actions, providing explicit spatial-temporal semantics. A Knowledge Injection Module (KIM) first integrates atomic action knowledge into video features. Based on this enhanced representation, a Knowledge Disentanglement Module (KDM) further disentangles atomic action knowledge to produce more precise semantic guidance for action disentanglement. A Knowledge Disentanglement Loss (KD Loss) is introduced to encourage clearer disentanglement of knowledge components within KDM. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KDA improves feature discriminability and achieves state-of-the-art performance on multi-label action recognition benchmarks. Moreover, KIM and KDM can be readily integrated into other methods, demonstrating strong generality.

</details>


### [2] [Weight and Height Estimation from a Single Human Image Captured in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26104)
*Hira Yaseen,Arif Mahmood,Waqas Sultani*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes automatic BMI estimation from a single image using deep networks with multiple modalities; introduces a new wild BMI dataset (6105 images); finds full-body images outperform half-body and face images for BMI prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BMI is a valuable health indicator, but automatic estimation from in-the-wild images is challenging due to pose, occlusions, backgrounds, and data scarcity of full-body BMI datasets.

Method: Employs single and multi-task deep learning with modalities RGB, depth maps, pose-affinity maps, and edge maps to predict BMI, weight, and height. Evaluates on a newly collected in-the-wild dataset of 6105 images with ground-truth height, weight, and BMI. Uses CNN backbones such as VGG, DenseNet, and ResNet on full-body, half-body, and face crops.

Result: Full-body images yield better BMI estimation performance than half-body or face images in the wild across tested CNN backbones; the study demonstrates feasibility of multi-modal and multi-task BMI estimation and highlights data collection as a key factor.

Conclusion: Full-body imagery is most informative for BMI estimation in unconstrained settings. The paper contributes a new in-the-wild BMI dataset and demonstrates the value of multi-modal and multi-task learning for predicting BMI, weight, and height.

Abstract: A person's physical characteristics such as weight and height are important indicators of his physical and mental health, daily life routines and finances. Body Mass Index (BMI) is a well known measure that encodes the characteristics of both the weight and the height. BMI has been used as a self-monitoring tool, and it has long-term implications on one's life. For example, it may help predicting the risk of various diseases and estimating longevity. Automatic BMI estimation using a single person image in the wild is a challenging task due to wide variations in human pose, camera geometry, personal appearance and distracting backgrounds. In this paper, we explore the performance of deep neural networks using single and multi-task learning by employing different modalities including RGB, depth-maps, pose-affinity maps, and edge-maps to predict BMI, weight, and height from daily life images available on social networking websites. Currently, no full body image dataset for BMI estimation is publicly available, therefore we propose a new dataset consisting of 6105 images with ground truth labels of height, weight and BMI. Our proposed dataset is collected in the wild containing images from various ethnicity and distributed over varying age groups and gender. It consists of frontal, back, full and half body, side poses, mirror selfies with varying backgrounds and scale variations and may contain artifacts hiding partial or full face. Extensive experimentation is performed using full body, half body and face images only using different CNN backbones including VGG, Densenet and ResNet. Our experimental results demonstrate that full body images have produced better results than the other half body and facial images in the wild.

</details>


### [3] [TraceCLIP: Recovering Local Semantics from Patch-to-CLS Contributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26107)
*Xinran Liu,Shouqian Shi,Yutong Chen,Ge Wang,Xin-Wei Yao,Sheng Zhong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TraceCLIP is a training-free framework that recovers patch-level semantic evidence from CLS attention in CLIP, enabling zero-shot dense segmentation without extra supervision; it yields consistent mIoU gains (1.3–4.5) on eight benchmarks across backbones and background settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CLIP trains with a global image-level objective, which weakly constrains local vision–language correspondence. Existing training-free methods either repurpose patch features or require extra supervision or external models. The work investigates whether local semantics reside inside the global CLIP representations and can be extracted without training.

Method: TraceCLIP isolates patch-specific terms encoded in the CLS attention output to recover latent patch-level semantic evidence. It then converts these contribution-derived semantics into a semantic-geodesic topology gate that calibrates final-layer patch affinities for dense feature reconstruction, all in a training-free manner.

Result: On eight zero-shot semantic segmentation benchmarks, TraceCLIP achieves 1.3–4.5 point gains in average mIoU over the strongest prior training-free methods, across different backbones and background settings, without additional training, external vision models, or region-level supervision. It demonstrates strong local semantic discrimination and text-conditioned spatial alignment.

Conclusion: The findings indicate that spatially localized semantics remain accessible within the internal structure of globally aligned CLIP representations, suggesting a broader potential for training-free extraction of local, region-level semantics from vision–language models.

Abstract: Dense vision-language understanding, including object localization, region recognition, and open-vocabulary semantic segmentation, requires associating language concepts with spatially grounded visual regions. CLIP provides a strong foundation for these tasks by learning a shared image-text embedding space from large-scale contrastive pre-training. However, its image-level objective aligns text with a CLS-derived global representation, leaving local vision-language correspondence only indirectly constrained. Existing methods either introduce additional supervision, external models, or task-specific adaptation, while training-free approaches mainly recover dense responses from existing patch features without examining where local semantics become most accessible within CLIP. We introduce TraceCLIP, a training-free framework that recovers latent patch-level semantic evidence by isolating the patch-specific terms written into the CLS attention output. TraceCLIP further converts contribution-derived semantic responses into a semantic-geodesic topology gate that calibrates final-layer patch affinity for dense feature reconstruction. Diagnostic experiments show that these contribution features exhibit strong local semantic discrimination and text-conditioned spatial alignment. On eight zero-shot semantic segmentation benchmarks, TraceCLIP achieves gains of 1.3 to 4.5 points in average mIoU over the strongest prior training-free methods across both backbones and background settings, without additional training, external vision foundation models, or region-level supervision. More broadly, these findings suggest that spatially localized semantics may remain accessible within the internal construction of globally aligned representations.

</details>


### [4] [DVPSFormer: Efficient Online Depth-aware Video Panoptic Segmentation for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26165)
*Yung-Hsu Yang,Luigi Piccinelli,Siyuan Li,Mattia Segu,Lei Ke,Martin Danelljan,Yuqian Fu,Zuria Bauer,Fisher Yu,Hermann Blum,Marc Pollefeys*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DVPSFormer provides a unified online 4D scene understanding model for depth, segmentation, and instance trajectories, using explicit scene discretization and a discrete-to-continuous depth head, plus online majority voting, achieving state-of-the-art on DVPS benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DVPS methods rely on multi-stage pipelines or offline tracking, hindering real-time decision-making in autonomous navigation; a single-pass online approach is needed.

Method: DVPSFormer architecture with explicit scene discretization (ESD) using segmentation queries to represent foreground/background; a discrete-to-continuous (D2C) depth head decoding metric depth in one pass; online majority voting (OMV) to refine instance classifications over time.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art results on Cityscapes-DVPS and SemKITTI-DVPS benchmarks; demonstrates reduced latency and real-time capability; code released.

Conclusion: Provides a streamlined online solution for safe robotic perception by tightly coupling semantic and geometric learning for joint depth, semantics, and instance tracking.

Abstract: Safe autonomous navigation requires a holistic understanding of dynamic environments, necessitating the simultaneous estimation of metric depth, semantic segmentation, and instance trajectories. While depth-aware video panoptic segmentation (DVPS) unifies these tasks, existing approaches often rely on computationally expensive, multi-stage pipelines or offline tracking, rendering them unsuitable for real-time decision-making. To address this, we propose DVPSFormer, a unified online architecture designed for efficient 4D scene understanding. Central to our approach is explicit scene discretization (ESD), a novel mechanism that leverages segmentation queries to represent foreground and background regions, enabling a discrete-to-continuous (D2C) depth head to decode metric depth in a single pass. This tightly couples semantic and geometric learning while significantly reducing latency. Furthermore, we propose an online majority voting (OMV) mechanism that exploits temporal consistency to refine classification during instance tracking. DVPSFormer establishes a new state-of-the-art on the Cityscapes-DVPS and SemKITTI-DVPS benchmarks, offering a streamlined solution for online robotic perception. Code and models are available at https://royyang0714.github.io/DVPSFormer.

</details>


### [5] [A Picture Says Thousands of Words - Harnessing Dermal Exposure Data from Images through Hybrid Deep Learning for Enhanced Safety Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26170)
*Hua Qian,Manisha Kotha,Tuan Tran,Jennifer Shin,Haining Zheng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Hybrid deep-learning and color-based CV pipeline quantify exposed skin in indoor painting images; ~80% agreement with human estimates; scalable, with future enhancements for body-part recognition, PPE detection, and video-based exposure analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for scalable, semi-quantitative dermal exposure metrics from images to supplement or replace manual estimates, enabling larger datasets and faster assessment.

Method: 1) Mask R-CNN for person instance segmentation to extract subject and remove background interference; 2) a color-based segmentation algorithm to identify exposed skin; 3) compute exposed-skin-to-body pixel ratio as the exposure metric. Dataset used: 170 indoor-painting images.

Result: Exposed-skin-to-body pixel ratios show approximately 80% agreement with human estimates, indicating a reasonably accurate and scalable proxy for manual scoring.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a scalable approach to extract semi-quantitative exposure information from images; potential extensions include body-part recognition, PPE detection, and video-based exposure analysis.

Abstract: This study developed a hybrid computer vision method to quantify exposed skin from images for dermal exposure assessment. Using 170 indoor-painting images, Mask R-CNN first identified human subjects and removed background interference; a color-based algorithm then segmented exposed skin. The resulting exposed-skin-to-body pixel ratios showed approximately 80% agreement with human estimates. The approach demonstrates a scalable way to extract semi-quantitative exposure information from images, with future extensions to body-part recognition, PPE detection, and video-based exposure analysis.

</details>


### [6] [Rad-JEPA 3D: Radiology Joint-Embedding Predictive Model for 3D Computed Tomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26196)
*Quoc-Huy Trinh,Minh-Van Nguyen,Ulas Bagci*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Rad-JEPA 3D learns volumetric CT representations via masked-view latent prediction, using a hybrid H-Mamba encoder to preserve coarse spatial structure: a Mamba state-space branch for inter-slice continuity and a grouped-query attention branch for cross-plane context, integrated with a lightweight router, plus layer-wise HSOR regularization to reduce redundancy. Pretrained on ~120k CT scans with 4.0B parameters, it achieves state-of-the-art spatial reasoning and competitive VQA performance, with ablations showing complementary gains from each component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address degradation of coarse spatial/geometric structure in volumetric encoders under self-supervised pretraining, which hampers downstream tasks such as organ disentanglement, abnormality detection, and spatial reasoning when paired with language models.

Method: Rad-JEPA 3D is a joint-embedding predictive framework that pretrains a volumetric CT encoder by predicting latent features of a full scan from a masked view. The core is a hybrid H-Mamba encoder: (1) Mamba state-space branch modeling inter-slice continuity via sequential scanning, (2) grouped-query attention branch capturing cross-plane spatial context, combined through a lightweight per-token router. Layer-wise Hidden States Orthogonal Regularization (HSOR) aligns student-teacher hidden states and reduces feature redundancy to improve intermediate representations. Pretraining data ~120k CT scans; model size 4.0B parameters.

Result: The approach yields state-of-the-art results on spatial reasoning benchmarks (Spatial-Med) and competitive performance on closed-ended VQA, outperforming many larger models. Ablations show the hybrid block and HSOR provide complementary gains and that learned spatial structure can substitute for larger language-model scale in volumetric reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Rad-JEPA 3D demonstrates that preserving spatial structure through a hybrid block and targeted regularization enables compact volumetric encoders to achieve strong downstream performance in organ recognition and spatial reasoning, with HSOR and the hybrid H-Mamba components contributing complementary gains.

Abstract: Self-supervised pretraining is central to 3D medical image analysis, where unlabeled CT volumes are abundant but expert annotations are scarce. Yet existing volumetric encoders often fail to preserve the coarse spatial and geometric structure that downstream reasoning depends on, limiting their performance on organ disentanglement, abnormality detection, and spatial understanding when paired with language models. We introduce Rad-JEPA 3D, a joint-embedding predictive framework that learns volumetric CT representations by predicting the latent features of a complete scan from a masked view. At its core is a hybrid H-Mamba encoder that fuses a Mamba state-space branch, which models inter-slice continuity through sequential scanning, with a grouped-query attention branch, which captures cross-plane spatial context, combined through a lightweight per-token router. To improve the quality of intermediate representations, we further propose Hidden States Orthogonal Regularization (HSOR), which aligns student-teacher hidden states and reduces feature redundancy throughout the encoder. This layer-wise regularization produces more consistent and discriminative volumetric representations, leading to improved performance on organ recognition and spatial reasoning tasks. Pretrained on approximately 120,000 CT scans, Rad-JEPA 3D attains state-of-the-art results despite its compact size: with only 4.0B total parameters, it achieves competitive results with state-of-the-art on closed-ended VQA and the best average spatial-reasoning score on the Spatial-Med benchmark. Ablation studies confirm that the hybrid block and HSOR contribute complementary gains, and that the induced spatial structure can substitute for raw language-model scale on volumetric reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [7] [WildShadowRemover: In-the-Wild Video Shadow Removal via Detail-Preserving Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26203)
*Jiamin Xu,Cong Wang,Zheng Dong,Chi Wang,Renshu Gu,Weiwei Xu,Gang Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A diffusion-based video shadow removal framework (WildShadowRemover) that fine-tunes a pretrained video diffusion model with LoRA, augmenting the VAE with detail injection and a shadow-mask-guided frequency-decomposed modulation to recover high-frequency textures while suppressing shadows; enhanced by monocular depth priors and evaluated on a new large synthetic WildShadow dataset, achieving superior shadow removal quality and temporal consistency in challenging real-world scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Shadow removal in real-world videos is difficult due to complex illumination, variable shadow appearances, and scarce diverse training data. Existing methods struggle with temporal coherence and generalization to unconstrained scenes.

Method: - Fine-tune a pretrained video diffusion model using LoRA (low-rank adaptation). - Freeze the VAE decoder and augment with a detail injection module to preserve fine image details. - Introduce a shadow-mask-guided frequency-decomposed modulation module to restore high-frequency textures while suppressing shadow artifacts. - Use monocular depth priors (Depth Anything 3) to provide geometry-aware guidance under difficult lighting. - Create WildShadow, a large-scale paired video shadow removal dataset/benchmark with diverse synthetic scenes.

Result: The proposed approach achieves superior shadow removal quality and temporal consistency compared with existing methods, generating temporally coherent shadow-free videos with enhanced visual quality and strong generalization to in-the-wild scenarios.

Conclusion: Leveraging LoRA fine-tuning, detail injection, depth-guided frequency modulation, and a large synthetic paired dataset enables robust, temporally coherent video shadow removal in unconstrained environments, with improved generalization to real-world footage.

Abstract: Video shadow removal in the wild remains challenging due to complex illumination, diverse shadow appearances, and limited training data. Despite its importance to numerous vision and graphics applications, it remains largely unexplored in unconstrained real-world scenarios. To address this gap, we present WildShadowRemover, a framework that adapts a pretrained video diffusion model for robust video shadow removal via LoRA fine-tuning. To preserve fine image details while retaining the model's powerful generative prior, we augment the frozen VAE decoder with a detail injection module and introduce a shadow-mask-guided frequency-decomposed modulation module to selectively restore high-frequency textures while suppressing shadow artifacts. Monocular depth priors from Depth Anything 3 further provide geometry-aware guidance under challenging lighting conditions. We also construct WildShadow, a large-scale paired video shadow removal dataset and benchmark, covering diverse synthetic scenes. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms existing approaches in shadow removal quality and temporal consistency, producing temporally coherent shadow-free videos with superior visual quality and strong generalization across challenging in-the-wild scenarios.

</details>


### [8] [Where Physics Meets Privacy: Federated PINNs for Privacy-Preserving Brain Tumor Biomechanical Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26207)
*Mahmuda Akter Sristy,Md Al-Mahfuz Chowdhury,Momota Ahsana Meem,Sajid Ahamed,Kazi Irfan Subhan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Federated physics-informed neural network for brain tumor mechanics using linear elasticity; privacy-preserving FL with FedAvg across three simulated sites yields comparable performance to centralized training, with high AUC and improved pituitary tumor accuracy; yields smooth, divergence-free displacement fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy constraints (GDPR/HIPAA) and cross-site generalization challenges in neuro-oncology imaging demand privacy-preserving, multi-site learning. Incorporating tissue mechanics via physics-informed losses can improve biomechanical tissue characterization without centralized data sharing.

Method: Three simulated clinical sites train local networks on MRI data with a physics-informed loss based on linear elasticity. Only model weights are exchanged with a central server using FedAvg over 100 rounds; raw data remains local. Evaluation metrics include accuracy and AUC; qualitative assessment of displacement fields.

Result: Federated model achieves 91.4% overall accuracy vs 90.0% for a pooled-data baseline; average AUC 0.985 across tumor classes; pituitary tumor accuracy rises from 85.6% to 94.5%. Training yields smooth, divergence-free displacement fields consistent with expected tissue deformation.

Conclusion: Federated training with physics-based constraints can achieve near-centralized performance while preserving data privacy, and may enhance generalization in neuro-oncology biomechanics without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: Brain tumors such as glioma, meningioma, and pituitary adenoma alter the mechanical behavior of soft brain tissue, yet common diagnostic methods rely on static imaging that cannot capture tumor growth, tissue displacement, or changes in stiffness over time. Deep learning models for this task typically require pooling patient data at one site, which conflicts with privacy rules such as GDPR and HIPAA and limits generalization across institutions, a challenge that is pronounced in neuro oncology given patient diversity. This study presents a federated physics informed neural network combining federated learning with a physics informed loss built on the equations of linear elasticity. Three simulated clinical sites each train a local network on patient specific MRI data using a physics informed loss, and only model weights are shared with a central server through the FedAvg protocol over one hundred rounds, keeping raw data at its site of origin. The federated model reached an overall accuracy of 91.4%, against 90.0% for a non federated baseline trained on pooled data, an average AUC of 0.985 across tumor classes, and a rise in pituitary tumor accuracy from 85.6 to 94.5%. Training produced smooth, divergence free displacement fields consistent with expected tissue deformation, showing that federated training can be paired with physics based constraints without a meaningful loss in performance.

</details>


### [9] [Lag-aware cross-hand alignment for dual-hand action segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26215)
*Fatemeh Ziaeetabar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Lag-Aware Cross-Hand Alignment (LACA) explicitly models directional temporal offsets between left/right hand streams to improve two-hand action segmentation with minimal overhead; LACA-C yields a future-free, real-time variant with strong cues and high throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-hand action segmentation often assumes synchronous hand representations, but real interactions exhibit nonzero, time-varying delays between hands. A lightweight, learnable cross-hand alignment can leverage cross-hand dependencies to improve segmentation and boundary localization without extra labeling.

Method: Estimate directional temporal-offset distributions between hand-specific feature streams; retrieve cross-hand information based on these offsets; use a learned null state to suppress transfers when no compatible cross-hand transition exists; supervise alignment with compatibility-aware targets derived automatically from frame-level annotations; evaluate on HA-ViD and ATTACH; integrate into Polyphony; introduce LACA-C to constrain alignment and inference to current/past observations for future-free, real-time use.

Result: Incorporation of LACA into Polyphony yields two-hand mean F1@50 improvements from 40.4 to 42.5 and boundary F1 from 56.5 to 59.6 on HA-ViD; improvements from 19.9 to 21.8 and 44.7 to 47.9 on ATTACH. Gains are achieved with only ~0.0086M additional parameters. LACA-C on ATTACH attains 83.6% transition-cue recall, median availability delay of 233 ms, 0.72 false cues per minute, and 224.9 current-position predictions per second, demonstrating strong performance for future-free perception.

Conclusion: Explicit cross-hand temporal alignment via LACA provides measurable gains in both action segmentation and boundary localization with minimal parameter overhead and supports timely, future-free perception through LACA-C, indicating the value of modeling inter-hand asynchrony in two-hand action understanding.

Abstract: Dual-hand action segmentation commonly fuses left- and right-hand representations at identical temporal indices, although coordinated hand transitions may occur with nonzero and time-varying delays. We introduce Lag-Aware Cross-Hand Alignment (LACA), a lightweight module that explicitly estimates directional temporal-offset distributions between hand-specific feature streams. LACA retrieves cross-hand information from the estimated offsets and incorporates a learned null state to suppress transfer when no compatible cross-hand transition is supported. Alignment is supervised using compatibility-aware targets derived automatically from frame-level training annotations, without requiring additional labels. Analysis of the HA-ViD and ATTACH training annotations reveals robust nonzero cross-hand matches for 44.7% and 48.9% of transition anchors, respectively, compared with 18.6% and 21.3% under temporally shifted controls. When integrated into Polyphony, LACA improves the two-hand mean F1@50 from 40.4 to 42.5 and boundary F1 from 56.5 to 59.6 on HA-ViD, and from 19.9 to 21.8 and 44.7 to 47.9, respectively, on ATTACH, relative to our reproduced Polyphony baseline. These gains require only approximately 0.0086 million additional trainable parameters. We further introduce LACA-C, a future-free variant that restricts alignment and the complete inference pipeline to current and past observations. On ATTACH, LACA-C achieves 83.6% transition-cue recall, a seed-averaged median availability delay of 233~ms, 0.72 false cues per minute, and segmentation-stage throughput of 224.9 current-position predictions per second. These results demonstrate that explicit cross-hand temporal alignment improves both action segmentation and boundary localization while supporting timely future-free perception.

</details>


### [10] [BG-REAL: A Public Real-Data Anchored Benchmark for Background Manipulation Detection and Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26232)
*Bugra Alperen Uluirmak,Rifat Kurban*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BG-REAL is a public, real-data anchored benchmark for background manipulation detection and localization, built from Open Images V7; 7,000 processed samples across 1,200 source groups, covering six edit families, with matched authentic controls and synthetic controls; includes QA, baselines, and evaluation protocols; demonstrates re-encoding artifacts as a pervasive false-positive risk across baselines; frames BG-REAL as a complementary benchmark to existing image-manipulation localization benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the under-specified nature of background manipulation in image forensics, where manipulated evidence may lie outside the salient foreground and existing benchmarks focus on object-centric edits; provide a real-data anchored resource to evaluate background-focused manipulation and localization methods.

Method: Construct a dataset from Open Images V7 instance-segmentation sources with 7,000 manipulated-like samples over 1,200 source groups; include 6 edit families, matched authentic controls, source-group splits, mask and leakage QA, 599 human-assisted QA rows; incorporate three external baselines (TruFor, MVSS-Net, HiFi-Net) and five seed-model evaluations; provide a reproducible pipeline, evaluation protocol, and reproduction docs; assess false-positive rates under a fixed threshold derived from held-out data.

Result: BG-REAL provides construction pipeline, evaluation protocol, figures, and reproduction documentation; baseline analysis shows false positives from re-encoding artifacts across methods, with FPRs from 0.57 to 1.00; demonstrates that leakage/re-encoding artifacts are a shared risk across baselines rather than model-specific.

Conclusion: BG-REAL offers a background-manipulation-focused complement to existing localization benchmarks, facilitating robust evaluation and reproducibility; not intended as fully real-only or general-purpose, but as a targeted resource for background-edit manipulation detection and localization.

Abstract: Background manipulation is a practical but under-specified image-forensics setting: the manipulated evidence can sit outside the salient foreground object, while many evaluations emphasize object-centric copy-move, splicing, or generic synthetic edits. We introduce BG-REAL, a public real-data anchored benchmark package for background manipulation detection and localization. The current release is built from Open Images V7 instance-segmentation sources and contains 7,000 processed samples over 1,200 source groups, including 6,000 public-data anchored samples and 1,000 synthetic control samples. BG-REAL covers six edit families, matched authentic controls, source-group splits, mask and leakage QA, 599 human-assisted quality-control rows, three completed external baselines (TruFor, MVSS-Net, and HiFi-Net), and five-seed model evaluation. Beyond aggregate accuracy, we use matched-authentic-control diagnostics to measure how often baselines misclassify re-encoded authentic images as manipulated at a threshold fixed on held-out validation data; false-positive rates range from 0.57 (TruFor, the lowest) to 1.00 (several weak or mask-informed baselines), indicating that re-encoding artifacts are a shared shortcut risk across baselines rather than a problem specific to any one model. The release provides the construction pipeline, evaluation protocol, paper-ready figures, and reproduction documentation. We frame BG-REAL as a background-manipulation-focused complement to general image-manipulation-localization benchmarks, not as a fully real-only or general-purpose benchmark.

</details>


### [11] [Spline-Based Boundary Representations for Sparse View Reconstruction and Simulation Using Isogeometric Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26234)
*Davor Dobrota,Vsevolod Skorokhodov,Chenghao Xu,Olga Fink,Malcolm Mielle*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Reconstructs watertight, smooth multi-patch B-spline models directly from sparse RGB images for simulation-ready use, with spline-based projection of fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image-based reconstructions yield detailed geometry but lack numerical robustness; simulation requires explicit, watertight, smooth CAD-compatible geometry; bridging computer vision and numerical analysis is needed.

Method: Joint optimization of a multi-patch B-spline boundary representation directly from sparse, pose-consistent RGB observations; integrates projection of observation-derived fields (thermal, semantic) onto the spline in the same basis.

Result: Produces compact, smooth, watertight geometries compatible with CAD and simulation; enables thermal simulation and modal analysis; demonstrates viability for simulation-driven design and digital twins.

Conclusion: Unifies image-based reconstruction and simulation-ready modeling within a single optimization framework, enabling new workflows in design, inspection, and digital twin applications.

Abstract: Image-based reconstruction aims to recover three-dimensional geometry from images. Recent advances have enabled the recovery of visually detailed models, yet their representations are not well-suited for numerical simulation. Simulation frameworks typically require explicit, watertight, and smooth geometries to ensure numerical robustness and accuracy, properties that surfaces extracted from image-based reconstructions lack. We propose FORGE-SIM, a method to directly reconstruct a multi-patch B-spline boundary representation from sparse posed RGB images without manual intervention. By optimizing the spline representation itself, our approach produces compact, smooth, and watertight geometries that are natively compatible with both Computer Aided Design and simulation workflows. Additionally, we introduce a strategy to project observation-derived fields, such as a thermal state and semantic information, onto the reconstructed models in the same spline basis, enabling immediate use in simulation. We demonstrate that the obtained models are of sufficiently high quality to enable thermal simulation and modal analysis. By unifying image-based reconstruction and simulation-ready modeling within a single optimization framework, this work removes a long-standing barrier between computer vision and numerical analysis. We anticipate that it will enable new workflows for simulation-driven design, inspection, and digital twin applications.

</details>


### [12] [LumaGuide: Distribution Shaping for Training-Free HDR Generation in Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26237)
*Bowen Chen,Shreshth Saini,Balu Adsumilli,Alan C. Bovik*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Training-free diffusion control (LumaGuide) steers sampling to match target luminance distributions for HDR generation in PQ space, without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome HDR and distribution-bias limitations of pretrained diffusion models; enable controllable generation without parameter updates.

Method: Differentiable energy-based guidance during sampling; align output luminance histograms in perceptually uniform PQ space; supports presets, reference images, text predictors; extends to video with temporal constraints.

Result: HDR outputs with coherent highlights and preserved shadows while maintaining semantic fidelity; demonstrates flexible distribution specification; applicable to video with temporal consistency.

Conclusion: Shows that controllable generation can be achieved via sampling-time distribution shaping without retraining, with broad HDR applicability and extension to video.

Abstract: Pretrained diffusion models generate realistic images but are constrained by the statistical biases of their training data, limiting their ability to produce high dynamic range (HDR) content. In this work, we introduce LumaGuide, a training-free framework for distribution shaping in diffusion models. Instead of modifying model parameters, LumaGuide steers the sampling process to match target feature distributions via differentiable energy-based guidance. We instantiate this framework for HDR generation by controlling luminance distributions in perceptually uniform PQ space. Our results show that aligning luminance histograms is sufficient to induce HDR-consistent behavior, including coherent highlights and preserved shadow detail, while maintaining semantic fidelity. Beyond HDR, LumaGuide enables flexible specification of target distributions through data-driven presets, reference images, or text-driven predictors, and extends naturally to video generation with temporal consistency constraints. More broadly, our work demonstrates that controllable generation can be achieved by directly shaping output distributions at sampling time, without retraining diffusion models.

</details>


### [13] [Lightweight Image Classification of Raptor Species for Edge Devices: Rare-Species Dataset Expansion via Video Frame Extraction, Knowledge Distillation, and TensorRT Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26238)
*Takeshi Nishikawa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Lightweight three-student distillation from a large DINOv2-L teacher for real-time raptor species classification on edge devices; dataset expansion and teacher refinement drive most gains; achieves strong macro recall with ~1/8 parameters and feasible Jetson deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable real-time, low-power edge bird-species classification to mitigate wind-turbine collisions, addressing confusion among closely related raptor species.

Method: Distill three lightweight models (MobileNetV4, ViT-Small, EfficientNet-B0) from DINOv2-L (teacher); enlarge dataset to 12,519 images including more Steller's Sea Eagle via video-frame extraction; apply group split separating video and source-image levels to reduce leakage; evaluate ensemble across five distillation seeds; compare with DINOv2-L vs DINOv3-L teachers; deploy with TensorRT FP16 on NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano.

Result: Macro recall 0.935 ± 0.004 over five seeds; 0.955 on conventional image-level split; retains 97.5% of teacher macro recall; on 1,258-image disjoint subset, White-tailed Eagle recall improved by up to 38.6 percentage points; misclassification of White-tailed Eagle as Steller's Sea Eagle reduced from 61% to 15%; TensorRT FP16 on Jetson Orin Nano yields 3.19 ms/image (313 images/s) with 99.95% argmax agreement with FP32; five-seed ablations show no clear ensemble gains from distillation or switching to DINOv3-L; primary gains from dataset expansion and teacher re-fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Primary performance gains derive from dataset expansion and teacher fine-tuning rather than the particular distillation setup or teacher architecture; the lightweight three-student ensemble delivers strong edge performance with substantial parameter savings and practical real-time deployment feasibility.

Abstract: We investigate lightweight raptor-species classification for real-time edge deployment in wind-turbine collision mitigation. Using DINOv2-L (304M parameters) as a teacher, we distilled three lightweight students (MobileNetV4, ViT-Small, and EfficientNet-B0). To reduce confusion between closely related species, we expanded the dataset to 12,519 images, including an increase in Steller's Sea Eagle images from 463 to 2,050 via video-frame extraction. Under a group split that separates samples at the video- and source-image level to mitigate source leakage at that granularity, the three-student ensemble achieved a macro recall of 0.935 +/- 0.004 over five distillation seeds (0.955 on a conventional image-level split, retaining 97.5% of the teacher's macro recall) with roughly one-eighth as many parameters. On a subset of 1,258 images disjoint from the former training images, White-tailed Eagle recall improved by up to 38.6 percentage points, while the rate at which it was misclassified as the Steller's Sea Eagle decreased from 61% to 15% of errors. TensorRT FP16 deployment of EfficientNet-B0 on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano achieved 3.19 ms/image including host-device transfer (313 images/s), with 99.95% argmax agreement with FP32. In five-seed controlled comparisons, neither distillation (versus CE-only) nor the change from a DINOv2-L to a DINOv3-L teacher yielded a clear ensemble-level improvement; the primary gains stem from the dataset expansion and teacher re-fine-tuning.

</details>


### [14] [Comparing the Performance of Foundation Model Derived Embeddings with Traditional Approaches for Distant Metastasis Prediction in Head and Neck Cancer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26276)
*Erich Schmitz,Meixu Chen,Bowen Jing,Jing Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CT foundation-model embeddings for DM risk prediction in head and neck cancer achieve higher AUC than radiomics or DL features alone and rival the combined feature approach, with less reliance on ROIs and annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce dependence on ROI segmentations and expert-driven features by leveraging foundation-model derived embeddings for DM risk prediction in HNC.

Method: Compared radiomics, DL-based features, and CT foundation-derived features extracted from preoperative CTs (2327 patients from RADCURE) using a multi-layer perceptron to predict distant metastasis risk.

Result: CT Foundation features yielded AUC 0.791, outperforming radiomics (0.772) and DL features (0.753); nearly matched the radiomics+DL combined model (0.794).

Conclusion: Foundation-model features offer a scalable, less annotation-intensive alternative to radiomics, with minimal preprocessing and comparable predictive performance.

Abstract: Background: Early prediction of distant metastasis (DM) risk in head and neck cancer (HNC) can enable timely interventions that may improve treatment outcomes. Many current machine learning methods rely on prior knowledge of the region of interest such as tumor segmentations, which require expert knowledge, is time-consuming and introduces user-dependent variability. Medical image-based foundation models have recently been developed for specific imaging modalities to streamline down-stream prediction tasks by extracting modality-relevant features. Purpose: In this study, we evaluate the effectiveness of using a foundation model as the feature extractor to predict DM risk in HNC patients and compare its performance with traditional approaches that require prior knowledge on the regions of interest. Methods: Preoperative CT images of 2327 patients from the RADCURE dataset were used. Three features-sets were created including radiomics, deep-learning based features, and CT Foundation derived features. The feature-sets were used individually in a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) to predict DM risk. Results: The model using CT Foundation embeddings outperformed the radiomics and deep learning-based models, achieving a Receiver Operating Characteristic Area Under the Curve (AUC) of 0.791, compared to AUC values of 0.772 and 0.753 for the radiomics and deep learning-based models, respectively. The CT Foundation based model had similar performance to a model that combined the use of radiomics and deep learning-based features that achieved an AUC of 0.794. Conclusions: Features based on foundation models offer a promising alternative to traditional radiomics while reducing the need for domain expertise and extensively annotated datasets. Their minimal preprocessing requirements also make them a more accessible and scalable option.

</details>


### [15] [HeteroPROPMT: A Real-time and Privacy-Preserving Heterogeneous Collaborative Perception Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26283)
*Armin Maleki,Hayder Radha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HeteroPROMPT enables real-time, privacy-preserving heterogeneous collaborative perception by aligning diverse agent features to an ego-centric BEV space via lightweight visual prompts, without retraining encoders or fusion stacks. It achieves state-of-the-art AP on OPV2V-H and V2XSet with far fewer trainable parameters, and a modality classifier with >99.99% deployment accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative perception across heterogeneous sensors, models, datasets, and training domains introduces feature-space shifts that degrade fusion and detection. Existing approaches retrain fusion components or rely on modality-specific interpreters and proprietary metadata, which limits scalability and privacy.

Method: Freeze encoders and fusion/detection stacks. Use visual BEV prompts to modulate features across channels and spatial locations, enabling rapid alignment to an ego-centric unified feature space. Employ lightweight, learning-based tuning for prompts. For metadata-free deployment, train an autoencoder to learn a compact unified representation to extract modality cues from shared features, enabling real-time routing to appropriate HeteroPROMPT modules without exposing proprietary information.

Result: Empirical evaluation on OPV2V-H and V2XSet shows improved Average Precision over state-of-the-art heterogeneous CP methods, with orders of magnitude fewer trainable parameters. The modality classifier achieves >99.99% accuracy during deployment, supporting real-time modality routing.

Conclusion: HeteroPROMPT delivers a scalable, privacy-preserving solution for heterogeneous CP by decoupling feature alignment from encoder/fusion training, enabling efficient cross-agent collaboration with strong performance and practical deployment capabilities.

Abstract: Collaborative Perception (CP) improves autonomous systems' awareness of their surroundings by sharing sensor data, intermediate features, and detection results. In real-world deployments, however, collaborating vehicles often use heterogeneous sensors, perception models, datasets, and training domains, creating feature-space shifts that degrade downstream fusion and detection. Existing approaches typically retrain fusion and detection components or introduce modality-specific feature interpreters. These methods scale poorly to newly joining agents and often require access to proprietary metadata, raising privacy concerns. We propose HeteroPROMPT, a real-time and privacy-preserving framework for heterogeneous collaborative perception. HeteroPROMPT rapidly aligns each heterogeneous agent's features with an ego-centric unified feature space through modular prompts and lightweight learning-based tuning, while keeping agent encoders and the collaborative fusion and detection stacks frozen. Its visual prompt-based training and inference modulate Bird's Eye View (BEV) features across channels and spatial locations with low computational overhead. For metadata-free deployment, an autoencoder learns a compact unified representation and extracts modality cues from shared features, enabling real-time modality classification and routing to the appropriate HeteroPROMPT modules without exposing proprietary agent information. Experiments on the OPV2V-H and V2XSet datasets show that HeteroPROMPT improves Average Precision over state-of-the-art heterogeneous CP methods while using orders of magnitude fewer trainable parameters. This offers a scalable and practical CP solution. The proposed modality classifier also predicts the joining agent's modality from compact features with greater than 99.99 percent accuracy during deployment. Code will be available at https://github.com/arminmaleki007/HeteroPROMPT.

</details>


### [16] [Eddeep: a deep-learning framework for fast eddy-current distortion correction in diffusion MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26292)
*Antoine Legouhy,Ross Callaghan,Yuchuan Qiao,Whitney Stee,Philippe Peigneux,Hojjat Azadbakht,Hui Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Eddeep is a two-stage deep-learning framework for fast eddy-current distortion correction in diffusion MRI, achieving comparable accuracy to FSL Eddy but with much faster inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eddy-current distortions cause between-volume misalignment in diffusion MRI and current correction methods (e.g., FSL Eddy) are accurate but computationally expensive; a fast, scalable solution is needed for large datasets and clinical deployment.

Method: Stage 1: a supervised image-translation network harmonizes diffusion-weighted and b=0 images to reduce contrast differences that hinder registration. Stage 2: an unsupervised registration network estimates eddy-current distortion and between-volume head-motion parameters under a physics-constrained quadratic distortion model, enabling single-pass correction. Training on UK Biobank; evaluated on in-domain (UK Biobank) and out-of-domain (Memodyn) data.

Result: Eddeep achieved correction quality comparable to FSL Eddy across metrics such as between-volume jitter, DKI residuals, signal irregularity, and mutual information, while substantially reducing inference time; demonstrated generalization to the Memodyn dataset.

Conclusion: Deep learning can provide accurate and efficient eddy-current distortion correction without iterative optimization, supporting faster diffusion MRI processing pipelines for large-scale studies and clinical deployment; code available at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: Diffusion MRI (dMRI) relies on diffusion-weighted echo-planar imaging, which is highly susceptible to eddy-current-induced geometric distortions. These distortions vary across diffusion volumes according to gradient strength and direction, causing between-volume misalignment that can bias downstream microstructural analyses. Current state-of-the-art correction methods, such as FSL Eddy, achieve high-quality correction through iterative prediction-correction schemes but are computationally expensive. We propose Eddeep, a deep-learning framework for fast eddy-current distortion correction in dMRI. Eddeep decomposes the problem into two stages. First, a supervised image translation network standardises the appearance of diffusion-weighted and b=0 images, removing contrast differences that hinder reliable registration. Second, an unsupervised registration network estimates both eddy-current distortion and between-volume head motion parameters under a physics-constrained quadratic distortion model, enabling correction in a single forward pass. The method was trained on UK Biobank data and evaluated on both in-domain (UK Biobank) and out-of-domain (Memodyn) datasets. Across a range of complementary metrics, including between-volume jitter, diffusion kurtosis imaging residuals, signal irregularity, and mutual information, Eddeep achieved correction quality comparable to that of FSL Eddy while substantially reducing inference time. These results demonstrate that deep learning can provide accurate and efficient eddy-current distortion correction without relying on iterative optimisation, supporting the development of faster diffusion MRI processing pipelines for large-scale studies and clinical deployment. The code is available at: https://github.com/CIG-UCL/eddeep.

</details>


### [17] [MoSAIC: Aligned Intervention Supervision for Part-Local Motion Style Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26304)
*Nazanin Amini,Kevin Desai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MoSAIC is a part-local, reference-conditioned diffusion model for motion editing that preserves source action while enabling selective regional style transfer via aligned intervention supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accurate, region-specific motion editing where targets for arbitrary part-local references are not readily available in datasets, and to prevent self-reconstruction from copying content motion.

Method: A latent diffusion framework that factorizes content and reference features by anatomical region, uses separate conditioning for the root trajectory, routes user-selected references to body parts, and employs aligned intervention supervision that constructs synchronized references and counterfactual targets through controlled local transformations to make regional responses and preserved motion directly observable during training.

Result: With 128 motions and 896 routed conditions, part-masked routing reduces preserved-region error from 70.64 to 66.45 mm and off-target leakage from 18.08 to 9.88 mm vs whole-body routing, while keeping a positive selected-region response. A matched-budget continuation study shows 8.8% relative gain in selected-target response and 2.0 percentage-point rise in requested-route influence concentration.

Conclusion: MoSAIC improves the response–preservation trade-off for selective, controllable part-local motion editing by effectively aligning supervision with targeted regional transformations.

Abstract: Editing character motion often requires transferring a gesture or gait from one or more reference motions while preserving the source action, timing, root trajectory, and unselected body regions. Existing motion datasets, however, rarely provide paired targets for arbitrary part-local content--reference combinations, and self-reconstruction training may allow a diffusion model to reproduce the content motion while underusing the routed reference. We present MoSAIC, a latent diffusion framework for part-local reference-conditioned motion style transfer. MoSAIC factorizes content and reference features by anatomical region, preserves the root trajectory through a separate conditioning pathway, and routes user-selected references to individual body parts. Its central contribution is aligned intervention supervision, which constructs synchronized references and counterfactual targets through controlled local transformations, making both the requested regional response and the motion to be preserved directly observable during training. In a frozen evaluation comprising 128 motions and 896 routed conditions, part-masked routing reduces preserved-region error from 70.64 to 66.45~mm and matched-noise off-target leakage from 18.08 to 9.88~mm relative to whole-body routing, while retaining a positive selected-region response. A matched-budget continuation study further shows that retaining aligned intervention supervision produces an 8.8\% relative increase in selected-target response and a 2.0-percentage-point increase in requested-route influence concentration. These results demonstrate that MoSAIC improves the response--preservation trade-off required for selective and controllable part-local motion editing.

</details>


### [18] [Seeing or Knowing? Visual Context Sensitivity in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26326)
*Jiaang Li,Chengzu Li,Zhaochong An,Yifei Yuan,Xi Liu,Serge Belongie,Vésteinn Snæbjarnarson*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Diagnostic study of multimodal LLMs showing that coarse visual attributes are preserved in final-layer tokens, but models struggle to control reliance on vision; WhatIfVis benchmark reveals vision-vs-prior trade-offs; supervised fine-tuning, activation patching, and a learned steering vector improve controllability; conclusion: bottleneck is post-perceptual integration rather than perceptual encoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate why vision-centric tasks fail for MLLMs when visual evidence conflicts with pretrained priors; quantify whether visual information is actually represented and how strongly models rely on it.

Method: Two diagnostic paradigms: (1) image reconstruction to test availability of visual information; (2) measuring multimodal context sensitivity. Introduce WhatIfVis benchmark across five coarse-grained dimensions (spatial-temporal, color, count, size, weight) with questions answerable from image or prior. Experiments with frozen MLLMs for reconstruction; analyses of context sensitivity; supervised fine-tuning (SFT); activation patching to localize vision-vs-prior trade-off; and a learned steering vector to control the trade-off.

Result: (i) Coarse attributes can be reconstructed from final-layer image tokens in frozen models, indicating preserved perceptual encoding; failures on such questions are due to post-perceptual utilization. (ii) Vanilla models exhibit unstable visual context sensitivity; SFT improves controllability and generalizes; activation patching identifies the relevant depths where vision-vs-prior trade-off occurs across six models. (iii) The trade-off can be steered by a learned vector; applying it improves controllability without explicit intent instructions.

Conclusion: The bottleneck is post-perceptual integration: MLLMs encode visual evidence for coarse attributes but cannot reliably control reliance on it. Controllability improves with supervised fine-tuning, activation patching, or a steering vector, suggesting future work should focus on training objectives and architectures that better disentangle and regulate vision-prior integration.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance by integrating visual inputs with the rich priors of pretrained language models. However, they often fail on vision-centric tasks, especially when visual evidence conflicts with pretrained knowledge. We explore these failures separately using two diagnostic paradigms: (1) probing whether visual information is available, via image reconstruction, and (2) measuring multimodal context sensitivity, the extent to which the model follows visual context versus the language prior. To support the second, we introduce the WhatIfVis, a benchmark spanning five coarse-grained dimensions (spatial-temporal, color, count, size, and weight) whose questions admit answers from either the image or the prior. Our analysis yields three findings: (i) Coarse-grained visual evidence is preserved, as these attributes can be reconstructed from the final-layer image tokens of frozen MLLMs. Failures on questions about these attributes therefore point to post-perceptual utilization, rather than to degraded visual encoding during perception. (ii) Even when explicitly instructed to use or ignore visual evidence, vanilla models (without supervised fine-tuning on the WhatIfVis) show unstable visual context sensitivity. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) improves this controllability and generalizes across domains, and activation patching further localizes the vision-versus-prior trade-off at architecture-specific depths across all six models. (iii) The vision-versus-prior trade-off is controllable along a learned vector. Applying this steering vector, even without any intent instruction, improves controllability over the vanilla model. Together, these results relocate the bottleneck, indicating that for the coarse attributes we study, MLLMs encode the visual evidence but cannot reliably control their reliance on it.

</details>


### [19] [Zero-Fi: Zero-Shot Wi-Fi-Based Human Activity Recognition via Contrastive Signal-Language Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26381)
*Yitong Shen,Cheng Guo,Peiliang Wang,Jingzhe Zhang,Yi Sheng,Haopeng Zhang,Hongfei Xue,Yili Ren*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Zero-Fi introduces a cross-modal, contrastive signal-language alignment framework for zero-shot Wi-Fi-based activity recognition, enabling recognition of unseen activities without labeled Wi-Fi data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Wi-Fi HAR methods assume a closed set of activities and require labeled samples for every target class, limiting generalization to unseen activities; a scalable zero-shot approach using semantic descriptions is desirable.

Method: Fuse complementary Wi-Fi signal features into unified representations and align them with natural-language activity descriptions in a shared embedding space via contrastive learning; the model learns cross-modal representations so that seen-class Wi-Fi features align with their language descriptions, enabling zero-shot recognition for unseen classes without Wi-Fi labels.

Result: Demonstrates effective zero-shot recognition of held-out activity classes on large-scale public benchmarks, validating cross-modal signal-language alignment as a means to extend Wi-Fi sensing to unseen activities.

Conclusion: Signal-language alignment offers a viable path to scalable, open-set Wi-Fi HAR, with potential improvements in semantics, feature fusion, and cross-modal mapping for better generalization to unseen activities.

Abstract: Wi-Fi-based human activity recognition has advanced substantially, but most existing methods assume a closed set of activities and require labeled Wi-Fi samples for every target class, limiting their ability to recognize unseen activities. We present Zero-Fi, a contrastive signal-language alignment framework for zero-shot Wi-Fi-based human activity recognition. Zero-Fi learns unified representations from complementary Wi-Fi signal features and aligns them with the semantic representations of natural-language activity descriptions in a shared embedding space. This cross-modal alignment enables Zero-Fi to recognize new activity classes without requiring labeled Wi-Fi samples or model adaptation for those classes. Experiments on large-scale public benchmark datasets demonstrate effective zero-shot recognition of held-out activity classes, highlighting the potential of signal-language alignment to extend Wi-Fi sensing beyond predefined activity classes.

</details>


### [20] [Registration-Grounded Spectral Fusion for Unregistered WLI/NBI Endoscopic Lesion Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26395)
*Pengyu Jie,Wanquan Liu,Rui He,Pengcheng Li,Weiping Wen,Deyu Meng,Junwei Han,Chenqiang Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Reliability-aware cross-modal fusion for misaligned WLI/NBI lesion segmentation using topology-regularized correspondence and complex-domain fusion that assigns WLI to magnitude (appearance) and NBI to phase (structure), suppressing unreliable regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WLI and NBI offer complementary information but are often misaligned due to viewpoint changes and tissue deformation, leading to incorrect fusion and degraded segmentation. A robust approach must (i) establish reliable cross-modal correspondences, and (ii) fuse information in a way that accounts for modality-specific roles and local reliability.

Method: 1) Compute topology-regularized feature correspondences between WLI and NBI; 2) estimate reliability of cross-modal matches to identify locally mismatched regions; 3) perform a learnable complex-valued fusion where WLI contributes mostly magnitude (appearance) cues and NBI contributes phase (structure) cues; 4) suppress unreliable cross-modal interactions in misregistered areas; 5) validate with ablations including role-reversal studies.

Result: Experiments on paired WLI/NBI endoscopic datasets show consistent improvements in lesion segmentation when using reliability-grounded registration and complex-domain fusion. Role-reversal and module ablation studies corroborate the necessity of both modality-role design and reliability-guided interaction.

Conclusion: Reliability-aware, topology-regularized cross-modal fusion in the complex domain improves segmentation for misaligned WLI/NBI data, with explicit handling of modality roles and local reliability; the approach generalizes to other paired modalities with registration challenges.

Abstract: White-light imaging (WLI) and narrow-band imaging (NBI) provide complementary views of endoscopic lesions, but their paired observations are often spatially misaligned due to viewpoint changes, tissue deformation, and sequential handheld acquisition. This makes direct WLI/NBI fusion prone to mixing non-corresponding regions and may even degrade segmentation around lesion boundaries. To address this problem, we propose a reliability-aware complex-domain fusion framework for paired-but-unregistered WLI/NBI lesion segmentation. The framework first establishes topology-regularized feature correspondence and further estimates where the cross-modal correspondence is reliable. Guided by this reliability, the model selectively fuses WLI and NBI features in a learnable complex representation. In this representation, WLI-derived cues mainly provide appearance-related magnitude responses, while NBI-derived cues provide structure-sensitive phase responses. Unlike conventional real-valued or symmetric multimodal fusion, the proposed method explicitly models the different roles of WLI and NBI and suppresses unreliable cross-modal interaction in locally mismatched regions. Experiments on paired WLI/NBI endoscopic datasets show that the proposed reliability-aware registration grounding and complex-domain fusion consistently improve lesion segmentation performance. Role-reversal and module ablation studies further validate the necessity of both the modality-role design and reliability-guided cross-modal interaction.

</details>


### [21] [Do Unified Multimodal Models Think in One Space? A Lens Through Cross-Branch Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26411)
*Yu Wang,Sharon Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Cross-branch semantic steering reveals an asymmetry in unified multimodal models: embeddings derived from understanding transfer to generation, enabling controllable synthesis and better semantic faithfulness, while reverse transfer is weak due to an object-centric vs low-level appearance feature mismatch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether a single architecture for vision and language exhibits a unified, transferable semantic space across its branches and to quantify cross-branch semantic alignment.

Method: An intervention-based framework that extracts semantic directions from one branch (understanding) and applies them to the other (generation). The authors compare steering vectors learned from the understanding branch to their ability to control generation and maintain semantic faithfulness, and contrast this with the reverse direction.

Result: Steering vectors from the understanding branch successfully transfer to generation, enabling controllable image synthesis and improved semantic faithfulness. The reverse direction is consistently less effective. The authors attribute this asymmetry to a representational mismatch: understanding-derived vectors capture transfer- able, object-centric semantics, whereas generation-derived vectors encode mainly low-level appearance features.

Conclusion: Architectural unification alone does not ensure semantic alignment across branches. Cross-branch semantic steering is a practical tool to probe and quantify cross-branch representations in unified multimodal models, revealing the likely need for alignment-focused training objectives.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) aim to integrate understanding and generation within a single architecture, yet it remains unclear whether these capabilities share a unified and transferable semantic space. This question is fundamentally challenging, as the two branches operate over heterogeneous representations (text tokens vs.\ visual latents) and distinct training objectives, making direct comparison difficult. To address this, we introduce \emph{cross-branch semantic steering}, an intervention-based framework that extracts semantic directions from one branch and applies them to the other. We show that steering vectors learned from the understanding branch can transfer to generation, enabling controllable image synthesis and improved semantic faithfulness. In contrast, the reverse direction consistently shows limited effectiveness. Our analysis suggests that this asymmetry may be related to a practical representational mismatch: understanding-derived vectors capture transferable, object-centric semantics, while generation-derived vectors primarily encode low-level appearance features. Our results reveal that architectural unification does not guarantee semantic alignment, and establish cross-branch steering as a practical tool for probing multimodal representations.

</details>


### [22] [When Fish Look Alike: Tracking Identities with Dual-branch Elasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26412)
*Vran Lee,Xin Liu,Yijie Wei,Yeqiang Liu,Hwa Liang Leo,Zhenbo Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TIDE presents a lightweight dual-branch tracker for dense, homogeneous targets that removes reliance on appearance cues, leveraging Adaptive Geometric Correspondence IoU and system-level elasticity to enable edge deployment. It yields competitive HOTA with dramatically reduced FLOPs on MFT-Edge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address tracking of densely packed, morphologically varying targets where appearance features degrade under occlusion, while enabling real-time edge deployment without the heavy computational cost of appearance-based re-identification. 

Method: Dual-branch architecture with a Lightweight L-branch and a Scalable S-branch, using Adaptive Geometric Correspondence IoU (AGC IoU) that fuses spatial and structural cues for data association, and decouples algorithm from hardware constraints to provide deployment elasticity.

Result: On MFT-Edge, L-branch achieves 28.43 HOTA with 20.47 GFLOPs, a 38.7x reduction versus heavy-backbone baselines like SU-T; S-branch achieves 29.98 HOTA, showing effective edge-cloud performance balance. Code and data released at the provided URL.

Conclusion: TIDE demonstrates that reliable tracking of dense, homogeneous targets is achievable at edge-scale computation through geometry-based association and architecture elasticity, offering practical, end-to-end deployment benefits and a path to scalable accuracy on edge devices.

Abstract: Tracking dense, homogeneous targets like schooling fish remains a major challenge for multiple object tracking due to extreme inter-individual homogeneity, severe physical clustering, and rapid non-rigid deformations. While heavy-backbone separated detection and embedding trackers like SU-T push accuracy boundaries using complex Re-Identification networks, their computational overhead prohibits edge deployment. Furthermore, these modules often fail when appearance features degrade under severe occlusions. To overcome this, we propose Tracking Identities with Dual-branch Elasticity (TIDE). Bypassing expensive appearance cues, TIDE utilizes the Adaptive Geometric Correspondence IoU, an association mechanism leveraging spatial and structural consistency to robustly handle complex morphological variations. Crucially, TIDE introduces system-level deployment elasticity, decoupling the algorithmic pipeline from strict hardware constraints. Evaluations on the MFT-Edge benchmark demonstrate that our Lightweight L-branch achieves a competitive HOTA of 28.43 using merely 20.47G FLOPs. This represents a 38.7-fold computational reduction compared to upper bounds like SU-T, directly facilitating real-time edge deployment. Concurrently, our Scalable S-branch establishes a 29.98 HOTA, successfully bridging the gap between high-precision cloud analysis and efficient edge tracking. The dataset and codes are released at https://vranlee.github.io/TIDE/.

</details>


### [23] [FAS-R1: A Unified Multi-Task MLLM for Reasoning Face Anti-Spoofing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26432)
*Hongyang Wang,Yichen Shi,Hongrui Li,Yiru Huo,Jun Feng,Zitong Yu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes FAS-R1, a 2-stage MLLM framework for unified face anti-spoofing with authenticity, attack-type, and spoof-region outputs. Uses a long-CoT dataset for cold-start fine-tuning and GRPO post-training, plus Degradation-Simulated Augmentation and Difficulty-Aware GRPO to improve robustness. Reports strong in-domain and cross-domain performance and rationale quality; code to be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond label-centric FAS toward structured, explainable outputs (attack semantics and image-grounded evidence) using large multilingual language-model–based reasoning, while addressing easy-sample bias and quality shifts.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) cold-start supervised fine-tuning on FAS-R1-23K long-CoT dataset, (2) FAS-specific GRPO post-training. Introduces Degradation-Simulated Augmentation (DSA) for robustness to visual quality variations and Difficulty-Aware GRPO (DA-GRPO) to balance easy/difficult samples, enabling predictions of authenticity, attack type, and spoof-region localization.

Result: 3B FAS-R1 achieves 98.75% authenticity accuracy, 93.33% attack-type accuracy, and 96.30/94.73% AP@40/AP@50 in-domain. Outperforms peers in cross-domain authenticity generalization and rationale quality. Scaling behavior favorable across base models; code release forthcoming.

Conclusion: The approach yields accurate, semantically rich FAS outputs with robust generalization and explainability, at the cost of added training complexity; further work could explore broader attack types and real-time deployment.

Abstract: Face anti-spoofing (FAS) is increasingly expected to provide not only bona fide/spoof decisions, but also attack semantics and image-grounded evidence for human inspection. Existing discriminative FAS models remain largely label-centric, while recent MLLM-based methods offer structured outputs but still rely mainly on supervised fine-tuning, often producing template-like rationales and weak optimization for difficult attacks. We propose FAS-R1, a two-stage reasoning-oriented MLLM framework for unified FAS prediction, covering authenticity classification, attack-type recognition and spoof-region localization. FAS-R1 first uses FAS-R1-23K, a high-quality long-CoT dataset, for cold-start supervised fine-tuning, and then performs FAS-specific GRPO post-training. Degradation-Simulated Augmentation (DSA) encourages stable spoof-cue reasoning across visual-quality shifts, while Difficulty-Aware GRPO (DA-GRPO) mitigates easy-sample dominance that may leave difficult task--attack groups under-optimized, especially for subtle or ambiguous attacks such as makeup and mask attacks. The main 3B FAS-R1 model achieves 98.75\% authenticity accuracy, 93.33\% attack-type accuracy, and 96.30/94.73\% AP@40/AP@50 in-domain. It also outperforms the compared systems in cross-domain authenticity generalization and answer-and-rationale quality. Experiments with different base models further show favorable scaling behavior. The code will be released soon.

</details>


### [24] [Interpretable Image-Level Acne Severity Grading via EfficientNet-B0 Transfer Learning and Grad-CAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26461)
*Sophie Zeng,Sean Kalaycioglu,Collin Hong,Haipeng Xie*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A four-class acne severity classifier using transfer learning on ACNE04 achieves high accuracy and macro-F1 with strong agreement against Hayashi criteria, plus interpretable Grad-CAM visuals and an open-source, cross-platform pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address inter-rater variability and inconsistent imaging in acne severity assessment by automating robust, reproducible grading.

Method: Fine-tuning an ImageNet-pretrained EfficientNet-B0 on ACNE04 (2,983 images) for four-class classification using AdamW, data augmentation, and validation macro-F1-based checkpointing. Evaluation on a stratified 15% test set. Grad-CAM used for localization. Open-source implementations in PyTorch/timm and MATLAB; clinician-facing interface and a fallback backbone for operation without pretrained weights.

Result: Test accuracy 93.5%, macro-F1 94.4%, per-class F1 0.92–0.97; 83% of errors occur between adjacent grades; quadratic-weighted Cohen’s kappa 0.956 (95% CI 0.935–0.973). Bootstrap CIs indicate stability. Grad-CAM highlights forehead, cheeks, chin. Pipeline is open-source cross-platform.

Conclusion: Lightweight transfer learning with EfficientNet-B0 yields accurate, balanced, and interpretable acne severity grading and provides a reproducible reference for future prospective and device-stratified validation.

Abstract: Acne vulgaris affects most adolescents and many adults. Accurate severity grading guides treatment, monitoring, and clinical trial endpoints, but manual assessment using the Investigator's Global Assessment or Hayashi criteria is limited by inter-rater variability and inconsistent imaging conditions. We developed a four-class acne severity classifier based on the Hayashi criteria using transfer learning with an ImageNet-pretrained EfficientNet-B0 model. The model was fine-tuned on the public ACNE04 dataset of 2,983 labeled images using AdamW optimization, geometric and photometric augmentation, and checkpoint selection based on validation macro-F1. On a held-out stratified 15 percent test set, the classifier achieved 93.5 percent accuracy and 94.4 percent macro-F1, with per-class F1 scores from 0.92 to 0.97. Eighty-three percent of errors occurred between adjacent grades. Quadratic-weighted Cohen's kappa was 0.956, with a 95 percent confidence interval of 0.935 to 0.973. Bootstrap confidence intervals indicated stable performance. Grad-CAM visualizations from the final convolutional block focused on clinically relevant facial regions, including the forehead, cheeks, and chin. The complete pipeline is provided as functionally equivalent open-source implementations in Python using PyTorch and timm, and in MATLAB R2026a. The software includes a clinician-facing inference interface and a fallback backbone option that supports operation without specialized pretrained-weight packages. These results show that lightweight transfer learning can provide accurate, balanced, and interpretable acne severity grading while offering a reproducible cross-platform reference for future prospective and device-stratified clinical validation.

</details>
