### [25] [HERMES: A Hybrid Ensemble for Head-and-Neck Tumor Segmentation, TN Staging, and Recurrence-Free Survival on PET/CT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26498)
*Kai Wang,Meixu Chen,Elie Nasr,Ryan Lanning,Moyed Miften*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Single-container, three-task pipeline for HECKTOR 2026: segmentation (GTVp/GTVn), radiological staging (N/T), and RFS prognosis from paired FDG-PET/CT and EHR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrate segmentation with downstream staging and prognosis to improve radiotherapy planning and patient stratification, while minimizing feature dimensionality and overfitting via honest out-of-fold design.

Method: 10-fold ensemble of STU-Net Small for GTV segmentation; derive compact geometry features from predicted masks aligned with AJCC/UICC 7th-edition axes for T/N staging; equal-weight ensemble of deep and clinical risk experts for prognosis with a concordance-tracking survival loss; containerized deployment with two decorrelated submissions; no tuning on the public validation set; performance reported on internal cross-validation and HECKTOR 2026 validation leaderboard.

Result: N-stage balanced accuracy improved from 0.691 to 0.720 (+0.030) on internal cross-validation. On HECKTOR 2026 validation leaderboard: weighted score 0.6454; Mean Dice 0.641; T balanced accuracy 0.580; N balanced accuracy 0.642; RFS C-index 0.679; qualified for testing.

Conclusion: HERMES demonstrates a compact, multi-task radiotherapy target segmentation and prognostic framework leveraging geometry-derived staging features and concordance-focused training, achieving competitive leaderboard performance and testing readiness.

Abstract: We present HERMES (Hybrid Ensemble for Radiotherapy-target segmentation, Malignancy staging, and Event-free Survival), a single containerized algorithm for the three HECKTOR 2026 subtasks: segmentation of the primary tumor (GTVp) and pathological lymph nodes (GTVn), radiological T/N staging, and recurrence-free survival (RFS), computed from a paired FDG-PET/CT scan and an electronic health record. A 10-fold ensemble of STU-Net Small networks produces the segmentation; the predicted mask then drives two downstream tasks. Rather than pass a generic radiomics vector to the staging models, we derive from the predicted masks a compact set of geometry features aligned with the size and number axes of AJCC/UICC 7th-edition radiological N/T staging. On internal cross-validation these features raise N-stage balanced accuracy from 0.691 to 0.720 (+0.030), our largest single design gain, at lower feature dimensionality. For prognosis we combine complementary deep and clinical risk experts in an equal-weight ensemble, and train one deep expert with a concordance-tracking survival loss of our own, whose value approximates the concordance index during training. Every component was selected on honest out-of-fold predictions under a regularization-oriented protocol, with no tuning on the public validation set, and deployed as two decorrelated submissions. On the HECKTOR 2026 validation leaderboard, HERMES achieved a weighted score of 0.6454 (Mean Dice 0.641, T balanced accuracy 0.580, N balanced accuracy 0.642, RFS C-index 0.679) and qualified for the testing phase. Team: AMC_HNC.

</details>


### [26] [Semantic-Aware Temporal Adaptation for UAV Anti-UAV Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26511)
*Xiaozhen Qiao,Da Zhang,Yubin Guo,Junyu Gao,Zhiyuan Zhao,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SATATrack is a semantic-aware temporal adaptation framework for UAV Anti-UAV tracking that leverages target language descriptions to propagate context and online feature distribution alignment, achieving state-of-the-art on UAV-Anti-UAV benchmarks and competitive results on related tasks; code to be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In dual-dynamic settings where both the observer and target are moving, rapid viewpoint changes, motion blur, scale variation, and distractors degrade fixed representations. A stable semantic anchor (language description) can guide tracking across frames, while online distribution alignment mitigates test-time shifts.

Method: Introduce Semantic-Aware Context Propagation (SACP) that uses the target description to guide temporal context propagation across backbone stages to preserve identity under rapid appearance changes. Use an auxiliary contrastive regularizer to suppress responses to semantically similar background regions during training. At inference, apply Temporal-Aware Distribution Alignment (TADA) to align feature distributions online without updating model parameters, combining recent-frame estimates with training-time statistics for stability.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on the UAV-Anti-UAV benchmark and remains competitive in Anti-UAV and UAV object tracking tasks; code will be released at the provided GitHub URL.

Conclusion: Semantic grounding via target language descriptions and online distribution alignment enable robust tracking under dual-dynamic conditions, offering strong performance and practical deployment potential for UAV security applications.

Abstract: UAV Anti-UAV tracking is an emerging low-altitude security task for localizing an adversarial UAV using the onboard camera of a moving observer UAV. It differs from conventional UAV tracking and ground-based Anti-UAV tracking because both the camera platform and the target move simultaneously. This dual-dynamic setting induces rapid viewpoint changes, motion blur, scale variation, and visually similar distractors, making reliable appearance matching difficult. Under such rapidly changing conditions, fixed visual representations are often insufficient because target appearance becomes unreliable and feature distributions may deviate from the training domain. The target language description remains stable across frames and can therefore serve as a semantic anchor for temporal state propagation, while online feature-distribution alignment can reduce video-specific test-time shifts. In this paper, we propose \emph{SATATrack}, a Semantic-Aware Temporal Adaptation framework for UAV Anti-UAV tracking. SATATrack introduces Semantic-Aware Context Propagation (SACP), which uses the target description to guide temporal context propagation across backbone stages and preserve target identity under rapid appearance changes. An auxiliary contrastive regularizer is used during training to discourage responses to semantically similar background regions. During inference, Temporal-Aware Distribution Alignment (TADA) aligns feature distributions online without updating model parameters, combining recent-frame estimates with training-time statistics for stability. SATATrack achieves state-of-the-art performance on the UAV-Anti-UAV benchmark while remaining competitive in Anti-UAV and UAV object tracking tasks. The code will be available at https://github.com/XiaozhenQiao/SATATrack.

</details>


### [27] [EgoSafe: A First-Person Mobile-Captured Benchmark for Visual Safety Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26518)
*Yuyun Chen,Tianao Li,TianQuan Feng,Cen Chen,Huiping Zhuang,Hao Peng,Ziqian Zeng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A new EgoSafe-Bench benchmark probes forensic, causal reasoning in egocentric safety scenarios using a Hierarchical Reasoning Evaluation (HRE) protocol; reveals a perception-reasoning gap in state-of-the-art LVLMs; 12k samples derived from 3k video clips; aims to foster robust, logically consistent video understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LVLMs excel at descriptive tasks but struggle with causal reasoning under epistemic uncertainty in partially observable, egocentric contexts. Existing evaluations rely on third-person surveillance and binary metrics, failing to reveal cognitive gaps in safety-critical scenarios; there is a need for benchmarks that enforce a rigorous reasoning process from feature anchoring to intent inference.

Method: Construct EgoSafe-Bench with 12,000 evaluation samples by pairing 3,000 egocentric video clips with QA chains governed by a proposed Hierarchical Reasoning Evaluation (HRE) protocol. HRE enforces a reasoning trajectory from initial feature anchoring to blind-spot deduction and intent inference, penalizing shortcut-based predictions. Evaluate state-of-the-art LVLMs (e.g., Qwen3-VL, Gemini, VideoLLaMA 3) to assess perception-reasoning coupling.

Result: The evaluation exposes a significant perception-reasoning decoupling: models achieve high descriptive scores but show fragility in causal reasoning and logical closure. The dataset and evaluation framework provide a challenging testbed to assess and improve logically robust video understanding in egocentric safety contexts.

Conclusion: EgoSafe-Bench and HRE offer a rigorous platform to foster development of logically robust video understanding systems for egocentric safety. The benchmark highlights cognitive gaps between perception and reasoning, guiding future work on improving causal inference, consistency, and resistance to shortcut strategies in LVLM-based video understanding.

Abstract: Reliable visual safety understanding in real-world scenarios demands more than just object recognition; it requires causal reasoning under epistemic uncertainty. While Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate impressive semantic alignment on standard benchmarks, they often struggle to distinguish between superficial correlation and genuine forensic logic when grounded in the dynamic, partially observable nature of first-person experiences. Existing evaluations, dominated by third-person surveillance footage and binary classification metrics, fail to expose this cognitive gap. To address this, we introduce EgoSafe-Bench, a benchmark specifically designed to probe forensic reasoning in egocentric safety scenarios. It comprises 12,000 unique evaluation samples, generated by pairing each of the 3,000 video clips with a QA chain governed by our proposed Hierarchical Reasoning Evaluation (HRE) protocol. Unlike standard benchmarks, HRE mandates a rigorous reasoning trajectory from initial feature anchoring to blind-spot deduction and intent inference, thereby enforcing logical consistency and penalizing shortcut-based predictions.Extensive evaluations of state-of-the-art LVLMs (e.g., Qwen3-VL, Gemini, VideoLLaMA 3) reveal a significant perception-reasoning decoupling: models often achieve high descriptive scores but exhibit notable fragility in causal reasoning and logical closure. Our work provides both a challenging dataset and a systematic evaluation framework to foster the development of logically robust video understanding systems.

</details>


### [28] [CineWeaver: Training-Free Reference-Controllable Multi-Shot Long Video Generation for Cinematic Storytelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26529)
*Yuyang Huang,Yabo Chen,Wenrui Dai,Ziyang Zheng,Haibin Huang,Chi Zhang,Junni Zou,Hongkai Xiong,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CineWeaver is a training-free framework that enables long-form, multi-shot, reference-controllable video generation using pretrained diffusion models by breaking temporal continuity during inference, plus shot routing and anchor memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-video diffusion models exhibit a bias towards temporal continuity, hindering multi-shot transitions and long-horizon generation. There is a need for a unified, retraining-free framework that can handle long-form, per-shot control and consistent appearance.

Method: Modify inference-time components of pretrained video diffusion models: (1) alter positional encoding and attention patterns to disrupt temporal continuity for clear shot transitions; (2) introduce shot-routed reference conditioning for per-shot fine-grained control; (3) add an anchor memory mechanism to maintain global appearance cues across long sequences. All without retraining the model.

Result: Experimental results claim high-quality cinematic videos of long durations with clear shot transitions, consistent identities, and stable global appearance, demonstrating the ability to achieve long-form, reference-controllable, and multi-shot generation in a training-free fashion. A project page is provided.

Conclusion: CineWeaver is the first unified, training-free framework to simultaneously enable long-form, reference-controllable, and multi-shot video generation, addressing a key limitation of existing methods.

Abstract: Cinematic video generation is challenging for text-to-video diffusion models due to concurrent requirements on multi-shot generation, fine-grained controllability over characters and scenes, and long-form generation across extended temporal horizons. Existing methods rely on customization and retraining to separately address specific requirements, and cannot simultaneously fulfill all the requirements with a unified framework. In this paper, we shed light on the training-free paradigm with the key insight that the difficulty of multi-shot generation arises from a structural bias toward temporal continuity in pretrained video diffusion models, and consequently, propose a unified framework named CineWeaver to achieve reference-controllable multi-shot long-video generation without retraining. We manipulate positional encoding and attention patterns to break temporal continuity during inference to enable clear shot transitions using pretrained video diffusion models. Furthermore, we extend the proposed framework with a shot-routed reference conditioning mechanism for per-shot fine-grained controllability, and develop an anchor memory mechanism to allow long-form generation with consistent global appearance cues. To our best knowledge, CineWeaver is the first unified framework to simultaneously enable \textbf{long-form}, \textbf{reference-controllable}, and \textbf{multi-shot} video generation in a training-free fashion. Experimental results demonstrate that CineWeaver produces high-quality cinematic videos of long durations with consistent identities, stable global appearance, and clear shot transitions. The project page is available at: https://cineweaver.github.io.

</details>


### [29] [TPCD: Tone-Pressure Contrastive Decoding and the Label-Free Gating Bottleneck in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26536)
*Jinkun Zhao,Kui Zhang,Wenjun Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes Tone-Pressure Contrastive Decoding (TPCD) to use high-pressure commitments as a negative branch in decoding for vision-language models. Empirically, pressure induces strong risk-taking; naive safe neutralization reduces risk but hurts positives; TPCD lowers risk but also reduces positives; gating strategies can improve safety while preserving some positives, but many approaches rely on post-hoc or surface-form cues and lack grounding-aware validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether pressure-induced commitments in VLMs can be leveraged as a contrastive signal to mitigate harmful outputs, and whether simple gating strategies can preserve utility while suppressing risk under high-pressure prompts.

Method: Compute logit differences between high-pressure and safe-neutral instructions (TPCD). Evaluate on an 800-sample tone-matters benchmark using LLaVA-1.5-7B. Explore benchmark-specific task priors/disagreement gates; perform held-out analyses on GLM-4.6V and Llama-3.2-Vision with n=800 negative and n=780 positive-matched runs. Assess attack success rate (ASR) and positive accuracy; compare against safe neutralization and branch-disagreement baselines; assess a category-prior-free answer-disagreement router.

Result: Under pressure, ASR = 66.75%; safe neutralization reduces ASR to 9.88%. Full TPCD reduces ASR to 0.50% but drops positive accuracy to 15.56%. A benchmark-specific task-prior/disagreement gate preserves 54.44% positive accuracy while lowering ASR to 1.63%. Across held-out runs on GLM-4.6V and Llama-3.2-Vision, simple gates outperform safe neutralization; a time-bound positive subtask shows weak performance. A category-prior-free answer-disagreement router lowers aggregate ASR to 6.93% with 79.94% positive accuracy but is post-hoc and surface-form based.

Conclusion: Pressure serves as a useful probe of commitment bias and a viable mitigation signal, but current gates lack independent grounding-aware validation. Simple gates can improve safety over safe neutralization, yet robust grounding-aware detectors are still needed for reliable deployment.

Abstract: High-pressure prompts can push vision-language models (VLMs) into unsupported commitments, such as reading illegible text, reporting indeterminate times, or affirming absent objects. This paper asks whether the pressure-induced distribution itself can serve as a contrastive-decoding negative branch. Tone-pressure contrastive decoding (TPCD) subtracts logits produced under a high-pressure instruction from logits produced under a safe neutral instruction. On the 800-example tone-matters benchmark, LLaVA-1.5-7B under pressure reaches 66.75% attack success rate (ASR); safe neutralization reduces ASR to 9.88%; full TPCD reaches 0.50% but collapses positives to 15.56%. A benchmark-specific task-prior/disagreement gate preserves measured positive accuracy (54.44%) while lowering ASR to 1.63% on LLaVA. Treating this LLaVA analysis as the design split, full $n=800$ negative and $n=780$ matched-positive held-out runs on GLM-4.6V and Llama-3.2-Vision show that simple gates can improve over safe neutralization, with sensitivity analyses bounding the weak time-positive subtask. A category-prior-free answer-disagreement router reduces held-out aggregate ASR to 6.93%, improving over both safe neutralization (10.98%) and branch disagreement (9.67%) while matching branch disagreement's 79.94% positive accuracy, although it remains post-hoc and surface-form based. We conclude that pressure is a useful probe of commitment bias and a viable mitigation signal, but the current gates are not yet independently validated grounding-aware detectors.

</details>


### [30] [From Spatial Semantics to Temporal Context: Leveraging Gaze Trajectory for Weakly Supervised Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26542)
*Shaoxuan Wu,Xiao Zhang,Xiaodi Zhao,Yunzhi Tian,Yilin Tang,Jun Feng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TrailNet leverages gaze trajectories for segmentation via a trajectory-guided spatio-temporal encoder and an uncertainty-aware decoder, with cycle distillation for gaze-free inference, achieving state-of-the-art Dice ~81% on two datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaze data indicate clinician attention (fixations/trajectories) but temporal dynamics are underutilized and gaze is noisy; need a model that fuses spatial semantics with temporal context and provides gaze-free inference.

Method: A trajectory-guided spatio-temporal encoder models temporal context and interacts with spatial semantics; a multi-scale uncertainty decoder enforces category mutual-exclusivity to reduce supervision noise; a cycle distillation teacher-student framework enables gaze-free inference.

Result: Dice scores: 81.25% and 81.85% on two public datasets; reported as beating state-of-the-art.

Conclusion: Trajectory-guided, uncertainty-aware approach with gaze-free cycle distillation effectively leverages gaze for improved medical image segmentation and enables gaze-free deployment.

Abstract: Medical image segmentation heavily depends on labor-intensive and time-consuming pixel-level annotations. Eye tracking offers a cost-effective solution that can be naturally integrated into clinical workflows. Recorded by eye trackers, gaze conveys the spatial regions of clinicians' attention through fixations and the temporal context of clinicians' progressive visual perception from trajectories. Nevertheless, effective modeling of temporal trajectories remains challenging, and noise in gaze caused by exploratory fixations greatly limits segmentation performance. To overcome these limitations, we propose the Trajectory-guided Uncertainty-aware Network (TrailNet), which exploits gaze-supervised medical image segmentation from spatial semantics modeling to temporal context by jointly leveraging fixations and trajectories. Specifically, the proposed trajectory-guided spatio-temporal encoder models temporal context and establishes complementary interactions with image spatial semantics to strengthen target perception. Furthermore, the multi-scale uncertainty decoder leverages category mutual-exclusivity constraints to produce deterministic predictions and mitigate supervision uncertainty induced by noise. To enable gaze-free inference, we further introduce a cycle distillation strategy that transfers feature-level knowledge via teacher-student networks. Experimental results on two public datasets demonstrate that TrailNet outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving Dice scores of 81.25% and 81.85%, respectively.

</details>


### [31] [MedARC: Training-Free Adaptive Redundancy Compression of Visual Tokens for 3D Medical Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26554)
*Yitao Zhu,Mengjun Liu,Yingji Fu,Haowen Pang,Anqi Qiu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MedARC is a training-free framework for adaptive redundancy compression of 3D medical VLM visual tokens. It uses a triad of cues—self-attention, cross-modal text-token similarity, and local feature deviations—to compute a token importance map and perform saliency-aware token merging, reducing tokens without harming diagnostic performance on CT-RATE and MR-RATE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D medical volumetric images produce long, highly redundant token sequences. Uniform token reduction risks losing clinically relevant information. A multi-signal, data- and model-aligned approach is needed to selectively compress redundancy while preserving query-relevant and structurally distinctive regions.

Method: MedARC computes token importance using three cues: (1) self-attention from the VLM vision encoder to capture intrinsic visual focus; (2) similarity between projected visual tokens and text embeddings to identify query-relevant regions; (3) deviations of local visual foundation-model features from the volume-level feature center to emphasize structurally distinctive anatomy. These cues are fused into an importance distribution that guides a saliency-aware merging strategy, enabling consolidation of redundant tokens rather than wholesale removal. The framework is training-free and designed for 3D medical VLMs; evaluation is conducted on CT-RATE and MR-RATE datasets.

Result: Token overhead and inference time are reduced while preserving or improving diagnostic performance. The multi-cue scoring cost is outweighed by the savings from processing fewer tokens, with larger benefits expected for larger language models.

Conclusion: MedARC provides a unified, training-free approach to adaptive visual-token redundancy compression in 3D medical VLMs, achieving efficiency gains and maintaining diagnostic effectiveness, particularly beneficial for larger language models.

Abstract: Integrating 3D medical images with vision-language models (VLMs) holds substantial promise for computer-aided diagnosis. However, volumetric images generate prohibitively long visual-token sequences with considerable spatial and inter-slice redundancy. Existing token compression methods typically apply uniform reduction or rely on a single importance signal, increasing the risk of removing regions that are clinically relevant to the query or structurally distinctive. To address this limitation, we propose MedARC, a unified, training-free framework for Adaptive Redundancy Compression of visual tokens in 3D medical VLMs. MedARC estimates token importance by integrating three complementary cues: self-attention from the VLM vision encoder, which reflects the model's intrinsic visual focus; similarity between projected visual tokens and text embeddings, which identifies query-relevant regions; and deviations of local visual foundation model features from the volume-level feature center, which highlight structurally distinctive anatomy. The resulting importance distribution guides a saliency-aware merging strategy that preserves informative tokens while consolidating redundant ones rather than simply discarding them. Experiments on CT-RATE and MR-RATE show that MedARC reduces visual-token overhead and inference time while preserving or improving diagnostic performance. Its multi-cue scoring cost is outweighed by the savings from processing fewer tokens, with greater benefits expected for larger language models.

</details>


### [32] [Representation Trajectories Matters: Complementary Evidence for OOD Detection and Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26565)
*Ignacio M. De la Jara,Cristian Rodriguez-Opazo,Hamed Damirchi,Stephen Gould,Damith Ranasinghe*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper studies how representations evolve across depth in vision models, distinguishing class-coherent transport from input-specific innovation, and shows that the computed computation paths yield reliable signals for OOD detection and clean data classification, complementing final-state detectors across diverse architectures and shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether intermediate-state transitions contain evidence about what the final representation discards/retains and whether such evidence can improve reliability signals for OOD and clean data beyond final-layer classifiers.

Method: Track sample identity across depth to analyze transformations between successive states, separating class-coherent transport from input-specific innovation and coordination of movement vs relational reorganization. Apply this analysis across supervised, self-supervised, vision–language, hierarchical, and convolutional encoders. Define and evaluate an ID-only transition-surprise score and Frozen update probes, using open-domain OOD benchmarks (OpenOOD) across architectural families.

Result: Found strong sample-specific continuity and architecture-specific depth profiles that recur across datasets. The ID-only transition-surprise score complements final-state detectors, reducing FPR95 in 131/152 non-saturated comparisons on OpenOOD; gains larger for visually disruptive and semantically distant shifts, with positive effects on near-OOD for most detectors. Frozen update probes improve 71/72 clean model–dataset cases; shifted-data gains vary by architecture and corruption type.

Conclusion: Computation paths provide a broadly useful reliability signal shaped by model organization and the encountered shift, offering complementary detection signals to final representations and enhancing robustness across architectures and data shifts.

Abstract: Vision models do not form a representation at once; each block revises it. We ask whether the resulting computation path contains evidence that the final representation discards, and whether that evidence improves OOD detection and image classification on clean and shifted data. Unlike approaches that treat intermediate layers as separate snapshots, we retain sample identity across depth and study the transformations connecting successive states. We separate class-coherent transport from input-specific innovation, and coordinate movement from relational reorganization. Across supervised, self-supervised, vision--language, hierarchical, and convolutional encoders, these paths show strong sample-specific continuity and architecture-specific depth profiles that recur across datasets. They are also practically useful. An ID-only transition-surprise score complements strong final-state detectors, reducing FPR95 in 131/152 non-saturated comparisons on a balanced OpenOOD grid; gains are largest for visually disruptive and semantically far shifts, and remain positive on near-OOD for most detectors. Frozen update probes improve 71/72 clean model--dataset cases, while shifted-data gains vary with architecture and corruption type. Computation paths therefore provide a broadly useful reliability signal whose value is determined jointly by model organization and the shift encountered.

</details>


### [33] [3DGBGS: 3D Granular Ball Gaussian Splatting for Compact Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26578)
*Meng Yang,Shuyin Xia,Dawei Dai,YiWang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 3DGBGS is an adaptive granular-ball extension of 3D Gaussian Splatting that partitions SfM point clouds into granular balls to create compact anchors and scale priors, achieving fewer anchors and lower storage with rendering quality on par with baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address inefficiencies of fixed-voxel anchor construction in anchor-based 3D Gaussian Splatting by enabling adaptive, non-uniform representation using Granular Ball Computing (GBC).

Method: Propose 3DGBGS: adaptive partitioning of SfM point clouds into 3D granular balls; larger balls represent smooth regions, smaller balls capture detail. Introduce Granular Ball Anchor Initialization (GBAI) using ball centers as anchors and Granular Ball Scale Prior (GBSP) using ball radii as local scale priors for Gaussian generation.

Result: On four benchmarks, initial anchors reduced by 37.1%, final anchors by 10.0%, and model storage by 9.8% on average, with rendering quality remaining comparable to state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: Adaptive granular-ball representation enables a more compact anchor-based 3D Gaussian Splatting without sacrificing rendering quality, validating the synergy between Granular Ball Computing and 3DGS for efficient novel-view synthesis.

Abstract: Three-dimensional Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables high-quality real-time novel-view synthesis through explicit Gaussian primitives and differentiable rasterization. 3DGS and Granular Ball Computing (GBC), proposed in 2019, share a natural compatibility in adaptive representation. The efficiency of 3DGS partly stems from a coarse-to-fine and on-demand refinement process that draws on the generation principle of GBC. This connection motivates us to further introduce adaptive granular ball organization into anchor-based 3DGS. Existing anchor-based methods typically construct anchors from sparse SfM point clouds through fixed voxelization, which cannot adequately adapt to spatially non-uniform point distributions and leads to a trade-off among anchor count, model compactness, and rendering quality. To address this issue, we propose 3DGBGS (3D Granular Ball Gaussian Splatting), a compact anchor-based framework for novel-view synthesis. 3DGBGS adaptively partitions SfM point clouds into 3D granular balls, using larger balls to compactly represent smooth and redundant regions and smaller balls to preserve complex geometry and local details. Based on this representation, Granular Ball Anchor Initialization (GBAI) uses granular ball centers to initialize compact anchor positions, while the Granular Ball Scale Prior (GBSP) exploits granular ball radii to provide local scale priors for Gaussian generation. Experiments on four benchmarks show that 3DGBGS reduces initial and final anchors by 37.1% and 10.0%, respectively, and model storage by 9.8% on average, while maintaining comparable rendering quality.

</details>


### [34] [Classification of Disease from Lungs X-ray Images using VGG16, VGG19 and ResNet50 Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26580)
*Nand Lal Yadav,Rajesh Kumar,Satyendra Singh,Sudhakar Singh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ResNet-50 outperforms VGG16/VGG19 in multi-class chest X-ray disease classification (pneumonia, TB, lung cancer, normal); shows potential for clinical deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing burden of respiratory diseases necessitates early, accurate diagnosis; imaging-based deep learning offers improved detection and rapid screening.

Method: Comparative evaluation of three CNN architectures (VGG16, VGG19, ResNet50) trained on a large chest X-ray dataset to classify four classes: pneumonia, tuberculosis, lung cancer, and normal.

Result: All three models yielded good performance; ResNet-50 achieved the best accuracy and computational efficiency among the three.

Conclusion: CNN-based approaches, particularly ResNet-50, show promise for aiding clinical diagnosis of pulmonary diseases and enabling earlier detection.

Abstract: With the increase in the number of cases related to respiratory diseases, there is an urgent need to detect them early and diagnose them accurately. Convolutional neural networks have given promising results when used for diagnosing diseases using imaging tests. In this study, we investigate the potential of applying deep learning algorithms such as VGG16, VGG19, and ResNet50 for classification of lung ailments based on X-ray images. A detailed analysis of the aforementioned models' performances was conducted to assess how well they can classify various types of lung ailments, including pneumonia, tuberculosis, lung cancer, and normal lungs. In order to do that, these deep learning models were trained on a vast amount of X-ray images. The results of our study show that while all three models provide good results, ResNet-50 performs best in comparison with other models due to its efficiency and high level of accuracy. We believe that these deep learning models can be successfully implemented in the practice of diagnosing pulmonary diseases in the future. It helps with early disease detection and improves patient outcomes.

</details>


### [35] [Level, Sharpness, and Corpus: Why Zero-Shot OOD Detector Rankings Do Not Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26582)
*Ignacio M. De la Jara,Cristian Rodriguez-Opazo,Stephen Gould,Damith Ranasinghe*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Zero-shot OOD detector rankings do not transfer across deployments; a detector that performs best on one in-distribution dataset may underperform on another. The authors propose Complementary Evidence Guard (CEG) to fuse base detector signals with level and sharpness features, improving robustness without extra data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark-driven selection assumes portability of detectors; across 17 in-distribution datasets, 3 Vision-Language Models, and 7 detectors, rankings reverse by deployment. Complementary evidence channels (absolute match, relative/spatial sharpness, and WordNet-based semantic coverage) drive non-transferability.

Method: Conduct a controlled portability audit across 17 ID datasets, 3 VLMs, 7 detectors. Analyze evidence channels. Propose CEG, a detector-agnostic wrapper performing non-compensatory fusion of base detector, level, and sharpness using only in-distribution percentiles. Evaluate without OOD samples, auxiliary corpora, or learned fusion; compare GL-MCM and MCM FPR95 improvements.

Result: Detector rankings reverse across deployments; every detector yields FPR95 > 80% on at least one domain. CEG reduces detector sensitivity, achieving GL-MCM 28.8 from 38.1 and MCM 30.5 from 42.6 (percentage points). Controls with entropy, logit variance, or random noise do not reproduce gains.

Conclusion: No single detector transfers reliably across deployments. CEG provides a detector-agnostic wrapper that preserves complementary evidence and yields consistent robustness gains without extra data or learned fusion.

Abstract: Selecting a zero-shot out-of-distribution (OOD) detector for a new deployment is typically based on benchmark rankings, implicitly assuming that the highest-ranked detector will transfer across domains. We show that this assumption does not hold. Through a controlled portability audit across seventeen in-distribution datasets, three vision-language models, and seven representative zero-shot OOD detectors, we find that detector rankings reverse across deployments, every detector exceeds $80\%$ FPR95 on at least one domain, and the preferred detector depends on both the in-distribution data and the underlying VLM. We trace these reversals to complementary evidence channels in vision-language logits. Corpus-free detectors rely on different combinations of absolute match level and relative or spatial sharpness, while WordNet-based methods additionally depend on external semantic coverage. A simple proposition shows that level and sharpness cannot generally be recovered from one another, explaining why no single detector transfers reliably across deployments. Motivated by this diagnosis, we introduce the Complementary Evidence Guard (CEG), a detector-agnostic wrapper that preserves complementary evidence through a non-compensatory fusion of the base detector, level, and sharpness using only empirical in-distribution percentiles. Controls replacing these channels with entropy, logit variance, or random noise do not reproduce the gains. Without OOD samples, auxiliary corpora, or learned fusion, CEG reduces detector sensitivity and improves GL-MCM from $38.1$ to $28.8$ and MCM from $42.6$ to $30.5$ family-balanced FPR95.

</details>


### [36] [R-SLPR: Region-based Small-to-Large Point-cloud Registration with Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26583)
*Yusen Wan,Zeyuan Chen,Qianshi Zou,Xu Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes R-SLPR, a three-stage region-based registration framework to align a small, partial point cloud to a much larger reference under severe scale mismatch, using region proposals, regional matching with Fibonacci Grid Segmentation and contrastive learning, followed by Cascade Anchor Selection and Refinement; achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on ModelNet40.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current learning-based point-cloud registration often assumes substantial overlap and similar scale between source and reference. In real-world settings, partial observations and scale mismatches hinder performance, motivating a shift from global alignment to local-region-based registration.

Method: A three-stage architecture: (1) Region proposal to identify candidate local regions in both source and target; (2) Regional matching using Fibonacci Grid Segmentation to generate local patches and a contrastive objective to robustly match them; (3) Iterative refinement via Cascade Anchor Selection and Refinement to progressively align the source with the target region, handling scale differences.

Result: On ModelNet40, the approach sets a new state-of-the-art, achieving markedly lower position and rotation MAE (0.009 and 1.104 respectively) compared to prior methods.

Conclusion: Region-based registration with explicit regional localization and iterative refinement effectively mitigates scale mismatch, enabling robust alignment for partial-to-global PC registration and offering strong empirical performance gains.

Abstract: Point-cloud (PC) registration is fundamental to three-dimensional (3D) perception in robotic systems. However, classic registration algorithms falter when aligning a source PC containing limited, incomplete, or ambiguous geometric cues against a reference. This challenge of registering a small, partial PC to a significantly larger global reference is pervasive in real-world deployment yet remains insufficiently addressed by existing learning-based approaches, which typically assume comparable scales and significant overlap. To bridge this gap, we propose the Region-based Small-to-Large Point-cloud Registra- tion framework (R-SLPR), a novel three-stage architecture that fundamentally reformulates the scale-mismatched registration problem into a sequence of region proposal, regional matching, and iterative refinement. Unlike conventional methods that fail to localize specific regions, R-SLPR explicitly identifies candidate regions prior to estimating rigid transformations, ensuring robust alignment even under severe scale mismatch. The framework introduces a Fibonacci Grid Segmentation method coupled with a contrastive learning objective to effectively generate and match local geometric patches. Building on this, a novel Cascade Anchor Selection and Refinement algorithm iteratively aligns the source with the target region to maximize precision. Extensive evaluation on ModelNet40 demonstrates that R-SLPR establishes a new state-of-the-art accuracy standard, outperforming prior approaches and significantly reducing position and rotation Mean Absolute Error (MAE) to 0.009 and 1.104, respectively.

</details>


### [37] [SpatialQ: Understanding 3D Gaussian Splatting Scene Quality via Visual-based MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26595)
*Jingxuan Su,Shenglin Wang,Tiesong Zhao,Ge Li,Wei Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a 3D-aware, multimodal quality assessment framework for 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) that goes beyond 2D perceptual cues by integrating view-wise representations, cross-view consistency, and geometry cues, followed by grounded multimodal reasoning with an MLLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current IQA methods rely on 2D perceptual cues and MLLMs are not suited for stable quality regression, which is inadequate for 3DGS where scene-level structure and cross-view consistency are crucial.

Method: 1) Develop a 3D-aware quality representation by augmenting a VGGT-based encoder with a dedicated quality head; encode multi-view images into view-specific features, aggregate for cross-view consistency, and incorporate depths and point-cloud structure through joint modeling. 2) Build a grounded multimodal reasoning module by feeding images, depth maps, point cloud renderings, and camera parameters into a Qwen-based MLLM.

Result: The framework yields structure-aware quality representations that capture both appearance and geometry cues and enables grounded reasoning across modalities to assess 3DGS quality more reliably than pure 2D IQA or generic MLLMs.

Conclusion: A dedicated 3D-aware representation plus a grounded multimodal reasoning component can improve quality assessment for 3DGS scenes by considering cross-view consistency and geometric structure, beyond traditional 2D appearance-based metrics.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as an effective representation for novel view synthesis and 3D scene reconstruction, creating an increasing demand for reliable quality assessment. Unlike conventional image quality assessment (IQA), the quality of a 3DGS scene depends not only on the perceptual fidelity of rendered views, but also on scene-level factors such as spatial structure and cross-view consistency. Existing IQA methods are limited by their reliance on 2D perceptual cues, whereas general multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are not designed for stable quality regression and may produce unreliable judgments. To address these limitations, a multimodal quality assessment framework is developed for 3DGS scene understanding. First, a 3D-aware quality representation learning framework is introduced by augmenting a VGGT-based encoder with a dedicated quality head. Multi-view images are encoded into view-specific features and aggregated to capture cross-view consistency, while geometric cues are incorporated through joint modeling of depth and point-cloud-related structural information, enabling the learning of structure-aware quality representations beyond appearance-driven features. Second, a grounded multimodal reasoning mechanism is constructed by jointly feeding original images, depth maps, point cloud renderings, and camera parameters into a Qwen-based MLLM.

</details>


### [38] [HumanCLAW: Can Vision-Language Models Act Through a Body?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27180)
*Siyao Li,Jiawei Gu,Shuai Liu,Kairui Hu,Zekun Li,Linjie Li,Chengcheng Tang,Po-Chen Wu,Ivan Shugurov,Lingni Ma,Michael Zollhoefer,Sizhe An,Abhay Mittal,Amy Zhao,Ranjay Krishna,Manling Li,Ziwei Liu,Chuan Guo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HumanCLAW introduces a decoupled evaluation of embodied VLMs by executing atomic skill commands as brief, full-body motions, enabling measurement of decision-making independent of motor execution; benchmark results show current VLMs lack embodied self-awareness, with best success 16.8%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To separate high-level decision making from low-level motor control in embodied vision-language models to diagnose whether failures arise from planning or execution.

Method: Define a harnessed VLM that outputs atomic skill commands; translate each command into sub-second full-body motion incorporating physics; observe effects in real-world environment; build HumanCLAW-Bench with 1,218 long-horizon egocentric episodes across 41 indoor scenes; evaluate nine state-of-the-art VLMs.

Result: None of the tested models solved the benchmark; best model achieves 16.8% success; findings highlight that target recognition is not the bottleneck, but lack of embodied self-awareness and body-state tracking undermines performance.

Conclusion: The framework successfully isolates decision-making from execution, revealing important cognitive-physical alignment gaps in current VLMs; provides a benchmark for embodied evaluation and points toward integrating embodied self-awareness and reliable body-state estimation in future VLMs.

Abstract: Evaluating whether a vision-language model (VLM) can act through a physical body is challenging. The outcome of an action couples the VLM's decision with motor control. When a task fails, it is hard to tell whether the VLM made a bad choice or the motor controller simply failed to execute it, e.g., losing balance and falling. In this work, we introduce HumanCLAW, an evaluation framework that decouples action decision-making from low-level execution. At every step, a harnessed, off-the-shelf VLM issues an atomic skill command, and the command is translated into a sub-second chunk of continuous full-body motion with real physical consequences, including gravity and collisions. The body can therefore act freely in the physical world, while execution-side disturbances, balance and motor errors, are factored out. What remains measurable is the model's action intelligence: its moment-to-moment choice of what the body should execute next. Based on this framework, we build HumanCLAW-Bench: 1,218 long-horizon, egocentric find-navigate-interact episodes across 41 indoor scenes. We test nine state-of-the-art VLMs and find that none solves the benchmark; the best model reaches only a 16.8% success rate. Recognizing the target is not the bottleneck. What current VLMs lack is embodied self-awareness: they lose track of their own body, failing to tell where it is, whether it has reached the goal, or whether it has hit an obstacle.

</details>


### [39] [Forensic Reproducibility Audit of a Radiology Vision-Language Model Benchmark: From Intended Protocol to Released Artifact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25589)
*Mateusz Kozłowski*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A retrospective forensic reproducibility audit of a preserved chest-radiograph vision-language model reveals pervasive provenance and artifact issues across prompts, DICOM rendering, and automated labels, undermining reported performance and prompting withdrawal of claims; emphasizes need for machine-verifiable controls for cohort, rendering, identity, provenance, and derived artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess reproducibility and provenance in medical-imaging AI benchmarks by auditing archived artifacts (prompts, DICOM metadata, outputs, labels, analyses, and releases) to determine whether reported performance claims are reproducible and properly supported.

Method: Audit of 300 planned model-prompt calls with 297 nonempty reports; analysis of 60 Claude/A labeled calls with a common C prompt; 30 studies representing 28 patients; identification of rendering and provenance gaps (MONOCHROME1 polarity inversion, missing dataset split, extractor truncation); reconstruction of a common cohort of 369 complete case-finding blocks; re-evaluation of Cochran's Q and McNemar tests; Holm adjustment for multiple comparisons; assessment that reported metrics reflect only archived automated-labels, not the intended prompt comparison or clinical performance.

Result: Reveals incomplete rendering (polarity inversion issues), missing dataset splits, truncated reports, and limited provenance; statistical findings (Cochran's Q, McNemar) are sensitive to data reconstruction and do not establish clinical performance; many results vanish under multiple-testing correction; majority of claims cannot be substantiated from archived labels and prompts.

Conclusion: Original performance, rankings, and prompt-effect claims are withdrawn; proposes machine-verifiable controls for cohort definition, DICOM rendering identity, prompt/model identity, call status, annotation provenance, keyed analyses, and derived artifacts to improve reproducibility.

Abstract: Medical-imaging AI benchmarks combine datasets, DICOM rendering, prompts, provider APIs, automated labels, statistical code, manuscripts, and repository releases. Agreement across these artifacts is usually assumed rather than tested. We performed a retrospective forensic reproducibility audit of a preserved chest-radiograph vision-language model (VLM) pilot; no model was called again and no image or report was newly annotated. We traced prompt bindings, DICOM metadata, output completeness, label extraction, matched analyses, and release propagation. Of 300 planned model-prompt calls, 297 yielded nonempty reports. Sixty Claude calls labeled A/B were executed with the same C prompt. The 30 studies represented 28 patients. Four MONOCHROME1 images were rendered without required polarity inversion, dataset split membership was not retained, and the unvalidated extractor truncated five reports to 4000 characters. Reconstructing one common cohort of 369 complete case-finding blocks changed Cochran's Q from 154.73 to 182.29. Of 45 McNemar comparisons, 27 had unadjusted p < 0.05 and 20 remained below 0.05 after Holm adjustment. These values describe only the archived automated-label matrix; they do not recover the intended prompt comparison or establish clinical performance. We withdraw the original performance, ranking, prompt-effect, and clinical claims and specify machine-verifiable controls for cohort, DICOM rendering, prompt and model identity, call status, annotation provenance, keyed analysis, and derived artifacts.

</details>


### [40] [Decoupled Visual Processing: Efficient Multimodal Adaptation via Modality-Specific Transformer Substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26596)
*Mingkuan Feng,Zhengqi Wen,Jianhua Tao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DVP decouples visual processing from the main transformer by routing visual tokens through a single trainable block after partial decoding, freezing the textual path, to reduce trainable parameters while maintaining competitive performance on MLLM benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning all parameters of multimodal LLMs is computationally expensive; in deeper layers, visual and textual representations diverge, suggesting a decoupled, parameter-efficient approach could suffice for visual adaptation.

Method: Replace the upper decoder layers of a pretrained LLM with a lightweight, independently trainable single transformer block dedicated to processing visual tokens. After half of the decoder, split tokens: visual tokens go through the new block, textual tokens continue through the frozen decoder. Concatenate streams before the language modeling head. During training, only the single block is updated.

Result: On LLaVA-1.5, DVP achieves competitive performance on MME, POPE, and ChartQA while updating only a fraction of the total parameters, demonstrating effective decoupled visual learning in MLLMs.

Conclusion: Visual representations in multimodal LLMs can be learned via a parameter-efficient, decoupled pathway without extensive fine-tuning of the entire model; this approach yields favorable efficiency-performance trade-offs.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities by integrating visual and textual understanding within a unified transformer architecture. However, fine-tuning all parameters of these models for visual instruction tuning is computationally expensive and often unnecessary, as the representation requirements for visual and textual tokens diverge significantly in the deeper layers of the network. In this paper, we propose Decoupled Visual Processing (DVP), an efficient training framework that replaces the upper decoder layers of a pretrained LLM with a lightweight, independently trainable single transformer block dedicated exclusively to visual token processing. Specifically, after shared processing through the first half of the decoder layers, visual and textual tokens are split: visual tokens are routed through a newly initialized single transformer block while textual tokens continue through the original frozen decoder layers. The two streams are then concatenated before the language modeling head. During training, only the single transformer block is updated, dramatically reducing the number of trainable parameters. Experiments on the LLaVA-1.5 framework demonstrate that DVP achieves competitive performance on MME, POPE, and ChartQA benchmarks while training only a fraction of the total parameters, suggesting that visual representations in MLLMs can be effectively learned through a decoupled, parameter-efficient pathway.

</details>


### [41] [VidMap: Exploiting Temporal Structure for Video-Based Structure-from-Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27194)
*Zador Pataki,Paul-Edouard Sarlin,Marc Pollefeys*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A unified SLAM-SfM framework that recovers camera calibration and metric poses from long uncalibrated video by blending sequential constraints, wide-baseline matching, loop closure via temporal ordering, and depth priors; outperforms state-of-the-art SLAM/SfM; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of SLAM (initialization sensitivity, transient failures, real-time constraints, reliance on known calibration) and SfM (no inherent image ordering, reliance on global optimization, struggles with symmetries and extreme motions). Propose a hybrid system for robust, offline metric reconstruction from unconstrained videos.

Method: Integrates strong sequential constraints with offline global optimization; leverages wide-baseline dense image matching; treats temporal ordering as a first-class citizen for reliable loop closure; augments global optimization with metric monocular depth priors.

Result: Evaluations on diverse, challenging datasets with extreme motion and visual symmetries show the approach is significantly more robust and accurate than both state-of-the-art SLAM and SfM, classical or learned, with either known or unknown camera calibration.

Conclusion:  Enables robust, accurate metric reconstruction for arbitrary, long, uncalibrated videos; bridges SLAM and SfM into a practical pipeline; code is publicly available.

Abstract: Accurately recovering the camera's calibration and metric poses for any unconstrained video would unlock large-scale training data for navigation and scene understanding. The dominant approaches to this problem are severely limited: Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) is sensitive to initialization and transient failures due to its causal, incremental nature; it is often over-optimized for real-time operation and generally requires known camera calibration; while Structure-from-Motion (SfM) typically forgoes any image ordering, enabling optimal initialization and global optimization, but lacks robustness to visual symmetries and extreme motions. To bridge this gap, we introduce a system that combines the strong sequential constraints of SLAM with the flexibility and global optimization of offline SfM, enabling the metric reconstruction of arbitrary, long, uncalibrated videos. This system leverages recent advances in wide-baseline dense image matching, treats temporal ordering as a first-class citizen for reliable loop closure, and augments global optimization with metric monocular depth priors. As a result, thorough evaluations on diverse, challenging datasets that exhibit extreme motion and visual symmetries reveal that our approach is significantly more robust and accurate than both state-of-the-art SLAM and SfM, classical or learned, with given or unknown camera calibration. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/cvg/vidmap.

</details>


### [42] [JEPADepth: Masked Predictive Representation Learning for Self-Supervised Monocular Depth Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26600)
*Ionuţ Grigore,Călin-Adrian Popa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: JEPADepth adds an I-JEPA-inspired representation-space objective to a standard self-supervised monocular depth pipeline, using a fixed pretrained DINOv3 encoder and a masked predictor. It yields consistent KITTI gains, strong zero-shot transfer, and incurs no extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome the limitations of photometric reconstruction losses that couple depth, pose, and appearance by introducing a representation-space self-supervised objective inspired by I-JEPA, to improve depth estimation without increasing deployment complexity.

Method: Augment a photometric monocular depth pipeline with a masked prediction loss in the representation space of a pretrained DINOv3 Vision Transformer. A predictor reconstructs target-region embeddings from visible context embeddings under structured masking; the predictor and target encoder are discarded at inference, leaving inference unchanged.

Result: On KITTI, the JEPA objective consistently improves the DINOv3-based photometric baseline, competes with state-of-the-art transformer methods, and outperforms CNN baselines. In zero-shot transfer (trained on KITTI), it achieves best or near-best performance on Make3D and Cityscapes across multiple metrics.

Conclusion: Incorporating an I-JEPA-inspired representation-space objective into self-supervised monocular depth estimation improves accuracy without extra inference costs, demonstrating effective use of self-supervised representation learning for depth.

Abstract: Self-supervised monocular depth estimation typically relies on photometric reconstruction losses that couple depth, pose, and appearance assumptions. In this paper, we propose JEPADepth, a self-supervised monocular depth framework that incorporates a complementary training objective inspired by Image Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (I-JEPA) for self-supervised depth learning. Our method augments a standard photometric pipeline with a masked prediction loss computed in the representation space of a pretrained DINOv3 Vision Transformer encoder. A predictor infers target-region embeddings from visible context-region embeddings under structured masking, and is discarded along with the target encoder at inference time, adding no deployment cost. On KITTI, adding the JEPA objective consistently improves performance over the same DINOv3-based photometric baseline, without changing the inference-time architecture. Compared to prior monocular self-supervised methods, JEPADepth is competitive with state-of-the-art transformer-based approaches and outperforms strong CNN-based baselines on the standard benchmark. In zero-shot transfer (trained on KITTI and evaluated without fine-tuning), JEPADepth achieves the best or near-best performance among the compared methods on both Make3D and Cityscapes across multiple metrics.

</details>


### [43] [TurboVLA: Real-Time Vision-Language-Action Model at 32 Hz on an RTX 4090 with <1 GB VRAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27205)
*Hengyi Xie,Chenfei Yao,Xianjin Wu,Xuanyang Xi,Yiping Tang,Di Xu,Yingying Zhu,Dingkang Liang,Xiang Bai,Han Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TurboVLA reframes VLA from a heavy V→L→A pipeline to a direct V+L→A mapping, enabling task-conditioned action prediction via lightweight, bidirectional vision-language interaction and a compact decoder, dramatically reducing compute/memory while preserving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the substantial computation and memory overhead inherent in LLM-centric VLA systems while maintaining competitive or superior robotic manipulation performance.

Method: Independent visualization and language encoding with a lightweight bidirectional V-L interface; directly map fused visual and linguistic features to continuous action chunks using a compact decoder, forming task-conditioned representations without using a central LLM as the intermediary.

Result: On LIBERO, TurboVLA achieves 97.7% average success with 0.2B parameters, 31.2 ms latency, and 0.9 GB VRAM on a consumer RTX 4090, matching or surpassing larger VLA policies; code available at the provided GitHub repository.

Conclusion: TurboVLA provides a simple, efficient alternative to LLM-centric VLA, showing that direct V+L to A mapping with lightweight interaction can match state-of-the-art performance while significantly reducing resource usage.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models commonly adopt an LLM-centric $V \to L \to A$ pathway, where visual observations are projected into the representation space of a large language model before being decoded into robot actions. Although effective, this design incurs substantial computation and memory overhead at every policy invocation. In this work, we introduce TurboVLA, a new VLA paradigm that reformulates the conventional $V \to L \to A$ pathway as a direct $V + L \to A$ mapping. Instead of using a large language model as the central interface between perception and action, TurboVLA independently encodes visual observations and language instructions, directly exchanges information between them through lightweight bidirectional vision-language interaction, and predicts continuous action chunks with a compact decoder. This simple design constructs task-conditioned representations directly from visual and linguistic features, significantly reducing the computational and memory costs of VLA inference. On LIBERO, TurboVLA achieves 97.7% average success with only 0.2B parameters, 31.2 ms inference latency, and 0.9 GB inference VRAM on a consumer-grade RTX 4090, matching or outperforming substantially larger VLA policies. These results establish TurboVLA as a simple and effective alternative to the prevailing LLM-centric VLA paradigm, offering a new perspective on how vision, language, and action can be connected for efficient robotic manipulation. Code is available at https://github.com/H-EmbodVis/TurboVLA.

</details>


### [44] [Understanding Knowledge Transfer Mechanism in Heterogeneous MLLM Fusion: A Simple Linear Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26608)
*Yinghao Hou,Jiahe Fan,Yuanhao Pu,Zongyuan Chen,Hong Xie*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CDPI reveals cross-scale transfer in heterogeneous multimodal LLMs is selective for high-level reasoning, mainly from the language model, and occurs in a small-ratio, low-interference regime, not broad inheritance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what a smaller model actually inherits during training-free cross-scale fusion of heterogeneous multimodal LLMs, and how capability transfer behaves when evaluation spans broader task collections.

Method: Introduce Cross-Scale Directional Parameter Injection (CDPI), a simple linear probe to analyze cross-scale knowledge transfer during heterogeneous fusion. Theoretical analysis shows first-order responses to a shared injection direction determine transfer selectivity, while second-order curvature constrains the regime. Empirical evaluation across four Qwen3-VL model pairs and twelve multimodal benchmarks, with component-wise ablations and ratio analyses.

Result: A consistent pattern of selectivity is observed: gains concentrate on reasoning—especially high-level reasoning—while perception performance remains close to the original target model. High-level reasoning gains mainly originate from the language model; positive selective transfer occurs primarily in the small-ratio regime.

Conclusion: Cross-scale heterogeneous MLLM fusion is best characterized as selective language-side reasoning transfer within a narrow, low-interference regime, rather than broad inheritance of capabilities.

Abstract: Training-free fusion of heterogeneous multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provides a direct route for cross-scale capability transfer, yet improvements in aggregate performance do not reveal what a smaller model actually inherits. Existing studies are largely designed and evaluated on limited task sets or aggregate metrics; as evaluation expands to broader task collections, whether different capabilities can transfer across scales remains poorly understood. To investigate this question, we introduce Cross-Scale Directional Parameter Injection (CDPI), a simple linear probe to analyze cross-scale knowledge transfer during heterogeneous fusion. A local theoretical analysis indicates that knowledge transfer selectivity is determined at first order by capability-dependent responses to a shared injection direction, while second-order curvature effects constrain the effective transfer regime. Across four Qwen3-VL model pairs and twelve multimodal benchmarks, our experiments reveal a consistent pattern of selectivity: gains concentrate on reasoning, particularly high-level reasoning, whereas perception performance remains close to that of the original target model. Component-wise ablations further show that high-level reasoning gains arise primarily from the language model, while ratio analysis finds that positive selective transfer occurs mainly in the small-ratio regime. These findings recast cross-scale heterogeneous MLLM fusion as selective language-side reasoning transfer within a narrow, low-interference regime, rather than broad capability inheritance.

</details>


### [45] [FakeIDet3-DB: Refining Digital Attacks and Patch Extraction for Secure ID Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26641)
*Muñoz-Haro Javier,Teruel Andres,Tolosana Ruben,DeAlcala Daniel,Vera-Rodriguez Ruben,Morales Aythami,Fierrez Julian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes FakeIDet3-DB, a privacy-preserving dataset of real ID manipulations (classical and AI-driven) with 5.2M patches; introduces PACE for patch extraction; baseline evaluations show detectors struggle to catch manipulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ID authentication relies on complex security patterns and real IDs; privacy rules prevent using real data for forensic training, creating a domain gap with synthetic templates; need realistic, privacy-compliant datasets and robust extraction methods for forensic evaluation.

Method: Develop FakeIDet3-DB with classical and Generative AI manipulations (copy-move, face-swapping, inpainting) plus image refinement; apply a GDPR-compliant, patch-based framework via PACE (Pseudo-Anonymized Contextual patch Extraction) using Integral Image mapping and distance-driven NMS to extract ~5.2M patches from 6.4K real/fake IDs; evaluate with state-of-the-art forensic models on detection and localization.

Result: State-of-the-art models struggle to detect and locate manipulations: ~32.45% EER for detection and ~83.48% AUC-ROC for localization.

Conclusion: The dataset and PACE method address privacy constraints in forensic training and reveal a domain gap between real-ID patterns and synthetic templates; further improvements in detection/localization are needed and broader ID-type coverage should be explored.

Abstract: Identity document (ID) authentication relies on the structural integrity of complex, high-frequency security patterns. However, advanced Generative AI models can now inject localized, high-fidelity manipulations, creating deceptive attacks that bypass standard verification. Training robust image forensic models to detect these anomalies is hindered by privacy regulations, forcing reliance on synthetic templates lacking the intricate visual patterns of real IDs. To bridge this domain gap, we introduce FakeIDet3-DB, the first comprehensive database of digital manipulations on real, government-issued IDs. FakeIDet3-DB encompasses classical (e.g., copy-move) and Generative AI-driven manipulations (e.g., face-swapping, inpainting) enhanced with advanced image refinement procedures to suppress visual artifacts. In addition, to comply with strict data protection regulations (e.g., GDPR), we adopt a recently-proposed framework based on patches. In order to maximize forensic utility, we formulate privacy-aware patch extraction from a real ID as a geometrically constrained image processing problem. We propose PACE, a Pseudo-Anonymized Contextual patch Extraction algorithm, which leverages Integral Image mapping and distance-driven Non-Maximum Suppression (NMS). PACE efficiently contours anonymization masks that prevent Personally Identifiable Information (PII) leakage while maximizing semantic density in peri-censorship regions, yielding almost 5.2M patches extracted from more than 6.4K images from real/fake IDs. Furthermore, an extensive evaluation of the proposed FakeIDet3-DB is performed using state-of-the-art models, showcasing they all struggle to detect and locate attacks coming from generative and classic techniques (32.45\% EER in detection and 83.48\% AUC-ROC in localization).

</details>


### [46] [FPSGen: Flexible Point Cloud Scene Generation with BEV-Supported Transport Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26645)
*Wenzhe He,Meng Wang,JiaWei Qian,Jinfeng Xu,Ying Liu,Ruihui Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FPSGen introduces BEV-based, scan-free point source construction with a teacher-student OT-based transport to enable unconditional and cue-conditioned outdoor scene generation; achieves state-of-the-art metrics on SemanticKITTI and KITTI-360.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate train-inference mismatch in LiDAR-based point completion methods, which rely on noisy/partial scans and struggle when LiDAR is unavailable; to enable unconditional and cue-conditioned generation without leaning on partial scans.

Method: Constructs BEV priors (density, height, mask) from active cues; samples density to form a BEV-supported point source; supports unconditional and conditioned initialization; uses a teacher-student approximate optimal transport to learn a velocity field from teacher-predicted endpoints, straightening transport paths; integrates BEV source construction with path-straightening transport for unified generation.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD) and voxel IoU on SemanticKITTI completion; maintains strong performance with a single point transport step; achieves best Coverage (COV) on KITTI-360 unconditional generation among compared methods.

Conclusion: FPSGen offers a unified, flexible framework for unconditional and cue-conditioned outdoor scene generation that reduces reliance on partial scans by leveraging BEV-based point sources and transport-based generation, with strong empirical performance.

Abstract: Existing point-based generative methods for outdoor scenes primarily focus on LiDAR-conditioned completion. During training, noisy point clouds are constructed by perturbing complete ground-truth scenes, whereas during inference, they are initialized by adding noise to duplicated partial scans. This train-inference mismatch inherits the sparsity and visibility bias of partial scans, leading to sparse distant regions and incomplete geometry in occluded areas. Moreover, the reliance on partial scans restricts generation when LiDAR observations are unavailable or replaced by layout cues. We present FPSGen, a flexible framework that constructs point sources independently of partial scans. FPSGen first predicts a bird's-eye-view (BEV) prior with density, height, and mask channels from the active cues. The density map is then sampled to form a BEV-supported point source, enabling both unconditional and conditioned initialization. A teacher-student approximate optimal transport scheme then uses teacher-predicted endpoints to learn a velocity field that induces straighter transport paths. By integrating BEV point source construction with path-straightening transport, FPSGen provides a unified framework for unconditional and flexible cue-conditioned scene generation. Extensive experiments show that FPSGen achieves state-of-the-art JSD and voxel IoU performance on SemanticKITTI completion while maintaining strong performance with a single point transport step. On KITTI-360 unconditional generation, it also achieves the best Coverage (COV) among the compared methods.

</details>


### [47] [Genie Sim PanoWorld: An Infinite Indoor 3D World Generation Pipeline via Panoramic Scene Modeling and Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26646)
*Yongxin Su,Linjie Hou,Feng Wang,Jialin Tang,Zhijun Li,Qian Wang,Maoqing Yao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A trajectory-aware, two-stage pipeline (panorama video generation and 3D reconstruction) that converts a single 360° image into a real-time navigable 3D scene, using a NavMesh-guided SE(3) roaming latent diffusion model and a fast panoramic reconstructor; it outperforms geometry-conditioned baselines and generalizes zero-shot to unseen indoor scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-360° panorama reconstruction with faithful camera trajectories is challenging: existing methods struggle with achieving metric trajectory control, handle large disocclusions during long-range motion, or demand high-end compute and multi-view data. A method that jointly generates trajectory-aware panoramic video and reconstructs a metric 3D scene could enable real-time, simulation-ready assets for embodied AI without per-scene optimization.

Method: A two-stage feed-forward pipeline: (1) trajectory-controllable panoramic video generation where a NavMesh-planned SE(3) roaming trajectory is injected into a latent diffusion model via dense geometry-warped conditioning; long-short trajectory mixed training and a self-consistency objective based on shortcut models enable four CFG-free denoising steps; (2) a feed-forward panoramic reconstructor that lifts the generated video into a high-fidelity 3D Gaussian scene suitable for real-time free-viewpoint roaming and downstream embodied AI use.

Result: Genie Sim PanoWorld achieves higher fidelity in panoramic video generation and downstream 3D reconstruction than geometry-conditioned baselines, generalizes zero-shot to unseen indoor scenes, and supports real-time, free-viewpoint roaming with simulation-ready assets.

Conclusion: Trajectory-guided panoramic video generation coupled with a fast panoramic reconstructor yields high-fidelity, navigation-ready 3D scenes from a single panorama, reducing reliance on per-scene optimization and heavy multi-GPU setups while enabling immediate deployment in embodied AI applications.

Abstract: We address the problem of reconstructing a high-fidelity, freely navigable 3D scene from a single $360^\circ$ panorama, without per-scene optimization or multi-view capture. Existing methods either lack metric trajectory control, which hinders reliable downstream 3D reconstruction, or struggle with large disocclusions under long-range camera motion while requiring high-end multi-GPU servers.We present Genie Sim PanoWorld, a two-stage feed-forward pipeline that bridges generation and reconstruction via an explicit, trajectory-controllable panoramic video. A NavMesh-planned $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ roaming trajectory is injected into a latent video diffusion model through dense geometry-warped conditioning; long--short trajectory mixed training and a self-consistency objective based on shortcut models together yield high-fidelity video in four CFG-free denoising steps.
  A feed-forward panoramic reconstructor then lifts the generated video into a high-fidelity
  3D Gaussian scene that supports real-time, free-viewpoint roaming and can be directly used as a simulation-ready asset for embodied AI applications. Experiments show that Genie Sim PanoWorld
  outperforms geometry-conditioned baselines in both panoramic video generation and
  downstream 3D reconstruction, while generalizing zero-shot to unseen indoor scenes.

</details>


### [48] [Anchoring and Steering Diffusion: Enhancing the Faithfulness of Text-to-Image Generation at Inference Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26647)
*Xinyi Wang,Yuyang Huang,Yalin Su,Pengcheng Luan,Tao Zhang,Feiming Wei,Wenxian Yu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AnchorSteer is a training-free diffusion customization framework that improves text–image alignment by jointly optimizing initialization (Semantic Anchoring with CLIP priors and LP-SDS distillation) and the denoising trajectory (Reflective Steering with a Think–Erase–Retouch loop guided by Vision-Language model diagnosis). It yields stronger alignment with complex prompts while keeping high visual fidelity, outperforming baselines on GenEval and T2I-CompBench++.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models have strong priors and visual quality but poor alignment to complex, compositional prompts. Existing training-free methods either modify the initial noise without reliable semantic control or adjust the denoising path without early semantic error correction. There is a need for a training-free method that explicitly aligns both initialization and trajectory with semantic prompts.

Method: Semantic Anchoring: replace Gaussian initialization with CLIP-aligned initializations derived from CLIP priors; LP-SDS distills CLIP-derived priors into the diffusion model's latent prior to bridge the domain gap. Reflective Steering: implement a Think–Erase–Retouch loop that uses VLM-based diagnosis to detect semantic deviations mid-generation and perform targeted latent refinements to suppress incorrect content and recover missing attributes.

Result: Empirical evaluation on GenEval and T2I-CompBench++ shows AnchorSteer consistently outperforms existing training-free baselines in text–image alignment while preserving high visual quality.

Conclusion: A two-component, training-free framework that (1) aligns initialization with semantic prompts via CLIP priors and LP-SDS, and (2) enables mid-generation self-correction via a Reflective Steering loop guided by VLM diagnosis. Demonstrates improved alignment without sacrificing image fidelity across standard benchmarks.

Abstract: While text-to-image diffusion models achieve impressive visual quality, they frequently struggle to maintain precise alignment with complex compositional prompts. An effective strategy is to improve the inference process of diffusion models, thereby better leveraging their pretrained priors to address misalignment. Existing training-free methods can be divided into two categories. The first category focuses on improving the randomly sampled initial noise, either performing costly search over noise pools or manipulating sampled noise without ensuring reliable semantic injection. The second category focuses on improving the denoising trajectory, lacking explicit mechanisms to timely diagnose and correct semantic errors. we propose \textbf{AnchorSteer}, a training-free framework that exerts fine-grained control over \textbf{both initialization} and \textbf{the denoising trajectory}.
