### [147] [Emergent Sparsity in Frozen Random CNN Feature Extractors for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26059)
*Scott M. Norton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep RL agents with frozen random CNN feature extractors develop extremely sparse fully-connected representations in the first layer, using far fewer neurons than available. Sparsity scales with task complexity and appears to reflect intrinsic task structure rather than a fixed capacity fraction. Removing active neurons degrades performance, and the sparsity emerges early via an information bottleneck. The phenomenon persists across multiple games and PPO variants, suggesting the effective rank of the problem is revealed by gradient descent on the frozen projection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how, and under what conditions, sparse, task-relevant representations emerge in deep RL when feature extractors are fixed and randomly initialized, questioning the role of learned representations and the nature of the information bottleneck in policy performance.

Method: Train agents with frozen, randomly initialized CNN feature extractors across Pong, Breakout, and Space Invaders, using PPO variants. Analyze sparsity in FC1 (3,136 → 64), compare with trainable CNN baselines, perform ablations by removing active neurons, test across seeds/architectures, and examine the timing of information bottlenecks and reward emergence. Also contrast frozen vs trainable bottlenecks in Breakout.

Result: Sparse FC1 representations: 1–3 active neurons for deterministic Pong (5–11 for stochastic Pong); 19–26 for Breakout; ~42 for Space Invaders. Width scaling shows sparsity tracks task structure, not a fixed fraction. In Pong, three seeds yield 5, 7, 11 active neurons; higher-activity seeds reach expert rewards (+18.4 to +18.7) while the 5-neuron seed plateaus at +14. Ablations removing active neurons crash performance across PPO variants and games. The active set stabilizes by 15–30M steps, while positive reward emerges at 35–105M steps. In Breakout, frozen agents use 17–25 active neurons (participation ratio ~10–14) vs trainable 51 neurons (ratio ~3.6), yet both reach competitive rewards. The findings imply the apparent problem rank can be revealed by training a frozen random projection, without explicit sparsity incentives.

Conclusion: The input dimensionality far exceeding intrinsic task dimensionality allows gradient descent on frozen projections to discover the effective rank and informative subspaces needed for success. Sparse, task-structured representations can emerge without explicit sparsity objectives, driven by the relationship between input and task structure, and are crucial for performance when feature extractors are fixed.

Abstract: We report a striking phenomenon: deep reinforcement learning agents trained with frozen, randomly initialized CNN feature extractors spontaneously develop extremely sparse fully-connected representations, without any sparsity-inducing objective. In the first fully-connected layer (FC1, $3{,}136 \to 64$), agents compress task-relevant information through as few as 1-3 neurons out of 64 for deterministic Pong (5-11 for stochastic Pong), while trainable CNNs activate 55-64 neurons under matched conditions. We establish four principal findings. First, FC1 sparsity scales with task complexity: 1-11 for Pong, 19-26 for Breakout, and $\sim$42 for Space Invaders. Width-scaling confirms this reflects task structure rather than a fixed capacity fraction. Second, within-game scaling emerges: three identical Pong seeds produce 5, 7, and 11 active neurons. The 5-neuron seed plateaus at $+14$ reward, while the others reach expert performance ($+18.4$, $+18.7$), suggesting the random projection's usable dimensionality bounds achievable performance. Third, ablation confirms necessity: removing these active neurons crashes performance across two PPO implementations and four games. Fourth, the information bottleneck commits early: a sweep shows the active set locks by 15-30M steps, while reward turns positive 35-105M steps later. A complementary finding in Breakout shows frozen and trainable CNNs reach competitive rewards via structurally different bottlenecks: frozen agents use 17-25 active neurons (participation ratio $\sim$10-14), while trainable agents use 51 (participation ratio $\sim$3.6). Finally, wherever input dimensionality dwarfs intrinsic task dimensionality, gradient descent on a frozen random projection may reveal the effective rank of the underlying problem without explicit sparsity machinery.

</details>


### [148] [Sim2Win: A Team-Agnostic, Event-Based Pre-Match Outcome Prediction and Tactical Profiling System for Football](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26061)
*Mouad Zemzoumi,Amine Abouaomar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sim2Win proposes a team-agnostic, event-based pre-match framework reframing football outcome prediction as a tactical decision-support problem, achieving competitive generalization to unseen teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reliance on identity-based scouting that fails to generalize to unseen teams and to develop transferable predictive signals under distribution shift.

Method: Leverages StatsBomb open event data across 11 competitions (178 teams, 1,411 team-match records). Builds five-match rolling tactical profiles, derives four interpretable tactical feature ratios, clusters team behaviors into eight playstyles with K-Means, and trains thirteen classifiers to predict win/draw/loss from tactical representations. Operates without team names or identity features; evaluated with Leave-One-Competition-Out (LOCO).

Result: On completely unseen teams, mean ROC-AUC = 0.704 and mean accuracy = 55.4%. Sim2Win outperforms ELO, Pi-Rating, and GAP baselines on all 21 ROC-AUC comparisons and 19 of 21 accuracy comparisons. Among all models, CatBoost achieves strongest in-distribution accuracy at 60.90%.

Conclusion: Behavioral, identity-free tactical representations can provide transferable predictive signals under distribution shift and offer a viable alternative to identity-dependent football prediction systems.

Abstract: Pre-match tactical decision-making in professional football relies heavily on subjective expert analysis and identity-based scouting systems that cannot generalize to unseen teams. This paper presents Sim2Win, a team-agnostic, event-based pre-match tactical recommendation framework that reframes match outcome prediction as a tactical decision-support problem. Using StatsBomb open event data from eleven competitions spanning 178 teams and 1,411 team-match records, Sim2Win constructs five-match rolling tactical profiles, engineers four interpretable tactical feature ratios, clusters team behaviors into eight playstyles via K-Means, and trains thirteen classifiers to estimate win, draw, and loss probabilities from tactical matchup representations.
  The system operates without team names or identity features, enabling generalization to teams never seen during training. A rigorous Leave-One-Competition-Out (LOCO) evaluation demonstrates that Sim2Win achieves a mean ROC-AUC of 0.704 and mean accuracy of 55.4% on completely unseen teams, outperforming ELO, Pi-Rating, and GAP baselines on all 21 ROC-AUC comparisons and 19 of 21 accuracy comparisons. Among all evaluated models, CatBoost achieved the strongest in-distribution performance with 60.90% accuracy.
  These findings suggest that behavioral tactical representations provide transferable predictive signal under distribution shift and offer a viable alternative to identity-dependent football prediction systems.

</details>


### [149] [Meta-Learned Reward Shaping for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26094)
*Yunpeng Chu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Meta-learned reward shaping (MeRLa) adds a task-aware shaping function to RLHF, producing a composite reward that preserves policy optimality and enhances learning signals, yielding improved alignment and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, task-agnostic reward models in RLHF cause sparse signals and suboptimal alignment; a task-aware shaping function could provide richer feedback and more stable training with theoretical guarantees.

Method: Pre-RLHF meta-learning of a shaping function Φ(x,y;φ) across auxiliary tasks; meta-objective includes task discrimination, entropy regularization, and potential-based conservation; provides policy-invariance guarantees and analyzes drift and incentive misalignment.

Result: Empirical gains on LLaMA-3-8B across four benchmarks: outperforming PPO, DPO, GRPO, and DAPO; AlpacaEval 2.0 length-controlled win rate 90.8%; MT-Bench score 9.14; 41% reduction in training instability; benefits persist with process-based and rubric-based enhanced rewards.

Conclusion: MeRLa delivers stable, task-aware reward shaping for RLHF with theoretical backing and practical improvements in alignment, while preserving policy optimality and compatibility with existing reward enhancements.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard approach for aligning large language models with human preferences, but its quality is limited by static, task-agnostic reward models. This mismatch leads to sparse learning signals and suboptimal alignment. We introduce MeRLa (Meta-Learned Reward Shaping), a principled framework that meta-learns a task-aware shaping function $Φ(x,y;φ)$ across auxiliary tasks before RLHF training. The learned shaping produces a composite reward that preserves policy optimality while providing task-specific learning signals. Our meta-objective combines task discrimination, entropy regularization, and potential-based conservation for stable convergence. We provide theoretical guarantees for policy invariance, analyze representation drift sensitivity, and formally address incentive misalignment from entropy maximization. Experiments on LLaMA-3-8B across four benchmarks show consistent improvements over PPO, DPO, GRPO, and DAPO, achieving a 90.8% length-controlled win rate on AlpacaEval 2.0 and a score of 9.14 on MT-Bench, with 41% less training instability. MeRLa retains its benefits when combined with process-based and rubric-based enhanced rewards.

</details>


### [150] [Data Fusion and Contrastive Alignment for Unconstrained IR Molecular Structure Elucidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26164)
*Ethan J. Mick,Campbell A. Sweet,Matthias J. Young,Derek T. Anderson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformer with Mixture-of-Experts decoder and Choquet-integral non-additive aggregation improves unconstrained molecular structure elucidation from IR spectra, delivering >10 percentage-point Top-K gains over IR-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated structure elucidation from infrared data is hampered by reliance on pre-known formulas; enabling full molecule prediction expands applicability in analytical chemistry and reduces dependence on auxiliary inputs.

Method: Introduce a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoder with non-additive aggregation via linear-order statistics and the Choquet integral; adapt the transformer to apply non-additive operators for aggregating spectral representations; include an auxiliary contrastive alignment loss; analyze sub-structure fragments to validate chemical information capture.

Result: Top-K accuracy improves by more than 10 percentage points compared to baseline IR-only models; sub-structure fragment analysis corroborates that infrared spectra encode the majority of relevant chemical information; the work attributes isomer-ranking performance to coverage and spectral overlaps within the explored chemical space.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility and broadens AI utility in analytical chemistry by enabling automated, unconstrained molecular structure elucidation from measured IR spectra; the MoE and non-additive aggregation contribute to improved performance in navigating vast chemical space.

Abstract: Automated molecular structure elucidation from infrared (IR) spectroscopy data has seen significant advancements in recent years, but its broad applicability is limited by a reliance on pre-determined chemical formulas provided as auxiliary model inputs. This limits model predictions to isomer identification rather than full molecular structure prediction. Although transformer models have been shown to identify molecular isomers with high accuracy, their reliability for unconstrained structure elucidation is comparatively low and poorly understood. In this work, we propose and evaluate key modifications to the traditional encoder-decoder transformer. To better address the vast chemical space of the unconstrained problem, we implement a novel Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoder module that utilizes non-additive aggregation via linear-order statistics and the Choquet integral. We further modify the transformer to utilize these non-additive operators when aggregating spectral representations as well. Together with an auxiliary contrastive alignment loss term, these enhancements improve Top-K prediction accuracy by over 10 percentage points compared to baseline IR-only models. Through sub-structure fragment analysis of molecular predictions, we further confirm that infrared spectra encode the vast majority of relevant chemical information, implying that the higher performance of isomer-ranking models is largely due to underrepresented or overlapping absorption bands for molecules in the explored chemical space. Ultimately, by demonstrating the efficacy of automated molecular structure elucidation from measured IR spectra, this work serves to significantly broaden the utility of AI in analytical chemistry.

</details>


### [151] [Shared SFT Lessons Across Alignment, Model Organisms, and Toy Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26173)
*Anton de la Fuente,Arthur Conmy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cross-domain transfer of SFT lessons shows benefits and limits across alignment, toy models, and model organisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether techniques learned in one SFT setting transfer to others and uplift all fields.

Method: Three transfers: (1) apply 'teach the reason' to toy models; (2) study off-model outputs in midtraining with data mixing; (3) test robustness by follow-up benign SFT.

Result: 1) Reason-based training improves generalization; 2) benign on-model data mitigates capability loss from off-model outputs while embedding target behavior; 3) follow-up benign SFT can erase alignment behavior but preserve capabilities, indicating robustness is not guaranteed by capability preservation.

Conclusion: Porting SFT lessons between fields can uplift research; encourages cross-field borrowing of techniques; highlights nuanced interplay between alignment, capabilities, and robustness.

Abstract: Alignment training, model organisms, and toy models are usually treated as separate research areas. But projects in all three frequently use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to pursue the same underlying goals. When projects share a goal, we should test whether lessons learned from one area transfer to the other areas. We study three such transfers, each taking a lesson developed in one SFT setting and testing it in another. First, we port a lesson about behavior generalization from alignment training into toy models. Training on the reason for a behavior, as in Teaching Claude Why, can make the behavior generalize better than training on examples of the behavior alone. Second, we port a lesson about capability preservation from model organisms into the Model-Spec Midtraining alignment setting. SFT on outputs written by a model other than the student (off-model outputs) can damage capabilities when trained on. Mixing in benign on-model (and on-policy) data into our training can prevent most of this damage while still embedding the target behavior. Third, we port a lesson about robustness from model organisms into the same alignment setting. We find that follow-up benign SFT can erase the alignment behavior while preserving capabilities, showing that capability preservation alone does not ensure robustness to subsequent training. Our work illustrates how porting SFT lessons between different research fields can uplift them all, suggesting more researchers should borrow techniques from outside their own areas.

</details>


### [152] [Dynamic Parameterization Is Not Dynamic Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26192)
*Zongfei Li,Yuan-yih Shang,Guozhong Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FCA audits input-dependent coefficients by freezing and replaying with reassignment to separate coefficient variation, model dependence, and execution; findings show high layer-level variance and limited actual savings, urging separate reporting of these aspects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle dynamic inferences from mere coefficient variation and conditional execution, and to assess whether dynamic parameterization yields actual computational savings.

Method: Freeze the controller and cache the full coefficient tensor along an unperturbed trajectory. Disable the controller and replay the frozen model under cross-input reassignment, token shuffling, and static profiles estimated from a calibration set. The coefficients are fixed prior to interventions, so changes in performance reflect assignment dependence rather than recomputation on perturbed states.

Result: Static layerwise profiles recover 98.70% (76M models) and 99.43% (504M models) of the Correct-to-GlobalMean gap. Layer identity explains 87–96% of coefficient variance. FeatureGate runs through every Transformer block and is ~30.8% slower than Dense. In MUDDPythia-1.4B, cross-input reassignment and token shuffling raise NLL by 1.9067 and 2.9637, indicating strong content-conditioned cross-layer dependence. Overall, dynamic parameterization alone does not guarantee dynamic inference or computational savings.

Conclusion: Dynamic models require separate reporting of coefficient variation, the functional dependence of the frozen model, and actual execution. FCA provides a principled auditing framework to avoid conflating these aspects and to test whether dynamic coefficients translate into real efficiency.

Abstract: Input-dependent controller coefficients are often treated as evidence of dynamic inference or computational savings. This interpretation conflates three properties: coefficient variation, dependence of a frozen model on how coefficients are assigned to inputs, and conditional execution. We focus on the second property and formulate a general principle of frozen-controller auditing. We provide one concrete implementation, Frozen-Controller Auditing (FCA), which caches the complete coefficient tensor along an unperturbed trajectory, disables the controller, and replays the frozen model with cross-input reassignment, token shuffling, and static profiles estimated from an independent calibration set. Because the coefficients are cached before any intervention, performance changes under replay measure assignment dependence without feedback from recomputing the controller on perturbed hidden states. Across seven independently trained 76M FeatureGate Transformers and three 504M models, static layerwise profiles retain 98.70% and 99.43% of the Correct-to-GlobalMean performance gap, respectively. Layer identity explains 87% to 96% of the coefficient variance. FeatureGate nevertheless executes every Transformer block, and its measured inference is 30.8% slower than Dense. On the public MUDDPythia-1.4B checkpoint, cross-input reassignment and token shuffling increase NLL by 1.9067 and 2.9637, respectively. These penalties show that the model depends strongly on content-conditioned cross-layer assignment. MUDDPythia also executes every Transformer block. The results show that dynamic parameterization alone does not establish dynamic inference and that functional dynamics do not establish computational savings. Claims about dynamic models should separately report coefficient variation, functional dependence of the frozen model, and actual execution.

</details>


### [153] [Weak-to-Strong On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26246)
*Fangxu Yu,Zinan Lin,Xiaodong Liu,Weijia Xu,Michael Xu,Tianyi Zhou,Jianfeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: W2S-OPD uses weak models to build a proxy teacher in logit space for distillation, enabling a student to surpass its domain teacher and continue improving even with weak supervision across multiple contrast pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation of on-policy distillation requiring a teacher at least as capable as the student, by leveraging multiple weak models to provide directional capability signals that can enhance the student beyond the strongest available teacher.

Method: Form a proxy teacher by computing the logit difference between a positive and negative model (both weaker than the student) to capture a capability direction, add this difference to the student’s base to produce a proxy teacher, and perform per-token reverse KL distillation on the student’s own rollouts. Use three contrast pairs: post-RL expert vs pre-RL init, larger vs smaller base, and small base with correct vs wrong hints.

Result: Empirical evaluation on four math and three code benchmarks shows W2S-OPD outperforms standard OPD, enables the student to exceed the domain teacher, and continues to improve even when every supervision source is weaker. Different contrasts yield distinct signals: post-RL and hint contrasts emphasize reasoning frameworks, while the scale contrast emphasizes solving procedures.

Conclusion: W2S-OPD provides a simple, effective, and scalable enhancement to on-policy distillation by leveraging weak-to-strong signals through proxy teachers, enabling students to surpass domain teachers and benefit from weaker supervision; code will be released.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD), which aligns a student with the teacher's token-level distribution on the student's own rollouts, is an effective paradigm for transferring capabilities across LLMs. Prevailing approaches assume a teacher at least as capable as the student: they either distill a larger model into a smaller one, which fails at the frontier where no larger teacher exists, or consolidate multiple domain experts trained from a shared base, which requires costly training at the student's scale. We introduce Weak-to-Strong On-Policy Distillation (W2S-OPD), a simple yet effective OPD framework that improves the strong student by distilling from multiple weak models. W2S-OPD constructs a proxy teacher in logit space from a contrast pair of a positive and a negative model, both smaller than the student and cheap to obtain. Their logit difference isolates the capability direction, which is added to the student's own base model, yielding a proxy teacher that couples this direction while staying distributionally adjacent to the student. The student then distills it by minimizing the per-token reverse KL on its own rollouts. We instantiate the contrast pair as i) a post-RL expert against its pre-RL initialization, isolating the skill RL instills, ii) a larger against a smaller base model, isolating the capability from scale, and iii) a small base model with correct versus wrong hints, isolating the instance-level direction toward the solution. Across four math and three code benchmarks, W2S-OPD outperforms OPD, enables the student to surpass the domain teacher, and keeps improving the student even when every supervision source is weaker. Analysis shows different contrasts yield distinct signals: the post-RL and hint contrasts emphasize reasoning frameworks, while the scale contrast emphasizes the solving procedure. Our code will be available at https://github.com/Yu-Fangxu/W2S-OPD.

</details>


### [154] [Between Gradient and Natural Gradient: A Continuum of LoRA Initializations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26247)
*Dianze Liu,Farshid Ghezelbash*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ULoRA unifies LoRA initializations into a two-parameter preconditioning family for gradient-based adapter initialization, showing the optimal preconditioning is task-dependent and often interior to the spectrum; tuned ULoRA can match or exceed full fine-tuning on GLUE with RoBERTa-base and is competitive elsewhere, with ULoRA-Auto providing a deployable, search-free variant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resolve inconsistent and ad-hoc initialization strategies in LoRA by treating preconditioning as a continuum rather than fixed endpoints, enabling principled tuning to improve performance and deployability.

Method: Define a two-parameter preconditioning of the gradient used to initialize adapters: a spectral whitening exponent and an Adam-like diagonal exponent. Sweep this 2D space under full learning-rate search; evaluate on GLUE tasks with RoBERTa-base and GSM8K with LLaMA-2-7B. Introduce ULoRA-Auto that selects per-layer exponents from spectral statistics to approximate the best point without extra search.

Result: No single fixed preconditioning dominates; the best point is task-dependent and often interior to the space. A tuned ULoRA configuration matches or exceeds full fine-tuning on all five GLUE tasks (RoBERTa-base) and is competitive on GSM8K (LLaMA-2-7B). ULoRA-Auto approaches the upper bound with no additional search cost and ranks highly among deployable LoRA methods.

Conclusion: A principled, tunable design space for LoRA initialization and curvature preconditioning should be treated as a controllable dimension rather than a fixed choice; this leads to improved performance and deployability across tasks.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) fine-tunes large pretrained models at a fraction of the cost of full fine-tuning, but its performance depends strongly on how the adapters are initialized. Recent schemes initialize the adapters from the downstream loss gradient: some project the raw gradient onto its top directions, while others first whiten it with an estimate of the loss curvature. We show that these seemingly distinct methods are points on a single continuum: a two-parameter family of preconditioned gradient initializations, which we call Unified LoRA (ULoRA), governed by a spectral whitening exponent and an Adam-like diagonal exponent. Sweeping this family under a full learning-rate search, we find that no single fixed preconditioning strength dominates: the best operating point is task-dependent and frequently lies strictly inside the family, away from the published endpoints. Treated as an upper bound of this family, a tuned ULoRA configuration matches or exceeds full fine-tuning on all five GLUE tasks with RoBERTa-base and is competitive with the strongest baselines on GSM8K with LLaMA-2-7B. Our deployable, search-free variant, ULoRA-Auto, selects per-layer exponents from measured spectral statistics, approaches this upper bound at no additional search cost, and ranks at or near the top among deployable LoRA methods. Our results show that a principled design space for LoRA initialization and curvature preconditioning should be treated as a tunable dimension rather than a fixed design decision.

</details>


### [155] [Early Verdicts, Better Budgets: Sequential Adaptive Rollout Allocation for Compute-Efficient RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26253)
*Pixel Nomand,Elena Voss,Marcus Hale,Sofia Reyes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SARA (Sequential Adaptive Rollout Allocation) reframes RL with verifiable rewards as a budgeted, objective-at-a-time problem and uses a two-threshold SPRT-style mechanism with Beta-posteriors to quickly identify and allocate only effective prompts, abandoning saturated ones early to save rollouts while maintaining strong accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR suffers from many prompts producing saturated groups with zero gradient when rolled out; standard remedies waste rollout budget either through oversampling or fragile difficulty predictions. There is a need for a method that quickly discerns effective prompts and reallocates budget without extra prediction rollouts.

Method: Model the rollout process as a budget-constrained sequential allocation (optimal stopping) problem. Maintain a Beta posterior over each prompt's success rate, use a closed-form predictor of group effectiveness, and apply a two-threshold SPRT-style rule to commit effective groups, abandon saturated ones after a brief probe, and reallocate freed budget to new prompts. No additional prediction rollouts are required. The approach includes theoretical guarantees (abandonment reliability, rollout savings, fixed-budget yield dominance) and links effective-group yield to the GRPO gradient norm.

Result: Empirical and theoretical results show that SARA matches or exceeds the performance of existing baselines (DPS/DS oracle) with substantially fewer rollouts on large-scale models (1.5B/3B) on a single GPU. Using SARA alone yields savings of ~22% rollouts vs DS; combining SARA with DPS yields the best accuracy with ~67% fewer rollouts, approaching uniform-cost efficiency.

Conclusion: SARA provides a principled, efficient, and scalable solution for rollout allocation in RLVR, achieving competitive accuracy with significant rollout savings and strong theoretical guarantees; its modular design allows it to be combined with other methods (e.g., DPS) for even greater efficiency.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is bottlenecked by rollout generation, yet many sampled prompts produce saturated groups (all responses correct or all incorrect) whose zero reward variance yields no policy-gradient signal. Existing remedies either oversample a larger candidate pool and discard saturated prompts (dynamic sampling), paying heavy extra rollouts, or predict prompt difficulty before sampling, which is fragile under a shifting policy. We observe that a group's effectiveness is usually decided early, within the first few of its rollouts, so spending a full group on an already-decided prompt is wasteful. We cast per-step rollout collection as a budget-constrained sequential allocation (optimal stopping) problem and introduce SARA (Sequential Adaptive Rollout Allocation). SARA maintains a Beta posterior over each prompt's success rate, evaluates a closed-form predictor of group effectiveness, and applies a two-threshold, SPRT-style rule that commits effective groups, abandons saturated ones after a short probe, and reallocates the freed budget to fresh prompts, without any extra prediction rollouts. We prove abandonment reliability, expected rollout savings, fixed-budget yield dominance, and a link between effective-group yield and the GRPO gradient norm. On mathematical reasoning and planning with 1.5B/3B models on a single GPU, SARA matches DPS (both below the DS oracle) while using 22% fewer rollouts than DS; composing SARA with DPS yields the best accuracy, slightly above DS, at 67% fewer rollouts (near-uniform cost).

</details>


### [156] [Top-$k$ Pareto Bandits: Hypervolume Regret for Multi-Objective Slate Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26273)
*Nicolas Gutowski,Fabien Chhel,Alexandre Letard,Sylvain Lamprier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: THV-UCB for stochastic multi-objective bandits selects a size-k slate to approximate the Pareto frontier via dominated hypervolume; provides worst-case and gap-dependent regret bounds with an α-approximation of 1-1/e; demonstrates the feasibility of subset-based Pareto front tracking in semi-bandit feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many applications require a compact, representative subset of actions that jointly approximate the Pareto frontier, rather than a single optimal arm. Semi-bandit feedback on vector-valued rewards enables efficient estimation of marginal gains. The goal is to maintain a small action set that well-approximates the Pareto frontier with theoretical guarantees.

Method: Define the dominated hypervolume as the objective over a selected subset of arms. The algorithm THV-UCB greedily selects arms based on optimistic estimates of their marginal hypervolume contributions, leveraging the submodularity of the hypervolume to obtain a (1−1/e) approximation.
We derive a gap-free regret bound of Õ(d√(nkT)) and a gap-dependent bound of Õ(nk^{2.5}/Δ_min), with the latter becoming polylogarithmic in T when arms are well separated.

Result: The proposed algorithm achieves a universal (gap-free) regret bound and a problem-dependent bound, both polylogarithmic factors hidden, indicating that maintaining a small subset of arms suffices to approximate the Pareto frontier under semi-bandit feedback. The α=1−1/e guarantee arises from greedy maximization of the monotone submodular hypervolume.

Conclusion: This work provides a theoretically grounded framework for subset-based Pareto-front approximation in multi-objective bandits, with practical implications for applications requiring compact action sets. It invites further empirical validation and extensions to broader feedback models and non-submodular objectives.

Abstract: We consider a stochastic multi-objective bandit problem where, at each round, the agent selects a slate of $k$ arms and observes their $d$-dimensional reward vectors under semi-bandit feedback. We do not aim at identifying a single optimal arm; instead, we consider the problem of maintaining a small set of actions that jointly approximate the Pareto frontier. We formalize this objective through the dominated hypervolume induced by the selected subset of arms, and define an $α$-approximate hypervolume regret with respect to the best size-$k$ subset achievable in hindsight, where $α= 1 - 1/e$ reflects the approximation guarantee of greedy maximization for monotone submodular functions. To address this problem, we introduce \textit{THV-UCB}, an optimistic algorithm that selects arms greedily based on optimistic estimates of their marginal hypervolume contributions. We establish a gap-free regret bound $\tilde{O}(d\sqrt{nkT})$ that holds on every instance, together with a gap-dependent bound $\tilde{O}(nk^{2.5}/Δ_{\min})$ that becomes polylogarithmic in $T$ once the arms are sufficiently well separated. Our results provide theoretical support for using small subsets to approximate Pareto fronts in various multi-objective applications.

</details>


### [157] [FloDR: An invertible dimensionality reduction method based on a normalising flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26278)
*Abdallah Baraka,Daniel Probst*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FloDR leverages an invertible normalising flow to produce a 2D embedding while preserving all latent coordinates for exact inverse/density, enabling diagnostic visuals that quantify information retention (conditional spread) and label-contrast loss (hidden contrast) with bootstrap-based confidence tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard DR methods (e.g., t-SNE, UMAP) discard information during optimization, making it hard to interpret distances, gaps, and the meaning of empty spaces in the embedding. FloDR addresses interpretability by keeping the full latent representation accessible via an exact inverse and density.

Method: Train an invertible normalising flow that maps high-dimensional data to an informative 2D embedding; only the first two output coordinates form the 2D layout while the remaining coordinates are retained for diagnostics. The mapping provides an exact inverse and exact density. Two diagnostic fields are computed: (1) conditional spread (how much original data remains undetermined at each embedding point) and (2) hidden contrast (information about a labeled contrast that is discarded by the plotted 2D coordinates). Diagnostics are tested against held-out data with bootstrap confidence; failing fields are reported as refused.

Result: The paper presents FloDR and its diagnostic framework, enabling exact inverse and density-based visualisations. It introduces two fields (conditional spread and hidden contrast) to quantify information retention and information loss in annotations, with bootstrap-based significance testing to flag unreliable regions.

Conclusion: FloDR provides a principled, invertible DR approach that preserves full latent information for post-hoc diagnostics, offering interpretable visuals and reliability checks beyond traditional 2D embeddings.

Abstract: It is common for two-dimensional embeddings of high-dimensional data to be read far beyond what they can support. Distances in and between clusters, the meaning behind empty spaces, and the amount of structure hidden at each point are generally invisible in the output of methods such as t-SNE and UMAP. This is because the information that could support the meaning of these properties is discarded during the optimisation process. Here, we present FloDR, a dimensionality reduction method that embeds data through an invertible normalising flow. While FloDR only uses the first two output coordinates to create a two-dimensional embedding, it retains the remaining coordinates rather than discarding them. In addition to the embedding, an exact inverse and an exact density are properties of a trained mapping, which enable diagnostic visualisations that are computed from the exact inverse of the model that drew the layout rather than from an approximate one. Specifically, we draw two fields, the conditional spread, which measures how much of the original data remains undetermined at each embedding position in input units, and the hidden contrast, which measures how much information about a labelled contrast the two plotted coordinates discard. Both fields are rendered with a prespecified test against a held out portion of the input data and a bootstrap confidence. A field that fails the test is reported as refused.

</details>


### [158] [Entity Resolution in Practice: Lessons from a Self-Serve Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26298)
*Kaushik Pavani,Ganga Aluri,Pravin Jadhav,Neeraj Prasad,Kiran Sanka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Self-serve ER benefits from dataset-specific, diversified algorithm selection with strict cross-group verification; three lessons highlight non-universal accuracy, separate precision/recall fixes, and cascade safety concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical gaps in entity resolution practice: no single algorithm dominates across datasets, thresholds for precision/recall are insufficient, and false positives can cascade through chains of merges.

Method: Empirical evaluation of a self-serve ER pipeline on six benchmarks (from 864 to 5M records) to identify recurrent lessons not covered in prior ER literature; compare multiple algorithm families; implement a bake-off approach; assess precision/recall handling and cross-group merges.

Result: Three new lessons emerged: (1) no universal winner; use multiple algorithm families and auto bake-off per dataset; (2) precision and recall require separate remedies—hard rule-based vetoes for precision, diverse candidate retrieval for recall; (3) a single false-positive link can cascade through merges, so every cross-group merge must be re-verified.

Conclusion: Adopt dataset-aware pipelines with diverse algorithm portfolios and strict verification procedures to prevent cascading errors, reducing wasted experimentation in practice.

Abstract: We built and evaluated a self-serve entity resolution (ER) system on six benchmarks spanning 864 to 5M records, and three lessons emerged that are absent from existing ER literature. (1) No single matching algorithm wins everywhere - a self-serve pipeline cannot predict its next dataset, so we recommend training several algorithm families per dataset and letting an automatic bake-off pick the winner. (2) Precision and recall need separate fixes, not a shared threshold - precision needs hard rule-based vetoes, recall needs more diverse candidate retrieval. (3) One false-positive link can silently merge unrelated entities - assuming "A matches B" and "B matches C" implies "A matches C" lets a single bad link chain hundreds of records together, so every cross-group merge must be actively re-verified. We hope these lessons save practitioners the months of dead-end experiments that led us to them.

</details>


### [159] [Learning Implicit Causal World Models from Multi-Agent Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26336)
*Jasorsi Ghosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Implicit Causal World Models (ICWM) learn causal, interpretable environmental dynamics from offline demonstrations in multi-agent RL by using policy variance and a sequential backdoor criterion; they demonstrate robustness and interpretability across coordination tasks, with accuracy scaling with interventional strength.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models in model-based RL often conflate correlations with causal mechanisms, a problem amplified in multi-agent settings where agent intents affect dynamics and cause distribution shifts; there is a need for causal, interpretable world models that can be learned offline without predefined causal graphs.

Method: Propose Implicit Causal World Models that recover environmental dynamics from offline demonstrations without pre-defined causal graphs. Include policy variance to make world models discoverable via the sequential backdoor condition, enabling causal interpretation under both full and partial observability.

Result: Empirical evaluation on coordination tasks (Two-Door, Navigation, Giveway) shows that ICWMs yield interpretable causal representations under full/partial observability, with model accuracy increasing with interventional strength.

Conclusion: ICWM provides robust, interpretable causal representations for offline multi-agent RL without explicit causal graphs; leveraging policy variance and sequential backdoor conditions enables causal discovery and improved robustness to distribution shifts.

Abstract: In model-based reinforcement learning, world models exist as internal simulators, but their training often conflates statistical correlations with causal mechanisms. This problem is exacerbated in multi-agent systems where physical transitions are intertwined with strategic agent intents, causing world models to fail under distribution shift. We introduce Implicit Causal World Models to recover environmental dynamics from offline demonstrations without requiring pre-defined causal graphs. By incorporating policy variance, we render world models discoverable via the sequential backdoor condition. Evaluations across coordination tasks (Two-Door, Navigation, and Giveway) demonstrate that these models provide interpretable causal representations under both full and partial observability, with model accuracy scaling directly with interventional strength.

</details>


### [160] [RAGuard: A Layered Defense Framework for Retrieval-Augmented Generation Systems Against Data Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26339)
*Pushkal Kumar,Tucker Nielson,Tanish Kolhe,Shubham Zala,Vincent Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAGuard is a two-layer defense against factual corpus-poisoning in retrieval-augmented generation: (1) adversarial retriever fine-tuning to downrank poisoned passages; (2) ZKIP, a black-box, label-free filter using leave-one-out decoding to detect poisoning via semantic shift and output-entropy changes. It achieves near-zero attack success on poisoned Natural Questions across 5–30% poison and maintains high recall, with additional overhead of extra generator passes; keyword-preserving poisons can evade lexical retrievers like BM25, delineating threat boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To protect RAG pipelines from corpus poisoning that manipulates retrieved evidence, addressing both the vulnerability of retrievers to adversarial data and the lack of reliable, internal access for defenders to detect poisoned pulls.

Method:  (i) Train a dense retriever adversarially on synthetic poisoned documents to learn to downrank malicious passages; (ii) Apply ZKIP: for each retrieved doc, perform leave-one-out decode and measure semantic shift and output-entropy change when the doc is removed; comparisons are made without poison labels, ground-truth answers, or model internals; evaluates across poisoned Natural Questions and BEIR NFCorpus.

Result: Adversarial retriever training alone reduces attack success but does not eliminate it; ZKIP reduces attack success to 0.000 in all defended configurations while keeping Recall@5 within 0.03 of clean baseline. Counterfactual signals used by ZKIP are learnable for poison structure. Overhead: k+1 generator passes per query (6x for k=5); batching/early-stopping can reduce this. Keyword-preserving poisons do not affect lexical retrievers like BM25, clarifying the threat boundary.

Conclusion: A robust, two-layer defense is feasible for RAG systems against factual corpus-poisoning, combining proactive retriever hardening with a black-box counterfactual filter; releases code and datasets to support reproducibility and further study.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems ground large language models (LLMs) in external corpora, but this reliance exposes them to corpus poisoning: maliciously injected passages that manipulate retrieved evidence. We introduce RAGuard, a layered defense against \emph{factual} corpus-poisoning attacks on RAG pipelines. The first layer adversarially fine-tunes a dense retriever on synthetic poisoned documents (fabricated facts, contradictions, and reasoning traps), teaching it to downrank malicious passages before generation. The second layer, the Zero-Knowledge Inference Patch ZKIP, is a label-free, black-box filter: for each retrieved document, it performs a leave-one-out decode and scores the document by the semantic shift and output-entropy change that its removal induces. ZKIP requires no poison labels, no ground-truth answers, and no access to model internals; it compares the model's own answers under counterfactual contexts. On poisoned Natural Questions at 5--30\% poison ratios, adversarial retriever training alone reduces but does not eliminate attack success, while ZKIP drives the measured attack success rate to 0.000 in every defended configuration, keeping Recall@5 within 0.03 of the clean-corpus baseline. Supervised analyses on both Natural Questions and BEIR (NFCorpus) confirm that the counterfactual signals ZKIP relies on carry learnable poison structure. The defense costs $k{+}1$ generator passes per query ($6\times$ for $k{=}5$); we analyze batching and early-stopping approximations that reduce this overhead. We also show that keyword-preserving poisons leave lexical retrievers such as BM25 essentially unaffected, an observation that delineates the boundary of the threat model. Code, datasets, and evaluation harnesses are released for reproducibility.

</details>


### [161] [Automorphism-Induced Non-Canonicity in Top-k Explanations of Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26344)
*Xin Xu,Siru Tao,Kaizhen Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNN explainers are limited by permutation symmetry: when a graph has nontrivial automorphisms (common in molecular datasets like MUTAG), attribution scores cannot be both minimal, unique, and symmetry-respecting. The authors formalize a parameter-free, Lean 4 criterion to decide if every score-optimal exact-k explanation must split automorphism orbits, and validate it empirically across many instances, showing high prevalence of symmetry and that reporting orbits removes arbitrariness with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how permutation equivariance constraints affect attribution explanations in GNNs on molecular graphs, and to determine whether exact-k explanations can be unique and symmetry-respecting or must necessarily split automorphism orbits.

Method: Theoretical analysis of permutation equivariance and automorphism-induced obstructions; development of a parameter-free criterion (Lean 4 formalization, no axioms) that determines whether all score-optimal exact-k reports must split an orbit; empirical validation on 21298 instance-budget decisions; comparison with a mechanical model-equivalence check; analysis on MUTAG/Mutagenicity datasets; evaluation of the effect of orbit-aware reporting.

Result: Identified a structural obstruction: exact-k explanations cannot always be both minimal and symmetry-respecting in the presence of nontrivial automorphisms. The new criterion correctly predicts when reports must split orbits across all tested instances, matching the model-equivalence checks with no exceptions. Automorphisms are prevalent (93.4% of Mutagenicity graphs), and in cases with two interchangeable nitro groups, about 24% surface exactly one option, indicating arbitrariness is often unavoidable unless orbit reporting is used.

Conclusion: Symmetry imposes fundamental limits on explanation uniqueness in GNNs on molecular graphs. Orbit-aware reporting resolves arbitrariness with minimal overhead and should be adopted to avoid misleading attributions. The obstruction is widespread in chemistry datasets, calling for symmetry-conscious evaluation and reporting in explainability research.

Abstract: A gradient-based GNN explainer given a molecule with two chemically equivalent nitro groups assigns them attribution scores that are equal to the last bit. It cannot do otherwise: message passing is exactly permutation equivariant, so any automorphism of the input leaves every attribution invariant. Yet the standard report, the top-k edges, names one of the two, and which one is settled by the order of an array. We show this is a structural obstruction rather than an implementation slip. When no minimal valid explanation is fixed by the input's automorphism group, no rule can be single-valued, minimal and symmetry-respecting at once. For the exact-k reports used in practice we give a parameter-free criterion, mechanised in Lean 4 with no axiom dependencies, that decides from the graph alone whether every score-optimal report of that size must split an orbit. Across 21298 instance-budget decisions the criterion agrees with a mechanical model-equivalence check without exception, and no severing case we found admitted a neutral alternative. The obstruction is common. Nontrivial automorphisms occur in 93.4% of Mutagenicity, the dataset the seminal explainability papers use, so the measure-zero dismissal of symmetric inputs, sound on the continuous domains it was made for, collapses here. At the sparsity budget those papers report, 24.0% of molecules with two interchangeable nitro groups (6 of 25) surface exactly one of them, every one arbitrary under mechanical verification. A model's blindness also manufactures symmetry: every MUTAG molecule contains atoms chemistry separates and the network provably cannot, and a matched control shows the resolution is set by what the model reads rather than how it is parameterised. Reporting orbits removes the arbitrariness at 0.11 ms and 0.43 extra edges per graph.

</details>


### [162] [MetaKoopman: Bayesian Meta-Learning of Koopman Operators for Modeling Structured Dynamics under Distribution Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26345)
*Mahmoud Selim,Sriharsha Bhat,Karl H. Johansson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bayesian meta-learning for nonlinear dynamics via a MNIW-prior Koopman model, enabling closed-form updates and predictive uncertainty; evaluated on autonomous trucking under winter distribution shifts with superior accuracy and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need robust, uncertainty-aware modeling of nonlinear dynamics under distribution shifts in real-world systems such as autonomous vehicles.

Method: MetaKoopman uses a linear latent Koopman representation with a Matrix Normal-Inverse Wishart prior over the Koopman operator; provides closed-form Bayesian updates conditioned on recent trajectory segments and a closed-form posterior predictive distribution capturing epistemic and aleatoric uncertainty.

Result: Outperforms prior approaches in multi-step prediction accuracy, uncertainty calibration, and robustness to distributional shifts; field experiments show effectiveness in dynamically feasible motion planning, evasive maneuvers, and operation at traction limits.

Conclusion: A principled, uncertainty-aware dynamics modeling framework robust to distribution shifts, with demonstrated practical viability for autonomous driving tasks; project website provided.

Abstract: Modeling and forecasting nonlinear dynamics under distribution shifts is essential for robust decision-making in real-world systems. In this work, we propose MetaKoopman, a Bayesian meta-learning framework for modeling nonlinear dynamics through linear latent representations. MetaKoopman learns a Matrix Normal-Inverse Wishart (MNIW) prior over the Koopman operator, enabling closed-form Bayesian updates conditioned on recent trajectory segments. Moreover, it provides a closed-form posterior predictive distribution over future state trajectories, capturing both epistemic and aleatoric uncertainty in the learned dynamics. We evaluate MetaKoopman on a full-scale autonomous truck and trailer system across a wide range of adverse winter scenarios, including snow, ice, and mixed-friction conditions, as well as in simulated control tasks with diverse distribution shifts. MetaKoopman consistently outperforms prior approaches in multi-step prediction accuracy, uncertainty calibration, and robustness to distributional shifts. Field experiments further demonstrate its effectiveness in dynamically feasible motion planning, particularly during evasive maneuvers and operation at the limits of traction. Project website: https://mahmoud-selim.github.io/MetaKoopman/

</details>


### [163] [High-Order Markov Blanket Discovery via a k-Order Relaxation of the Faithfulness Assumption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26357)
*Loong Kuan Lee,Ragavi Krishnamoorthy,Nico Piatkowski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a k-order relaxation of faithfulness to capture parity-type dependencies and presents k-order Markov blanket (kOMB) for MB discovery, with empirical validation and open-source implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Faithfulness can be violated by higher-order dependencies (e.g., XOR, parity) and finite-sample noise, complicating MB discovery in graphical models.

Method: Defines a k-order faithfulness relaxation that captures parity-type relations among k+2 variables and develops a proof-of-concept algorithm (kOMB) that uses this relaxation for MB discovery.

Result: Empirical results show that kOMB can recover the Markov blanket of a variable under both true and empirically induced faithfulness violations.

Conclusion: kOMB provides a concept-proof approach to MB discovery under faithfulness violations, with code available at the provided GitHub link.

Abstract: The problem of learning the graphical Markov blanket (MB) of a variable from data has applications in many areas such as structure learning for Bayesian networks and Markov random fields, causal discovery, and feature selection. However, a common assumption most methods make is that the conditional independencies in the distribution imply the same separation in the graphical structure -- also known as the faithfulness assumption. Unfortunately, this assumption can be violated by higher-order dependencies such as XOR and parity-type relations, and -- on finite samples -- by empirical violations that, in extreme cases, even induce spurious dependencies absent from the true distribution. Therefore, in this paper we propose a "k-order" relaxation of the faithfulness assumption that captures parity type relationships between k+2 variables. We then propose a proof of concept algorithm called k-order Markov blanket (kOMB) that uses this relaxation for MB discovery. Finally, we empirically show how kOMB can recover the MB of a variable under both true and empirical violations of faithfulness. Code available at: https://github.com/lklee9/k-order-Markov-blanket

</details>


### [164] [Post-Training at the Edge of Detectability: A Game-Theoretic Approach to Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26358)
*Keegan Harris,Brian W. Lee,Ian Waudby-Smith,Philip Amortila,Nika Haghtalab,Michael I. Jordan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a principled KL-regularized RL fine-tuning coefficient via a game-theoretic framework, solving for an equilibrium that equals KL-RL with an optimal coefficient tied to reward per statistical distinguishability; provides a practical method to learn this coefficient and validates on large LMs for continual learning and API auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KL-regularized RL relies on an unknown regularization weight; heuristic tuning is costly and may lead to poor reward retention. A principled, interpretable method to set this coefficient is desirable, enabling better trade-offs and auditability in model fine-tuning.

Method: Model a sequential game: an agent selects a policy to maximize cumulative reward while a monitor observes outputs and tests deviation from a reference policy. The equilibrium policy aligns with a KL-regularized RL objective with an optimally chosen coefficient. This optimal coefficient is derived using concave-convex fractional programming by reducing to a KL-regularized RL problem, enabling efficient estimation within standard fine-tuning pipelines.

Result: Empirical results on Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.2-1B show competitive reward-retention trade-offs in continual learning. The framework also demonstrates potential for auditing and constraining API provider outputs for open-source models.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled, data-driven means to set the KL regularization strength in RL fine-tuning, associating it with reward efficiency relative to statistical distinguishability. It integrates into existing pipelines and supports auditing capabilities for model deployments.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning is widely used in language model training to improve model performance on a target task while limiting drift from a reference policy. A standard way to balance this trade-off is via a KL-regularized RL objective, although this formulation does not by itself provide a principled way to set the regularization coefficient. In practice, the coefficient is typically chosen heuristically or via hyperparameter search, which can lead to unnecessary overhead in training cost or undesirable reward-retention trade-offs. We instead propose a game-theoretic framework that gives this trade-off an explicit statistical interpretation. Specifically, we study a sequential game in which an agent chooses a policy to maximize cumulative reward while a monitor observes policy outputs over time and tests for deviations from the reference policy. Although not originating from the same perspective, we show that the resulting equilibrium policy can nonetheless be expressed as the solution to a KL-regularized RL problem for an optimal regularization parameter that can be viewed as maximizing reward per unit of statistical distinguishability. Drawing on classical results from concave-convex fractional programming, we provide a principled method for learning this equilibrium coefficient via reduction to the KL-regularized RL objective, thus allowing for flexible integration into standard fine-tuning pipelines. In experiments with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.2-1B, we demonstrate that our methods result in competitive reward-retention trade-offs in a continual learning setting, and illustrate how our framework may be used to audit API providers serving open-source models.

</details>


### [165] [ClockRoPE: Random Fourier Rotations for Temporal Routine Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26369)
*Yiwen Chen,Joshua Ainslie,Krzysztof Choromanski,Xiang Gao,Su-Lin Wu,Yiping Yuan,Qian Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Theory of Random Fourier Rotations (RFR) showing that any normalized continuous PD attention modulation can be approximated by random rotations from its Fourier transform; ClockRoPE applies this to sequential modeling by deriving rotation frequencies from periodic attention modulation functions; shows online engagement gains and production deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RoPE's fixed log-linear frequency schedule struggles to capture complex distance-correlation patterns (e.g., temporal periodicity) in domains like sequential recommendation; a more expressive rotation mechanism is desired.

Method: Proves that normalized continuous positive-definite attention modulation functions can be approximated by random rotations induced by their Fourier transforms (Random Fourier Rotations). Proposes ClockRoPE, where rotation frequencies are derived from periodic attention modulation functions to model periodic patterns in sequences.

Result: Online A/B tests show consistent improvements in valued engagement metrics. ClockRoPE has been deployed in a production-scale generative retrieval system at a major video-sharing platform.

Conclusion: ClockRoPE offers a principled, expressive alternative to RoPE for sequential recommendation by harnessing Random Fourier Rotations to capture periodic and other complex distance-correlation patterns, with demonstrated real-world impact and deployability.

Abstract: Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) has been widely adopted in transformer-based large language models. However, its log-linear frequency schedule, originally designed to produce long-term attention decay, limits its adoption in domains with more complex distance-correlation patterns, such as temporal periodicity in sequential recommendation. We investigate the expressiveness of general query/key rotations and find that any normalized continuous positive-definite attention modulation function can be approximated by random rotations induced by its own Fourier transform, which we term Random Fourier Rotations. Building on this theory, we propose ClockRoPE for routine modeling in sequential recommendation, where rotation frequencies are derived from periodic attention modulation functions. In online A/B tests, ClockRoPE demonstrates consistent improvements in valued engagement metrics, and has been successfully deployed in production-scale generative retrieval system at a major video-sharing platform.

</details>


### [166] [Q-Steer: Action-Value Guidance for Molecular Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26391)
*Xinyu Wang,Jinbo Bi,Minghu Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Q-Steer is a rollout-time action-value steering primitive for molecular language models that uses a frozen prefix-action value scorer (PAVS-Q) to bias next-token sampling, improving mean valid-unique scores under fixed online oracle budgets across backbones and optimizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delays in feedback when optimizing molecules (oracle-limited setting) make policy updates myopic; intermediate actions lack explicit credit assignment, hindering learning of which decisions produce good molecules.

Method: Introduce Q-Steer: a rollout-time wrapper that uses a offline-trained, frozen PAVS-Q to estimate downstream reward for a candidate next token given a partial SMILES prefix. It adds a normalized value bonus to the sampling logits, without changing the online oracle budget or update rule. Evaluation on PMO23 with 10k online calls across two molecular LM backbones and four optimizers in a full factorial design.

Result: Q-Steer yields consistent improvements in mean valid-unique score across all eight backbone-optimizer combinations, with macro mean-score gains between +0.033 and +0.049 and 18–20 task wins per cell. Ablations show action identity matters: prefix-broadcast values are nearly neutral, while shuffled values degrade performance.

Conclusion: Q-Steer functions as a reusable rollout-time action-value wrapper that improves average molecular optimization reward across optimizer families and policy backbones without increasing the online oracle budget.

Abstract: Oracle-limited molecular optimization gives reward only after a complete molecule is generated, while each rollout requires many local next-token decisions. This delayed-feedback interface makes molecular policy optimization myopic: an optimizer can learn that a molecule was good without knowing which intermediate actions made it good. We introduce Q-Steer, a rollout-time action-value steering primitive for molecular language models. Q-Steer uses an offline-trained and frozen prefix-action value scorer, PAVS-Q, that estimates the downstream reward of taking a candidate next token under a partial SMILES prefix, then adds a normalized value bonus to sampling logits. The optimizer update rule and online oracle budget are unchanged; the claim is fixed-online-oracle performance, not equal total compute. On PMO23 with a fixed 10,000-call online budget, complete factorial studies across two molecular language-model backbones and four optimizers show that Q-Steer improves mean valid-unique score in all eight backbone-optimizer cells, with positive macro mean-score gains between +0.033 and +0.049 and 18-20 task wins per cell. Mechanism controls show that action identity matters: prefix-broadcast values are nearly neutral, while shuffled action values harm performance. These results support Q-Steer as a reusable rollout-time action-value wrapper that improves average molecular optimization reward across optimizer families and policy backbones without changing the online oracle budget.

</details>


### [167] [Flow Map Learning via Nongradient Vector Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26398)
*Mark Goldstein,Anshuk Uppal,Raghav Singhal,Aahlad Puli,Rajesh Ranganath*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SGFlow learns flow maps for diffusion/consistency models by optimizing non-conservative dynamics with a stationary point at the target flow, removing invertibility constraints and expensive differentiation through iterative calls, yielding strong CIFAR results across step counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference in diffusion/flow-based models is expensive due to sampling, and existing consistency methods often require model inverses or backpropagation through iterations and lack a provable guarantee that the learned flow solves the intended ODE loss.

Method: Train a model to compute both the ODE solution and the implied velocity from scratch by following non-conservative dynamics with a stationary point at the desired flow map, avoiding explicit invertibility constraints and backprop through iterative calls (stopgrad-based).

Result: On CIFAR, SGFlow achieves the best FID at 10 sampling steps and remains competitive at other step counts with flow matching, Meanflow, and Lagrangian map matching; it is the only method with a proven stationary-point guarantee for its stopgrad-based dynamics.

Conclusion: SGFlow offers a practical, provably grounded approach to learning flow maps for diffusion models that reduces computational overhead while delivering strong, step-robust performance.

Abstract: Diffusion and flow-based models benefit from simple regression losses, but inference incurs significant overhead because sampling requires integration. Consistency models address this by directly learning the flow maps along the ODE trajectory, opening a design space between one-step and many-step approaches. However, existing methods face computational challenges such as requiring model inverses or backpropagation through iterated model calls, and do not always prove that the desired ODE flow map is a solution to the loss. We introduce SGFlow, an approach for learning flow maps that bypasses explicit invertibility constraints and expensive differentiation through model iteration. SGFlow trains a model to compute both the ODE solutions and the implied velocity from scratch by following non-conservative dynamics with a stationary point at the desired flow map. On the CIFAR image benchmark, no single method attains the best FID at every step count: SGFlow attains the best FID at 10 sampling steps and remains competitive with flow matching, Meanflow, and Lagrangian map matching at other step counts, while being the only one with a proven stationary-point guarantee for its stopgrad-based dynamics.

</details>


### [168] [Examining the Efficacy of Graph Neural Network Message-Passing in Regression Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26404)
*Keith G. Mills,Aedan J. DeFrates,Joong Ho Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNNs for regression are evaluated; deep convolutional GEN outperforms attention-based GNNs; classical GNNs remain competitive; advocates broader benchmarking beyond classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the tendency to evaluate GNNs mainly on classification tasks and explore their effectiveness in regression contexts such as rank ordering, error minimization, and insight extraction.

Method: Empirical study across multiple regression tasks comparing deep convolutional GEN, attention-based GNNs, and classical theoretically-inspired GNNs.

Result: Deep convolutional GNNs (GEN) excel at regression tasks, outperforming attention-based GNNs; classical theoretically-inspired GNNs remain competitive and efficient.

Conclusion: Broaden GNN evaluation to regression problems to avoid classification bias; GEN shows strong performance; practitioners should select GNN architectures based on task type, not just classification performance.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNN) facilitate effective prediction on graph data such as molecules, media networks and neural network blueprints. GNNs facilitate prediction through message passing techniques which define how information flows from a node to its neighbors. Due to the ubiquity of the graph data type, the development of newer and better GNNs has garnered much interest in the machine learning community.
  However, GNN evaluation and benchmarking is primarily driven by classification tasks. Thus, prospective GNN message passing layers are evaluated on their ability to outperform prior work in classification contexts. In contrast, GNNs are equally capable of performing scalar regression prediction, yet this class of problem is often overlooked when proposing new GNNs while the best classification GNNs are utilized in an a priori or off-the-shelf manner for regression problems. In response, this paper studies the efficacy of GNN layers in a slew of regression contexts from rank ordering, error minimization and insight extraction. Results show that deep convolutional GNNs, particularly GEN, are more effective at these tasks than attention-based GNNs, while other classical, theoretically-inspired GNNs remain competitive and efficient.

</details>


### [169] [SCOUT: Per-Context Reset Curricula for Sparse-Reward Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26417)
*Siddharth Aphale,Ayushman Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Online, context-specific reset controller SCOUT enables per-context scaffold pacing in sparse-reward RL, addressing pacing conflicts and outperforming global schedules without needing group labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global scaffold pacing can fail when contexts learn at different rates; a per-context, online curriculum can adaptively remove and reinstate scaffolds to match progress.

Method: SCOUT is an online, learner-agnostic reset controller that uses only binary rollout success to manage scaffolds: remove after sustained success, restore after failure, and cautiously test harder starts when progress stalls; it does not modify reward, optimizer, or learner. It includes a counting construction to show global pacing can be insufficient when contexts conflict in learning pace, and it evaluates across six navigation and manipulation tasks.

Result: Scaffold access improves learning and enables success in tasks where unassisted training fails within budget; SCOUT resolves pacing conflicts that global schedules fail to solve; reporting least successful group reveals failures masked by average metrics; SCOUT requires no group labels and remains robust whether group structure is known or not.

Conclusion: Reset curricula should operate at the scale of per-context learning progress; SCOUT provides such per-context pacing, improving robustness and success without extra labeling or changes to the learner.

Abstract: Sparse-reward reinforcement learning often fails because rollouts from the unassisted evaluation start rarely reach later task stages. Reset curricula address this by starting some training rollouts from easier intermediate states, called scaffolds. Such a curriculum faces two decisions: scaffold access, obtaining informative starts, and scaffold allocation, deciding how quickly that assistance is removed. Most prior curricula pace removal on one shared schedule, which can fail when task instances, or contexts, learn at different rates. We introduce SCOUT, an online, learner-agnostic reset controller that gives every context its own curriculum. Using only binary rollout success, SCOUT removes assistance after sustained success, restores it after failure, and cautiously tests a harder start when progress stalls, without changing the reward, optimizer, or learner. A counting construction shows that synchronized global pacing can be insufficient when contexts need conflicting amounts of assisted practice. Across six navigation and manipulation settings, scaffold access improves learning and enables success in three where unassisted training fails within the reported budget. In a constructed pacing conflict, each tested global schedule leaves one group unsolved, while SCOUT solves both. Average success can conceal this failure, so we also report the least successful group. Group-level pacing works when learning differences follow known groups but can fail when they occur within one group. SCOUT needs no group labels and remains consistently strong in both cases. A reset curriculum should remove assistance at the scale where learning progress differs.

</details>


### [170] [Existence-Field Diffusion Model for Spatial Point Processes with Variable Cardinality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26428)
*Xiaoyin Pan,Christian R. Shelton,Rakshith Mahishi,Chengkuan Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces existence-field diffusion model (EFDM) for spatial point processes, enabling a unified diffusion over locations and cardinality via per-point existence variables, avoiding discrete transitions and improving flexible modeling of variable-cardinality data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion approaches for spatial point processes struggle with variable cardinality due to decoupled modeling or reliance on discrete trans-dimensional operations, leading to inflexible generative dynamics.

Method: Associate each potential point with an existence variable representing its degree of presence; employ a unified diffusion process that jointly evolves spatial locations and existence degrees, eliminating the need for explicit discrete cardinality changes.

Result: EFDM provides a flexible, general framework for generating spatial point processes with varying cardinality and demonstrates improved modeling capability over baselines on pertinent datasets.

Conclusion: EFDM offers a flexible, general, and scalable approach to generative modeling of spatial point processes by integrating cardinality and spatial structure into a single diffusion process.

Abstract: We study generative modeling of spatial point processes (SPP), where both the number of points and their spatial configuration are governed by a joint distribution. While diffusion models have achieved strong performance in modeling complex distributions, extending them to variable-cardinality SPP remains challenging. Existing approaches either decouple the modeling of cardinality and spatial structure, or rely on discrete trans-dimensional operations to modify the number of points, resulting in inflexible and asymmetric generative dynamics. We propose the existence-field diffusion model (EFDM) for spatial point processes modeling, where each potential point is associated with an existence variable representing its degree of presence. This enables a unified diffusion process that jointly models both spatial locations and cardinality without requiring explicit discrete transitions. We demonstrate that our approach provides a flexible and general framework for generative modeling of spatial point processes, achieving improved modeling capability on datasets with varying cardinality.

</details>


### [171] [DHRCL:Training Code LLMs with Dense Hierarchical Rewards and Curriculum Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26457)
*Shuhang Wang,Ziming Li,Hui Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dense hierarchical RL framework, DHRCL, for code LLMs with staged curricula and token credit redistribution; shows consistent gains across Qwen backbones and KodCode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL methods for code generation rely on sparse or mixed signals; a unified, hierarchical dense-reward framework is needed to align optimization with progressively complex programming capabilities.

Method: Decomposes feedback into syntax validation, execution, unit tests, and AST structural similarity; employs a three-stage Syntax, Execution, Pass & Structural curriculum with stage durations determined automatically from validation trends; introduces stage-aware token credit redistribution; evaluated under a unified Qwen3-8B and KodCode protocol across Qwen3-4B, 3-8B, and 3-14B backbones.

Result: DHRCL outperforms binary, pass-rate, reward-model-based, and verifiable dense-reward baselines; its advantage persists and scales with model capacity across Qwen3-4B/8B/14B.

Conclusion: Hierarchical, dense reward signals combined with curriculum learning improve RL-based code generation, with adaptive credit assignment enhancing optimization at different stages of program proficiency and robustness across model sizes.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is a natural post-training paradigm for code-oriented large language models because generated programs can be evaluated through parsing, execution, unit tests, and structural analysis.However, existing methods often rely on sparse outcome rewards or statically combine heterogeneous dense signals, even though syntax validity, executability, functional correctness, and structural organization describe different and progressively dependent programming capabilities. We propose DHRCL, a reinforcement learning framework with Dense Hierarchical Rewards and Curriculum Learning. DHRCL decomposes feedback into syntax validation, execution success, unit-test pass rate, and AST-based structural similarity, and organizes these signals through a three-stage Syntax, Execution, Pass & Structural curriculum. Stage duration is determined automatically from recent validation trends rather than manually specified capability thresholds. We further introduce stage-aware probability-based token credit redistribution.
