The mechanism follows a consolidation-to-refinement principle: it emphasizes established token patterns during syntax-oriented optimization, applies uniform propagation for non-local execution feedback, and allocates more credit or blame to less-established token decisions during final functional optimization. Under a unified Qwen3-8B and KodCode protocol, the experiments compare DHRCL with binary, pass-rate, reward-model-based, and verifiable dense-reward baselines. We further evaluate DHRCL across Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and Qwen3-14B backbones, showing that its advantage remains consistent as model capacity increases.

</details>


### [172] [Neural Architecture Search for Traffic Prediction: A Survey of Methods, Challenges, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26467)
*Truong Giang Vu,Li Yang,Richard W. Pazzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey of NAS methods for traffic prediction, organized by search strategy; analyzes search space design and cost-quality trade-offs; discusses open challenges and future directions including cross-city generalization and spatial-temporal foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual neural architectures for traffic forecasting are labor-intensive and struggle to generalize across cities; NAS offers automated design aligned with traffic data's spatial-temporal structure.

Method: Systematic review categorizing NAS approaches into gradient-based, evolutionary, and one-shot weight-sharing; analyzes search space design, strategies, scalability, and discusses open problems and future directions.

Result: Synthesizes existing NAS applications to traffic prediction; highlights trade-offs, strengths/limitations of each search paradigm; identifies gaps and challenges such as scalability, generalization, dynamic graphs, and lack of NAS for spatial-temporal foundation models.

Conclusion: NAS is promising for traffic prediction but requires advances in scalable search, robust transferability across cities, handling dynamic graphs, and exploring NAS-enabled spatial-temporal foundation models; future work should address these directions.

Abstract: Traffic prediction is a core task in intelligent transportation systems, supporting applications such as adaptive signal control, route guidance, and ride-hailing dispatch. Deep learning models, including graph convolutional networks, recurrent networks, and Transformers, achieve strong results on standard benchmarks, but their architectures are designed by hand, requiring significant expert effort and producing models that often generalize poorly across cities and datasets. Neural Architecture Search (NAS) offers a systematic alternative to manual design. It automates the search over candidate architectures of deep learning models, finding designs that match the spatial-temporal structure of traffic data without manual trial and error. This survey reviews NAS methods applied to traffic prediction, organized by search strategy: gradient-based methods, evolutionary methods, and one-shot weight-sharing methods. For each category, we analyze how the search space is designed to cover spatial and temporal traffic operators, and how the search strategy balances cost against architecture quality. We also discuss open challenges, computational scalability to large road networks, manual search space design, cross-city generalization, dynamic graph structure, and the open question of NAS for spatial-temporal foundation models, and identify directions for future research.

</details>


### [173] [Learning Dynamic User Personas from Implicit Interaction Streams via Iterative Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26473)
*Haifeng Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IRIS learns dynamic user personas from implicit interaction streams to personalize LLMs without explicit feedback, validated on synthetic and Reddit data with strong decision-prediction performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit preference supervision (e.g., pairwise comparisons, demographic attributes) limits real-world personalization; need scalable methods that operate on natural interaction data.

Method: IRIS extracts behavioral signals from everyday conversations to form dynamic persona representations and uses a prediction-driven closed loop to iteratively refine these personas without explicit feedback. Evaluation protocols assess behavior prediction, persona stability, and decision prediction. A synthetic study using autobiographical text tests stability and recall. Real-world anonymized Reddit AITA data (100 authors) tests personalization performance against baselines.

Result: On synthetic data, IRIS yields stable, distinguishable personas and reveals limitations of memory-only recall metrics. On Reddit AITA data, IRIS achieves the highest decision-prediction accuracy among evaluated methods (61.0%), outperforming static personas, memory-only retrieval, and no-personalization baselines.

Conclusion: Implicit behavioral modeling offers a scalable alternative to explicit preference learning for personalized LLMs and supports adaptive conversational systems and embodied agents requiring continuously evolving user models.

Abstract: Personalizing large language models (LLMs) to individual users is essential for improving user experience, yet existing approaches typically rely on explicit preference supervision such as pairwise comparisons or demographic attributes, limiting their applicability in natural interaction settings. We propose IRIS, a framework that learns dynamic user personas directly from implicit interaction streams by extracting behavioral signals from everyday conversations and iteratively refining persona representations through a prediction-driven closed loop without requiring explicit feedback. We introduce an evaluation protocol based on behavior prediction, persona stability, and decision prediction. A proof-of-concept study on a synthetic interaction stream derived from public-domain autobiographical text shows that IRIS produces stable personas and distinguishes individual users while revealing limitations of memory-only approaches on recall-oriented metrics. We then validate IRIS on anonymized real-world Reddit r/AmItheAsshole (AITA) data, with personas built solely from each author's historical interactions. Across 100 authors, IRIS achieves the highest decision prediction accuracy among all evaluated methods (61.0%), outperforming static personas, memory-only retrieval, and no-personalization baselines. These results suggest that implicit behavioral modeling provides a scalable alternative to explicit preference learning for personalized LLMs and offers a practical foundation for adaptive conversational systems and embodied agents that require continuously evolving models of their users.

</details>


### [174] [Conformal Changepoint Localization and Root Cause Analysis with Corrupted Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26481)
*Seunghun Yu,Meiyi Zhu,Petar Popovski,Joonhyuk Kang,Osvaldo Simeone*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weighted conformal changepoint localization and root-cause analysis (W-CONCH/W-CROC) robust to data contamination by downweighting corrupted observations; combines huber-type contamination, uncertainty signals from evidential/Bayesian learning, and meta-learning to minimize confidence-set size while preserving coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Guarantee finite-sample coverage for changepoint localization and root-cause analysis in safety-critical systems under data contamination (outliers, sensor faults, adversarial perturbations). Standard conformal methods produce large, uninformative sets under contamination.

Method: Introduce W-CONCH and W-CROC with a Hub er-type contamination model. Derive observation weights from bounds on corrupted densities; leverage second-order classifier uncertainty signals (e.g., evidential deep learning, Bayesian methods). Add a meta-learning stage to optimize weights with a differentiable surrogate for confidence-set size, improving informativeness while preserving coverage.

Result: Empirical evaluation on image-based and real-world changepoint/root-cause benchmarks shows substantial reductions in confidence-set size due to uncertainty-based weighting, with maintained target coverage under contamination.

Conclusion: Weighted, uncertainty-aware conformal methods (W-CONCH/W-CROC) enhance informativeness of confidence sets in contaminated settings and can be further improved via meta-learning for weight optimization; applicable to safety-critical monitoring domains.

Abstract: Detecting when the statistical behavior of an engineered system changes, and identifying which component is responsible, are core problems in the monitoring of telecommunication networks, robotic platforms, security infrastructure, and multi-agent systems. In safety- and mission-critical deployments, such decisions must be accompanied by statistical reliability guarantees rather than by point estimates alone. Conformal changepoint localization (CONCH) and conformal root cause analysis (CROC) meet this need by returning confidence sets that contain the true changepoint, or the true root-cause stream, with a user-specified probability, without parametric assumptions on the data-generating process. In practice, however, observations are frequently corrupted, e.g., by outliers, sensor faults, or adversarial perturbations. While the finite-sample coverage of these procedures is preserved under contamination, the resulting confidence sets can become uninformatively large. Adopting a Huber-type contamination model, this paper proposes weighted CONCH (W-CONCH) and weighted CROC (W-CROC), which downweight observations that are likely to be corrupted with the goal of reducing confidence set size when data may be corrupted. The weighting mechanism, derived from a formal bound on the unknown corrupted data densities, leverages pre-existing second-order classifier-based uncertainty signals, such as those produced by evidential deep learning or Bayesian learning. W-CONCH and W-CROC are further generalized by introducing a meta-learning procedure for the weights that optimizes a differentiable surrogate of the confidence set size. Experiments on image-based and real-world changepoint and root-cause benchmarks show that uncertainty-based weighting substantially reduces confidence set size while maintaining the target coverage.

</details>


### [175] [From Conceptual Hydrologic Models to Conceptually Interpretable Neural Networks: A Snow-Water Mass-Conserving-Perceptron Framework for Discovering Catchment-Scale Precipitation-Storage-Runoff Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26492)
*Yuan-Heng Wang,Hoshin V. Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mass-conserving perceptron networks for hydrology tested across 513 CAMELS-US basins; 1–5 state MCP units; two-state yields large gains, diminishing returns beyond two; five-state reaches KGE_ss ~0.90; MCP competitive with LSTM and more parameter-efficient; AIC/KGE selection reveals compact, basin-specific directed graphs; supports state-number and interaction design in MCP-based hydrologic representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embed physical mass conservation into neural-network hydrologic representations to achieve interpretable, physically consistent models that match or surpass traditional ML approaches while enabling compact, basin-specific architectures.

Method: Recast coupled two-state SOIL-MCP and SNOWMCP as mass-conserving neural networks; explore cross-node sharing in HYDROMCP; build single-layer MCP networks with 1–5 states; evaluate across 513 basins (CONUS); compare against LSTM; use AIC and KGE-based selection to derive basin-specific directed graphs and assess model complexity.

Result: Hydrologic-model and neural-network forms achieve comparable predictive performance. KGE_ss increases from 0.82 (1-state) to 0.89 (2-state) to 0.90 (5-state) across CONUS, indicating diminishing gains beyond two states. Basin-specific MCP and LSTM yield median KGE_ss of 0.90, with MCP using fewer parameters. AIC- and KGE-based selection identifies compact directed-graph representations balancing accuracy and complexity. These findings provide empirical guidance on the numbers, types, and interactions of states needed; future work suggests joint training across multiple hydrologic responses.

Conclusion: The results offer an empirical basis for choosing the number and types of states in MCP-based hydrologic representations and highlight the potential for compact, interpretable network structures that are physically constrained; advocate future joint-training across responses (e.g., streamflow, SWE, groundwater) to further enhance multi-response modeling.

Abstract: The Mass-Conserving Perceptron (MCP) establishes a modeling paradigm in which conceptual hydrologic models can be reformulated as physically constrained, conceptually interpretable neural networks. Here, we develop a snow-water MCP network framework and evaluate it across 513 CAMELS-US basins. We first recast a coupled two-state SOIL-MCP and SNOWMCP conceptual model as a mass-conserving neural network and show that the hydrologic-model and neural-network formulations achieve comparable predictive performance. We then examine cross-node state-information sharing within two-state HYDROMCP architectures and evaluate broader single-layer networks constructed from three types of interpretable MCP units with one to five states. Across CONUS, the median KGEss increases from 0.82 for one-state networks to 0.89 for two-state networks and 0.90 for five-state networks, suggesting diminishing aggregate gains beyond two states. Basin-specific MCP and LSTM selection yields the same median KGEss of 0.90, while the selected MCP networks use fewer parameters on average. Complementary AIC- and KGE-based selection identifies compact, basin-specific directed-graph representations that balance predictive accuracy and model complexity. These analyses provide an empirical basis for identifying the numbers, types, and interactions of states needed for hydrologic representation. Future studies should test joint training against multiple hydrologic responses, such as streamflow, snow water equivalent, and groundwater storage.

</details>


### [176] [From Interface to Inference: Eliciting Any-Order Inference from Any-Order Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26504)
*Seunggeun Kim,Jaeyeon Kim,Taekyun Lee,Yuyuan Chen,Yilun Du,Sham Kakade,Sitan Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes two complementary methods to enable true any-order inference in diffusion-based masked models: insertion-based and latent-space masked diffusion, addressing positional uncertainty that blocks interface-to-inference transfer; demonstrates improved performance on coding and math tasks and releases code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive models struggle with non-causal, any-order tasks (e.g., code generation, infilling). Although masked diffusion offers an any-order training objective, it does not automatically yield any-order inference due to positional uncertainty. The goal is to design models that natively support any-order inference.

Method: Two approaches: (1) Insertion-based masked diffusion (building on FlexMDM) relaxes fixed-position constraints by enabling insertions, allowing generation across non-contiguous regions; (2) Latent-space masked diffusion shifts prediction to coarser semantic segments, enabling search over latent generation orders. Training/testing on 7B FlexMDM for Python and 125M LatentMDM for GSM8K.

Result: Empirically, the two approaches induce distinct any-order inference behaviors and improve downstream performance. The authors report better results on code (Python) and GSM8K tasks and provide code release.

Conclusion: Native any-order inference can be achieved via complementary insertion-based and latent-space diffusion strategies, addressing positional uncertainty and enabling flexible generation orders; these approaches broaden the applicability of masked diffusion to non-causal tasks.

Abstract: Many discrete reasoning tasks, such as code generation, are inherently non-causal: programmers move between high-level structure and local details, a process we call any-order inference. For autoregressive language models, which lack a native any-order interface, non-causal abilities such as infilling and next-edit prediction require hand-designed mechanisms. Can we instead design models that natively support any-order inference? Masked diffusion models have recently emerged as compelling candidates, as their any-order training objective naturally offers an any-order prediction interface. This interface, however, does not automatically yield any-order inference. We demonstrate that this interface-inference gap stems from positional uncertainty: fixed-canvas, token-level models may know what semantic component should appear without knowing where to place it. In light of this, we propose two complementary approaches: (1) Insertion-based masked diffusion, building on FlexMDM (Kim et al, 2025), relaxes fixed-position commitments via insertions, enabling generation across non-contiguous regions. (2) Latent-space masked diffusion shifts prediction to coarser semantic segments, enabling search over latent generation orders. Empirically, we train a 7B FlexMDM for Python coding and a 125M LatentMDM for GSM8K and show that both approaches induce distinct any-order inference behaviors and improve downstream performance. We release our codebase at https://github.com/SeunggeunKimkr/genuine-any-order.

</details>


### [177] [Collaborative Weighting with Pessimistic Critic for Mitigating Overestimation in Off-Policy Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26509)
*Gong Gao,Xiao Lai,Ziqi Xie,Guojie Chen,Xianhui Liu,Weidong Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CWAC enhances off-policy continuous control by explicitly handling predictive uncertainty with a distributional critic and collaborative weighting of TD-errors and uncertainty, plus stochastic pessimistic value estimation; it integrates with SAC/TD3/DDPG and improves performance with low overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TD learning in off-policy continuous control introduces noisy targets and non-stationary optimization; greedy policy updates amplify early estimation errors, causing persistent overestimation and instability. Existing bias mitigation methods can overemphasize high-uncertainty transitions due to data limitations, worsening bias.

Method: Use a distributional critic to model return uncertainty. Introduce a collaborative weighting mechanism that jointly reweights TD-errors and uncertainty to favor reliable samples. Employ a stochastic pessimistic value estimation by sampling from the return distribution to curb error propagation during policy updates. Integrate seamlessly into existing off-policy algorithms (SAC, TD3, DDPG) with minimal overhead.

Result: Empirical experiments show significant performance gains across a diverse set of simulated tasks, demonstrating improved stability and efficiency in learning; code is publicly available.

Conclusion: CWAC offers a unified, uncertainty-aware framework that reduces overestimation and instability in off-policy actor-critic methods, with straightforward integration into standard algorithms.

Abstract: Deep off-policy reinforcement learning algorithms for continuous control typically rely on neural value function approximation to guide policy improvement. However, temporal-difference (TD) learning introduces noisy targets, resulting in non-stationary optimization, while greedy policy updates amplify early-stage estimation errors. The recursive propagation of such errors leads to persistent overestimation bias and degraded training stability in actor-critic methods. Existing approaches attempt to alleviate this issue via prioritized sampling or modified value learning objectives, but often overemphasize high-uncertainty transitions caused by limited data coverage or bootstrapping errors, thereby further amplifying bias.In this paper, we propose Collaborative Weighting Actor-Critic (CWAC), a unified framework that explicitly accounts for predictive uncertainty in value estimation. CWAC employs distributional critic to model return uncertainty and introduces a collaborative weighting mechanism that jointly reweights TD-errors and uncertainty, enabling robust learning from reliable samples while suppressing noisy updates. In addition, we incorporate a stochastic pessimistic value estimation scheme via sampling from the return distribution, which effectively mitigates error propagation during policy improvement. CWAC can be seamlessly integrated into existing off-policy algorithm frameworks such as SAC, TD3, and DDPG with minimal overhead. Empirical results demonstrate that our proposed method significantly enhances performance across a diverse range of simulated tasks. Our code is publicly available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/CWAC-348E.

</details>


### [178] [Temporally Centered SIGReg Improves Multi-Task LeWorldModel Learning: From Analysis to Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26924)
*Chang Liu,Fei Suo,Yanzhou Jin,Yusuke Iwasawa,Yutaka Matsuo,Yaonan Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Marginal Gaussian priors via SIGReg clash with multi-task latent structure; applying SIGReg to temporally centered residuals fixes aliasing, preserving anti-collapse and enabling stable multi-task world-model learning with strong LIBERO gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address instability and collapse in end-to-end multi-task world-model learning caused by regularizing the latent marginal distribution toward an isotropic Gaussian.

Method: Replace SIGReg regularization target from the latent marginal distribution to temporally centered residuals, removing pressure on cluster center separation, while maintaining anti-collapse effects; evaluate on LIBERO multi-task benchmarks.

Result: 1.7x improvement in long-horizon downstream success; average success across four suites rises from 53.2% to 73.6%; without external pretraining, performance rivals Diffusion Policy from scratch and approaches large-scale pretrained baselines.

Conclusion: The study reveals a structural incompatibility between marginal Gaussian priors and multi-task latent structure and proposes a simple, effective residual-based SIGReg as a scalable solution for end-to-end multi-task world-model learning.

Abstract: Recent work on LeWorldModel (LeWM) has shown that the Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularizer (SIGReg) enables stable end-to-end world-model learning from pixels by regularizing the latent marginal distribution toward an isotropic Gaussian, thereby preventing representation collapse. While effective and elegant in single-task settings, this recipe does not extend reliably to multi-task training, leading to substantially worse downstream behavior-cloning performance. In this paper, we show that marginal Gaussianization compresses the separation between task-dependent latent clusters relative to within-cluster variation. This compression introduces representation aliasing across tasks and states, and makes the learned representations highly sensitive to small visual perturbations. To address this problem, we apply SIGReg to temporally centered residuals rather than to the latent marginal distribution. This surrogate target places no direct regularization pressure on the separation among cluster centers, removes the requirement that the full latent follow a single isotropic Gaussian, and retains the anti-collapse effect of SIGReg. On the LIBERO benchmark, our method improves downstream success on the long-horizon suite by 1.7x and raises the average success rate across four suites from 53.2% to 73.6%. Without external pretraining, it slightly outperforms Diffusion Policy trained from scratch and approaches the performance of large-scale pretrained policy baselines. These results reveal a structural incompatibility between marginal Gaussian priors and multi-task latent structure, and provide a simple route toward stable and scalable end-to-end multi-task world-model learning.

</details>


### [179] [HiFloat4 Format for End-To-End Reinforcement Learning Post-Training of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26515)
*Hei Yi Mak,Shadan Golestan,Hoang Le,Mehran Taghian Jazi,Yunke Peng,Yaoyuan Wang,Yao Wang,Junsong Wang,Tianchi Hu,Fengchen He,Guipeng Hu,Tanzila Rahman,Anandharaju Durai Raju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: End-to-end FP4 RL post-training is feasible by correcting rollout activations with a lightweight Rollout-ResQ, aided by the HiF4 4-bit format; this reduces the BF16 accuracy gap to near 1% on large LMs, while the FP4 format choice largely bounds recoverable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify the primary sources of degradation in 4-bit RL and determine whether end-to-end FP4 training and rollout can approach full-precision performance, while exposing rollout-training mismatch as a key failure mode and evaluating appropriate quantization strategies.

Method: Perform end-to-end FP4 reinforcement learning post-training with both rollout and training in 4-bit precision. Introduce Rollout Residual Quantization (Rollout-ResQ): a single sparse residual correction added to FP4 rollout matmuls, constrained to a hardware-friendly sparsity pattern. Use the HiFloat4 (HiF4) format with three-level hierarchical scaling. Evaluate on Qwen2.5-3B and Qwen2.5-Math-7B; compare to BF16 and full-precision baselines and test MXFP4.

Result: Rollout-ResQ with HiF4 substantially narrows the accuracy gap to BF16: from 4.9% to 1.1% on the tested models. For MXFP4, the gap reduces from 13.6% to 5.3%. The dominant error source is rollout activation quantization; rollout-training mismatch is identified as the principal failure mode, and standard pretraining fixes are insufficient. The HiF4 format is crucial to preserving resolution under FP4 and enabling effective end-to-end FP4 RL post-training.

Conclusion: HiF4 enables practical end-to-end FP4 RL post-training, and Rollout-ResQ provides an effective activation-side correction that brings FP4 RL close to BF16 with modest overhead. FP4 format choice sets the recoverable accuracy ceiling, and rollout-training mismatch is the key challenge to address.

Abstract: We present, to our knowledge, the first end-to-end FP4 RL post-training, in which both the rollout and training policies, including their forward and backward passes, operate at 4-bit precision. A systematic study reveals that the dominant source of degradation in FP4 RL is not training-side quantization error but rollout activation quantization: outliers stretch the dynamic range so far that a large number of activation values underflow to zero under FP4. Counterintuitively, restoring the training policy to higher precision while keeping the rollout in FP4 makes accuracy worse than full FP4 baseline, exposing rollout-training mismatch as the principal failure mode and ruling out standard pretraining-style fixes. We address this with Rollout Residual Quantization (Rollout-ResQ): a single residual correction term constrained to a hardware-friendly sparsity pattern, added only to the FP4 rollout matmul -- a lightweight correction that recovers most of the precision lost to outlier-driven underflow without inflating the rollout's compute footprint. On Qwen2.5-3B and Qwen2.5-Math-7B, Rollout-ResQ paired with the HiFloat4 (HiF4) format -- whose three-level hierarchical scaling preserves resolution under FP4's tight 4-bit budget -- closes the accuracy gap to BF16 from 4.9% to 1.1%, bringing fully quantized FP4 RL within striking distance of full precision. Applied to the open-standard MXFP4, the same recipe narrows the gap from 13.6% to 5.3%, revealing that FP4 format choice is a key factor that determines the ceiling on recoverable accuracy. Together, these results establish HiF4 as the enabling format for end-to-end FP4 RL post-training, and Rollout-ResQ as the activation-side mechanism that makes the gap to BF16 closable.

</details>


### [180] [What Can Latent World Models Know? Physical Parameter Identifiability in Multimodal Predictive Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27017)
*Kaizhen Tan,Xin Xu,Siru Tao,Hanzhe Hong,Yang Feng,Heqing Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent world models identifiably capture some physical parameters depending on inputs and prediction targets; stiffness is retained only if touch is forecast; drag is hard to recover and largely set by objective structure; overall, the objective and data shape what the latent can and cannot represent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand which physical quantities latent representations in predictive world models contain, and how this content is determined by observations and training objectives, using controlled interventions in a physics-variant environment (POKEWORLD).

Method: Introduce a certificate-gated protocol that first certifies parameter recoverability from raw observations, then tests whether the parameter is represented in the latent. Use POKEWORLD with visually identical objects differing in mass, drag, and contact stiffness. Compare single-step vs multi-step prediction, vision-only latents, and multimodal objectives across RH20T with two robots and 4,258 episodes. Assess recoverability certificates and latent readouts (R^2) for stiffness and drag under different objectives, including a supervised head.

Result: Stiffness enters the latent only when touch is forecast (R^2 ≈ 0.50) versus −0.02 when signal is merely fused. Vision-only latents discard even perfectly visible object state under single-step prediction. Drag shows a high recoverability certificate (≈0.89) but plateaus near 0.13 under deterministic objectives; a supervised head raises this to ≈0.45. Readouts that are slow or ratio-type under sensed coordinates fall outside what standard objectives capture. The effects hold across two robots and 4,258 episodes; full multimodal objective yields held-out gains that grow with scale, whereas partial information or prediction pressure yields flat results.

Conclusion: The content acquired by a latent strongly depends on the objective structure: inputs cap what can be learned, and what the model is trained to predict determines what persists in the latent. Drag becomes identifiable only with stronger prediction pressure, while stiffness requires forecasted touch; additional data helps mainly for parameters already represented, with full multimodal objectives enabling broader identifiability as data scales.

Abstract: A central premise of latent world models is that predicting the future forces a representation to internalize the physics of its environment. Which physical quantities does a trained latent actually contain, and what decides this? We answer with controlled interventions in POKEWORLD, an interactive environment whose visually identical objects hide mass, drag, and contact stiffness. A certificate-gated protocol first certifies each parameter as recoverable from raw observations, then measures whether it enters the latent, so a null result can be attributed to the objective rather than to the environment. The resulting identifiability map has two organizing mechanisms and one frontier. Inputs limit what can be known, while prediction targets decide what is retained. Stiffness enters the latent only when touch is forecast ($R^2=0.50$, compared with $-0.02$ when the same signal is merely fused into the input), and under single-step prediction a vision-only latent discards even perfectly visible object state. Drag marks the frontier. It carries a recoverability certificate of 0.89 yet plateaus near 0.13 under every deterministic prediction objective we test, while a supervised head on the same trunk reaches 0.45. Parameters whose readout is slow and ratio-type under the sensed coordinates fall outside what these objectives acquire. On RH20T, an input-target factorial across scaling curves reproduces both mechanisms across two robots and 4,258 episodes. Every arm missing information or prediction pressure stays flat over a fivefold data range, and only the full multimodal objective forecasts force beyond a persistence baseline, with held-out gains that grow with scale. Objective structure determines which physical parameters a latent acquires, and additional data improves only the parameters it already acquires.

</details>


### [181] [From Unsupervised Subgroups to Hypothetical State-Intervention Policies: An Evaluation of Selected Subgrouping Methods in Observational Health Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26521)
*Vasundhara Acharya,Bulent Yener*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for creating pretreatment-driven subgroups using causal-discovery informed covariate selection, unsupervised clustering, and held-out doubly robust evaluation to guide budget-constrained policy prioritization in observational data; finds modest estimated utilities but no statistically significant policy differences after multiple testing; conclusions are assumption-dependent and not causal proof.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Observational biomedical data often yield unstable subgroups when individuals are observed under a single exposure, with unknown true treatment effects and uncertain causal structure. There is a need for interpretable, pretreatment-based units for policy prioritization that do not rely on exposure/outcome/treatment-effect estimates.

Method: 1) Causal-discovery-informed covariate selection and discovery-evaluation sample splitting. 2) Inductive unsupervised clustering methods (K-means, hard and membership-weighted Fuzzy C-means, Bayesian Gaussian Mixture Models) plus a supervised causal-forest-derived CATE-tree comparator. 3) Uncertainty-aware subgroup selection. 4) Held-out doubly robust policy evaluation under a 70% budget for hypothetical state contrasts (BMI/obesity vs non-obesity; glucose state shifts) in PIMA Indians Diabetes dataset and lifetime-smoking-history contrast in NHANES.

Result: Highest ungated utilities: BMI policy with Bayesian GMM = 0.799; glucose policy with hard or membership-weighted FCM = 0.735; smoking-history policy with K-means = 0.775. All paired 95% CIs for policy-risk differences included zero; none remained significant after Holm adjustment. Bayesian pooling generally preserved ungated allocations; Empirical Bernstein gating was more conservative. Similar estimated utilities could correspond to different prioritized individuals.

Conclusion: The findings should be interpreted as assumption-dependent decision-support for hypothetical state contrasts, not proof of intervention benefit. The framework emphasizes interpretability and uncertainty handling but results depend on modeling choices and data, warranting cautious application and external validation.

Abstract: Conventional subgroup analyses can yield unstable and difficult-to-interpret conclusions, especially in observational biomedical data where each individual is observed under only one exposure state, true individual treatment effects are unavailable, and causal structure is uncertain. We investigate whether subgroups constructed from pretreatment characteristics, without using exposure, outcome, or estimated treatment-effect information, can serve as interpretable units for budget-constrained policy prioritization. We propose a framework combining causal-discovery-informed covariate selection, discovery-evaluation sample splitting, inductive unsupervised clustering, uncertainty-aware subgroup selection, and held-out doubly robust policy evaluation. We compare K-means, hard, membership-weighted, and stochastic Fuzzy C-means, Bayesian Gaussian mixture models, and a supervised causal-forest-derived CATE-tree comparator. Policies are evaluated under a 70% budget for hypothetical obesity-to-non-obesity and elevated-to-lower-glucose state shifts in the PIMA Indians Diabetes dataset and for a lifetime-smoking-history contrast in NHANES. The highest estimated ungated utilities were 0.799 for the BMI policy using Bayesian GMM, 0.735 for the glucose policy using hard or membership-weighted FCM, and 0.775 for the smoking-history policy using K-means. All paired 95% confidence intervals for policy-risk differences included zero, and no comparison remained statistically significant after Holm adjustment. Bayesian pooling generally preserved ungated allocations, whereas Empirical Bernstein gating was more conservative. Policies with similar estimated utility could nevertheless prioritize different individuals. The findings should be interpreted as assumption-dependent decision-support evidence for hypothetical state contrasts rather than proof of intervention benefit.

</details>


### [182] [The Art of Not Forgetting A Local Learning Architecture for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26523)
*Ashmith Atmuri,Yashaswini Rao Bhogarajula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CMP reduces forgetting in continual learning via sparse codes and local learning with persistent memory, outperforming a parameter-matched Transformer with EWC on domain-incremental byte-level modeling, but exhibits a sizeable single-domain accuracy gap and mixed results on vision benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate catastrophic forgetting without end-to-end backpropagation by combining sparse representations, local learning, and persistent memory, and to evaluate whether this approach can outperform backpropagation-based continual-learning baselines.

Method: Proposes CMP (Cognitive Memory Primitive): inputs represented as sparse relational codes stored in a two-tier competitive memory, with learning through local updates and no end-to-end backpropagation through the feature-generating system. Evaluated on a controlled domain-incremental byte-level language modeling protocol (15 domains, 3 seeds) and compared against a parameter-matched Transformer trained with online Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC). Reported additional experiments include separate head-to-head comparisons, domain-order analyses, a single-domain vision benchmark, and attempts to combine CMP with an independent accuracy-improving mechanism.

Result: CMP shows substantially lower backward transfer than the Transformer+EWC baseline and exhibits stable forgetting across the 15-domain replication; head-to-head and domain-order analyses indicate consistently lower forgetting than the Transformer baseline under the reported settings. However, there is a substantial single-domain accuracy gap relative to the Transformer, a null result on a vision benchmark, and a documented failure to combine CMP with an independent accuracy-improving mechanism.

Conclusion: Sparse representations, local learning, and persistent memory constitute a promising direction for continual learning, but more work is needed to dissect the contributions of learning rules, representations, and architecture to mitigate forgetting and to address trade-offs in accuracy and cross-domain generalization.

Abstract: We introduce CMP (Cognitive Memory Primitive), a continual-learning architecture that repre?sents inputs as sparse relational codes, stores them in a two-tier competitive memory, and learns through local updates without end-to-end backpropagation through its feature-generating system. We investigate whether combining sparse representations, local learning, and persistent memory can reduce catastrophic forgetting relative to conventional backpropagation-based continual?learning approaches. On a controlled domain-incremental byte-level language modeling protocol, CMP demonstrates substantially lower backward transfer than a parameter-matched Trans?former trained with online Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC). Across a three-seed replicated 15-domain experiment, CMP exhibits stable forgetting behavior, while separate head-to-head comparisons and domain-order analyses show consistently lower forgetting than the evaluated Transformer baseline under the reported experimental settings. We report these findings alongside a substantial single-domain accuracy gap relative to the Transformer, a null result on a vision benchmark, and a documented failure to combine CMP with an independent accuracy-improving mechanism, reflecting our commitment to reporting both positive and negative outcomes. These results suggest that the combination of sparse representations, local learning, and persistent memory is a promising direction for continual learning, while motivating further investigation into the respective roles of learning rules, representations, and architectural design in mitigating catastrophic forgetting.

</details>


### [183] [AgentGFM: A Graph Foundation Model with Node-Agent Information-Flow Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26533)
*Jingbo Cui,Jitao Zhao,Di Jin,Dongxiao He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AgentGFM treats graph nodes as agents with a shared, trainable policy to adaptively control information flow during message passing, enabling per-node propagation decisions. It uses a predict-act-observe-correct loop and demonstrates transfers across node-, graph-, and large-scale scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed propagation schemes in Graph Foundation Models fail to capture diverse local topologies across graphs and nodes, limiting transferability of relational patterns. Allowing nodes to autonomously control information flow aims to improve adaptability and generalization.

Method: Formulates node-level control as an agent-based decision process. All node agents share an end-to-end trainable policy. Each node interacts with the graph via a predict-act-observe-correct loop; during act, the node decides (i) source reception, (ii) signal-channel selection, and (iii) gain-aware node-wise halting. The observation is compared to the prediction, and the discrepancy updates node state for subsequent steps. Training is conducted with a unified policy across nodes, across diverse graph topologies.

Result: Extensive experiments across node-level, graph-level, and large-scale transfer tasks show that AgentGFM achieves improved adaptability and transferability across diverse graph topologies compared to fixed schemes.

Conclusion: Adaptive, per-node information-flow control via a shared policy is feasible and beneficial for GFMs, enabling more flexible propagation and better transfer performance across varied graphs.

Abstract: Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) aim to learn transferable knowledge from multi-domain graphs and adapt to unseen scenarios. As a fundamental source of relational semantics in graphs, the transferability of topological patterns has long been central to GFM research. However, local structural patterns may vary across graphs and even among nodes within the same graph. Despite such structural variation, most existing GFMs rely on manually designed propagation schemes and apply them to new graphs largely unchanged. Such fixed schemes may not suit the diverse structural patterns of different nodes. This raises a key question: can each node autonomously determine how information should be propagated through the graph? We refer to this capability as information-flow control. Inspired by recent advances in agent technology, we formulate this problem as agent-based decision making and treat each node as an agent. Accordingly, we propose AgentGFM, in which all node agents follow a shared end-to-end trainable policy rather than using independent models. For adaptive information-flow control, each node interacts with the graph through a predict-act-observe-correct process. During the act stage, the node makes three decisions: source reception, signal-channel selection and gain-aware node-wise halting. The resulting observation is compared with the prediction and their discrepancy is used to correct the node state and guide subsequent interactions. Extensive experiments across node-level, graph-level and large-scale transfer scenarios demonstrate the effectiveness of AgentGFM across diverse graph topologies.

</details>


### [184] [From Tokens to Watt-hours: Analytical Energy Estimation for LLM Inference on Modern GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26571)
*Tina Vartziotis,Rodopi Kosteli,Elli Vartziotis,George Dasoulas,Michael Keckeisen,Konstantinos Skianis,Sotirios Kotsopoulos,Francesca Dominici*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A structured, calibrated analytic estimator for LLM inference energy on NVIDIA H100 GPUs that avoids runtime power measurements by combining parameter-scaled transformer FLOP accounting, calibrated memory-traffic factors, and hardware energy coefficients for FP16/BF16 tensor-core compute and high-bandwidth memory movement. It separates prompt prefill from autoregressive decoding and decomposes energy into compute, parameter-access, KV-cache write, and attention-read, enabling analysis of scaling with model size, context length, and generated-token count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct hardware-based energy measurements for LLM inference are often impractical or unavailable, limiting cross-model comparisons, early-stage design, and sustainability reporting. A transparent, reproducible estimator enables fair comparisons and design-time energy budgeting without instrumenting runtimes.

Method: An analytic estimator that (a) scales FLOPs with model parameters to estimate compute energy, (b) uses calibrated memory-traffic factors to estimate energy from data movement, and (c) applies hardware-specific energy coefficients for FP16/BF16 tensor-core operations and HBM transfers on NVIDIA H100. It explicitly separates prompt prefill from autoregressive decoding and further decomposes total energy into compute, parameter-access, KV-cache write, and attention-read components to study scaling with model size, context length, and generated-token count.

Result: The estimator yields energy estimates for input tokens, output tokens, and complete inference requests, with the ability to analyze how energy scales with model size, context length, and number of generated tokens. It provides transparent, assumption-explicit approximations intended for model comparison and design-time evaluation rather than exact power measurements.

Conclusion: The method offers transparent, reproducible energy approximations suitable for green-coding analysis, model comparison, and design-time evaluation of LLM inference workloads. It should complement—not replace— physical power measurements, and its accuracy depends on calibration quality and hardware-specific coefficients.

Abstract: The operational energy consumption of large language model (LLM) inference is becoming an increasingly important component of the environmental footprint of deployed AI systems. However, direct measurement of inference energy often requires hardware telemetry, power instrumentation, or infrastructure-specific monitoring, limiting its applicability in comparative studies, early-stage system design, and sustainability reporting. This report presents an analytically structured, empirically calibrated, GPU-level methodology for estimating LLM inference energy on NVIDIA H100-class accelerators without direct runtime measurement. The proposed estimator combines parameter-scaled transformer FLOP accounting, calibrated memory-traffic factors, and hardware-specific energy coefficients for FP16/BF16 tensor-core computation and high-bandwidth-memory movement. It explicitly separates prompt prefill from autoregressive decoding, enabling energy estimates for input tokens, output tokens, and complete inference requests. The methodology further decomposes total energy into compute, parameter-access, key-value-cache write, and attention-read components, allowing the scaling behavior with model size, context length, and generated-token count to be analyzed. The resulting estimates are not intended to replace physical power measurements; rather, they provide transparent, reproducible, and assumption-explicit approximations suitable for model comparison, green-coding analysis, and design-time evaluation of LLM inference workloads.

</details>


### [185] [Simultaneous Coverage and Efficiency Guarantee in Online Conformal Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26577)
*Rahul Vaze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified online conformal inference (ACI) framework that overcomes three core limitations—non-cancelling long-run miscoverage, lack of control over prediction-set size, and benchmarking under distribution shift—by providing guarantees across adversarial, stochastic full-score, and covariate-dependent settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ACI methods control only signed long-run coverage, ignore prediction-set width, and benchmark against a fixed predictor, which becomes inappropriate under distribution shift. The work aims to enforce absolute, non-cancelling coverage violations and data-dependent efficiency against a dynamically evolving benchmark.

Method: Adversarial:View ACI updates as projected online gradient descent on the pinball loss; derive simultaneous coverage and efficiency guarantees for arbitrary monotone Lipschitz efficiency objectives without distributional or convexity assumptions. Stochastic (full-score):Introduce a sliding-window quantile tracker and prove a matching minimax lower bound, establishing rate-optimality. Covariate-dependent stochastic:Develop a partitioned ACI that tracks a function-valued oracle threshold and prove simultaneous coverage and efficiency guarantees.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: (i) in the fully adversarial setting, simultaneous coverage and efficiency for any monotone Lipschitz efficiency objective; (ii) in the stochastic full-score setting, a rate-optimal algorithm with matching minimax lower bound; (iii) in the covariate-dependent stochastic setting, a partitioned ACI with guaranteed coverage and efficiency against a function-valued threshold.

Conclusion: A unified online-learning framework delivering non-cancelling coverage and efficient prediction sets under distribution shift, with provable guarantees across adversarial, stochastic full-score, and covariate-dependent regimes.

Abstract: Adaptive conformal inference (ACI) of Gibbs and Cand{è}s and its variants are the standard approach to online conformal prediction under distribution shift, but they suffer from three fundamental limitations. First, their guarantees control only the \emph{signed} long-run coverage error: persistent miscoverage in one direction can be masked by compensating errors later, so a method can satisfy the theoretical guarantee while being badly wrong for extended periods. Second, existing guarantees say nothing about prediction-set size, so validity can be achieved trivially at the cost of unduly wide prediction sets. Third, the efficiency guarantees that do exist compare against a \emph{fixed} predictor chosen in hindsight, a benchmark that becomes increasingly less meaningful once the data-generating distribution shifts, since the very notion of an optimal threshold then changes over time.
  We consider a unified online learning framework that simultaneously controls absolute, non-cancelling coverage violation and prediction-set efficiency against a dynamically evolving benchmark for three important models. In the fully adversarial setting, exploiting the fact that the standard ACI update is exactly projected online gradient descent on the pinball loss, we derive simultaneous coverage and efficiency guarantees for arbitrary monotone Lipschitz efficiency objectives, with no distributional or {\it convexity} assumptions. In the stochastic setting with full-score feedback, we propose a sliding-window quantile tracker and establish a matching minimax lower bound showing our algorithm is rate-optimal. In the covariate-dependent stochastic setting, we develop a partitioned ACI algorithm that tracks a function-valued oracle threshold, and derive simultaneous coverage and efficiency guarantees.

</details>


### [186] [Benchmarking ConvLSTM for One-Day-Ahead IMDAA Rainfall-Field Prediction across Four Indian Cities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26581)
*Tanmay Ghosh,Shaurabh Anand,Rakesh Gomaji Nannewar,Nithin Nagaraj*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConvLSTM is not consistently superior to simpler models for one-day-ahead rainfall on small daily grids; FC-LSTM often wins on domain-mean error; persistence can outperform in some cities; results stress benchmarking across metrics and caution against assuming ConvLSTM's superiority with gridded inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether convolutional recurrence provides tangible benefits for gridded, daily rainfall forecasts on small reanalysis grids, versus simpler baselines, across multiple cities.

Method: Comparative evaluation of ten naive, statistical, tree-based and neural approaches using atmospheric-only and rainfall-history-plus-atmospheric inputs on IMDAA data (June–September 1998–2020) for Bengaluru, Delhi, Kolkata and Mumbai; performance assessed on complete fields, domain-mean rainfall, spatial anomalies and high-rainfall day detection.

Result: ConvLSTM did not consistently outperform simpler alternatives; FC-LSTM yielded the lowest domain-mean rainfall error in Bengaluru, Kolkata and Mumbai, while persistence was best in Delhi. ConvLSTM achieved the lowest spatial-anomaly error only in Mumbai. Rainfall-history inputs improved all three neural architectures in Mumbai; overall ConvLSTM–FC-LSTM differences were small. Neural models tended to underestimate rainfall magnitude and under-predict threshold exceedances; persistence had the highest detection performance in all cities. Sensitivity analyses showed models were most influenced by the latest input day, with broader lag sensitivity in Mumbai.

Conclusion: Gridded inputs alone do not justify ConvLSTM; model choice should be grounded in rigorous benchmarking across average, spatial, and extreme-event metrics. In daily gridded rainfall forecasting on small domains, simpler architectures can rival or outperform ConvLSTM.

Abstract: Convolutional long short-term memory networks (ConvLSTMs) are widely used for precipitation forecasting, but most evidence for their performance comes from dense, high-frequency radar sequences. This study tests whether convolutional recurrence improves one-day-ahead rainfall-field prediction on small daily reanalysis grids. Indian Monsoon Data Assimilation and Analysis (IMDAA) fields for June-September 1998-2020 were analysed for Bengaluru, Delhi, Kolkata and Mumbai. Ten naive, statistical, tree-based and neural approaches were compared using atmospheric-only and rainfall-history-plus-atmospheric inputs. Performance was assessed for complete fields, domain-mean rainfall, spatial anomalies and high-rainfall days.
  ConvLSTM did not consistently outperform simpler alternatives. FC-LSTM produced the numerically lowest domain-mean rainfall error in Bengaluru, Kolkata and Mumbai, whereas persistence performed best in Delhi. ConvLSTM produced the numerically lowest spatial-anomaly error only in Mumbai, where rainfall fields showed greater short-term spatial continuity and rainfall-history inputs improved all three neural architectures. The difference between ConvLSTM and FC-LSTM was nevertheless small. Neural models underestimated rainfall magnitude and predicted too few threshold exceedances on high-rainfall days, while persistence achieved the highest detection performance in every city. Post-hoc analyses showed that the selected models were most sensitive to the latest input day, with broader recent-lag sensitivity in Mumbai. These findings show that gridded inputs alone do not justify ConvLSTM and that architecture choice should follow strong benchmarking across average, spatial and high-rainfall performance.

</details>


### [187] [Uncertainty-Guided LLM Semantic Augmentation for Heterogeneous Treatment Effect Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26599)
*Jialu Xu,Mengkun Liang,Guannan Liu,Xiaojie Mao,Junjie Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CURL: a plug-in adapter that uses estimator uncertainty to allocate representation capacity for CATE estimation by building two channels (assignment-oriented and heterogeneity-oriented) via prompts to a frozen LLM, improving multiple learners across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating CATE requires accurate nuisance adjustment and representation of outcome-heterogeneity interactions; naive encodings risk losing semantic relations and higher-order interactions and can be locally unstable due to separate assignment- and heterogeneity-side channels.

Method: CURL queries a frozen LLM with role-conditioned prompts to derive two types of representations from covariates (assignment-oriented and heterogeneity-oriented); routes these through two separated pathways as a plug-in adapter, guided by estimator uncertainty to allocate capacity.

Result: On four benchmarks, CURL improves ten host learners in most settings; ablation studies, dynamics refinement, route reassignment, and probe analyses support the intended design and roles of the two channels.

Conclusion: Estimator-uncertainty guided, dual-channel representation learning via a frozen LLM offers robust, plug-in enhancement for CATE estimation by leveraging semantic capacity to address instability; results across multiple learners and benchmarks demonstrate practical gains, with room for exploration of efficiency and prompts.

Abstract: Estimating heterogeneous treatment effects is central to targeted interventions, such as personalized promotions and precision medicine. We focus on the conditional average treatment effect (CATE), a standard estimand for characterizing such heterogeneity. Even under standard identification conditions, finite-sample CATE estimation requires learning the nuisance structure for covariate adjustment and treatment-effect heterogeneity, often together with an effective representation of X. Raw numerical and categorical encodings can leave semantic relations and higher-order interactions implicit, making this joint task locally unstable. A motivating study further shows that this instability appears through partially separable assignment- and heterogeneity-side channels. Building on this observation, we propose CURL (Causal Uncertainty-guided Representation Learning), a plug-in adapter that uses estimator uncertainty to allocate pretrained semantic capacity to locally unstable units. CURL queries a frozen LLM through two role-conditioned prompts, constructs assignment- and heterogeneity-oriented representations from the observed covariates, and routes them through separated pathways. On four benchmarks, CURL improves ten host learners in most settings, while ablation, refinement-dynamics, route-reassignment, and probe analyses support the intended design and roles of the two channels.

</details>


### [188] [FedWeave: Rethinking the Unit of Specialization in Heterogeneous Federated MoE-LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26618)
*Donghang Duan,Xu Zheng,Lizong Zhang,Chong Mu,Meng Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedWeave introduces asymmetric aggregation for federated MoE-LoRA to separate expert aggregation from router optimization, enabling prototype-level expert alignment across heterogeneous clients while maintaining mixed-task router trajectories, yielding improved performance and robust sparse inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Task heterogeneity in federated MoE-LoRA causes cross-task gradient conflicts and interference when aggregating updates. Existing client-level specialization struggles with cross-task coherence; there is a need for pattern-level (expert) purity and contrastive routing signals.

Method: FedWeave adopts asymmetric aggregation: it performs prototype-level expert aggregation by discovering local prototypes and aligning them across clients, while keeping router training on mixed-task trajectories. It separates expert aggregation from router optimization, enabling unsupervised prototype discovery for local buckets, sparse inference with a single active expert, and theoretical analysis of off-pattern contamination, router trajectory fragmentation, and sparse-inference risk bounds.

Result: On a heterogeneous multi-task benchmark with mainstream LLM backbones, FedWeave consistently outperforms strong baselines. Ablation studies validate the importance of prototype-based expert aggregation and the separation of router optimization.

Conclusion: Asymmetric aggregation improves expert convergence and router consensus in federated MoE-LoRA, enabling effective prototype-level specialization with mixed-task router observations, and supports robust sparse inference with theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: Federated PEFT enables LLMs to collaboratively adapt to decentralized private data without sharing raw examples. However, task heterogeneity across clients can cause cross-task interference and gradient conflicts during aggregation. Federated MoE-LoRA addresses this challenge through specialized LoRA experts and conditional routing. Yet existing methods typically specialize at client granularity, implicitly assuming task-coherent clients. Our core insight is that experts need purity, namely pattern-coherent updates that preserve specialization, whereas routers need contrast, namely mixed-task observations that support expert comparison. We propose FedWeave, a framework that adopts asymmetric aggregation, separating expert aggregation from router optimization to meet these two requirements. FedWeave uses unsupervised prototype discovery to form local buckets and align them across clients, enabling prototype-level expert aggregation while retaining mixed-task client trajectories for router training. At inference, FedWeave performs sparse inference with one active expert while preserving nearly all soft-routing performance. Our theoretical analysis explains why asymmetric aggregation is advantageous: it controls expert convergence in stationarity through off-pattern contamination, identifies the consensus error induced by fragmented router trajectories, and bounds sparse-inference risk. On a heterogeneous multi-task benchmark with mainstream LLM backbones, FedWeave consistently outperforms strong baselines, while ablations verify the effectiveness of our design.

</details>


### [189] [Enhancing Automated Machine Learning via Homogeneous Train-Test Splitting Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26625)
*Yearn Tan Yin Tze,Charles Grellois*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Examines how train-test distribution similarity impacts AutoML evaluation, showing geometry-based splits yield unstable estimates and proposing an Optimised-Distribution method that maximizes similarity (MMD ~89%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable model evaluation requires train/test splits that reflect the same underlying data distribution; imbalanced, clustered, or spatially autocorrelated data can violate this, biasing AutoML results.

Method: Compared five splitting strategies (random, stratified, Kennard-Stone, Duplex, SPXY) across 15 UCI datasets; assessed distributional similarity with chi-square, Kolmogorov-Smirnov, and MMD tests; introduced an Optimised-Distribution method that explicitly optimizes similarity and reported mean MMD similarity.

Result: Geometry-based splits consistently yield near-zero MMD scores, causing instability in performance estimates; Optimised-Distribution achieves the highest mean MMD similarity at 89.0% across strategies.

Conclusion: Explicitly optimizing distribution similarity improves the reliability of AutoML evaluation; geometry-based splits with near-zero MMD can destabilize estimates; further work should link similarity metrics to predictive performance and explore practical guidelines for split selection.

Abstract: Accurate model evaluation in machine learning depends critically on how datasets are split into training and testing subsets. Standard random splitting assumes that both partitions share the same underlying distribution, an assumption often violated in datasets with class imbalance, natural clustering, or spatial autocorrelation. This paper investigates the role of statistical similarity in train-test splitting and its consequences for AutoML model evaluation. Five established strategies are compared across fifteen UCI benchmark datasets: random splitting, stratified sampling, Kennard-Stone, Duplex, and SPXY. Similarity is assessed using chi-square, Kolmogorov-Smirnov, and Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) tests. Geometry-based methods consistently produce near-zero MMD scores, introducing instability into downstream performance estimates. The proposed Optimised-Distribution method treats similarity as an explicit optimisation objective and achieves the highest mean MMD similarity, 89.0%, across all strategies evaluated.

</details>


### [190] [Understanding Context Sampling in TabPFN on Small Tabular Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26628)
*Mohammed Abdullah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Larger, diverse context sets improve TabPFN accuracy and stability; random sampling suffices due to feature-space coverage, while distribution-matching or expensive selection methods offer little benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how context size and the strategy for selecting labeled examples (the context) affect prediction stability and accuracy in TabPFN on small tabular datasets.

Method: Repeatedly sample contexts of varying size from 15 OpenML datasets; evaluate prediction accuracy (AUC) and stability; compare random sampling to K-Means and farthest-point sampling; use mixed-effects analysis to identify predictors of accuracy (diversity, coverage, and feature-mean shift).

Result: Larger contexts yield higher accuracy and substantially lower AUC variability; accuracy correlates with diversity and coverage rather than simple feature-mean matching; random sampling provides feature-space coverage in expectation and achieves similar accuracy to expensive methods; K-Means and farthest-point sampling cost orders of magnitude more for little to no accuracy gain.

Conclusion: For TabPFN on small tabular datasets, sampling randomness suffices for constructing effective contexts; prioritizing context diversity and feature-space coverage over distributional matching is beneficial; practitioners should prefer larger random contexts to balance accuracy and stability with minimal selection cost.

Abstract: TabPFN performs classification through in-context learning: it conditions on a set of labeled training rows (the context, or prototypes) and predicts test labels without gradient updates. On small tabular datasets, practitioners must still choose the context size and which rows constitute the context. We study how these choices affect prediction stability, accuracy, and selection cost using repeated context sampling on 15 OpenML datasets. Specifically, we investigate (i) whether larger contexts reduce prediction variability across random draws, (ii) whether accuracy depends on preserving the training distribution or on feature-space coverage, and (iii) whether expensive selection methods such as K-Means and farthest-point sampling provide benefits over uniform random sampling.
  We find that larger contexts are both more accurate and substantially more stable, with AUC coefficient of variation decreasing from roughly 6 to 18% at k=16 to 1 to 4% at larger context sizes on datasets with room for improvement. Although accuracy correlates with distribution representativeness in random contexts, controlled experiments show that matching feature means alone can reduce accuracy by up to 0.5 AUC because it reduces context diversity. Mixed-effects analysis identifies diversity and coverage, rather than feature-mean matching, as the stronger predictor of accuracy (diversity beta=+0.23, p=3x10^-12; feature-mean shift beta=-0.01, p=0.71). K-Means and farthest-point sampling achieve similar accuracy to random selection while requiring two to three orders of magnitude more selection cost. These results show that random sampling succeeds because it provides feature-space coverage in expectation, not because it reproduces the underlying data distribution.

</details>


### [191] [RAG-HAR+: Towards Cost-Efficient LLM-Based Human Activity Recognition for Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26631)
*Hansi Karunarathna,Nirhoshan Sivaroopan,Chamara Madarasingha,Anura Jayasumana,Kanchana Thilakarathna*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A training-free, retrieval-augmented HAR framework (RAG-HAR) and its extension RAG-HAR+ that uses an offline Retrieval Designer Agent to tailor feature groups for dataset-specific retrieval; employs majority voting on retrieved neighbors and an LLM-based Ambiguity Resolver for uncertain cases; shows competitive performance across six HAR benchmarks with reduced LLM usage and supports a mobile prototype.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HAR often requires dataset-specific training and large labeled data; retrieval-augmented generation can guide classifier without training, enabling cross-domain adaptation to new sensor configurations and activity taxonomies while reducing labeling and adaptation costs.

Method: Offline Retrieval Designer Agent constructs dataset-specific feature groups from a diverse pool of motion descriptors. During inference, sensor windows are described statistically and compared using the designed features to retrieve similar labeled examples. Majority voting is applied for confident cases; uncertain cases are deferred to an LLM-based Ambiguity Resolver. A mobile prototype extension demonstrates practical feasibility.

Result: Competitive or improved performance across six HAR benchmarks with reduced LLM usage, token costs, and inference time; demonstration of retrieval-first HAR viability in mobile sensing scenarios.

Conclusion: RAG-HAR+ strengthens retrieval while reducing dependence on LLMs, offering a practical, training-free HAR approach. The offline feature design and retrieval-first process enable efficient deployment, including on mobile devices.

Abstract: Human Activity Recognition (HAR) from wearable sensors supports applications in healthcare, rehabilitation, fitness tracking, and smart environments. Yet, existing deep learning approaches require dataset-specific training, large labeled corpora, and repeated adaptation to new sensor settings or activity taxonomies. Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Human Activity Recognition (RAG-HAR) addresses this by framing HAR as a training-free, retrieval-augmented task, in which statistical descriptions of sensor windows are used to retrieve similar labeled examples that guide LLM-based classification. We introduce RAG-HAR+, a retrieval-first and cost-optimized extension that strengthens retrieval while reducing dependence on LLM-based inference. RAG-HAR+ uses an offline Retrieval Designer Agent to design dataset-specific feature groups from a diverse pool of motion descriptors, enabling sensor windows to be compared using features better aligned with dataset-specific activity patterns. During inference, RAG-HAR+ uses majority voting over retrieved neighbors for samples with strong retrieval evidence and defers only uncertain cases to an LLM-based Ambiguity Resolver Agent. Across six HAR benchmarks, RAG-HAR+ maintains competitive or improved performance while reducing LLM usage, token consumption, and inference time. We further extend the RAG-HAR mobile prototype to demonstrate the practical feasibility of retrieval-first, LLM-assisted HAR in mobile sensing scenarios.

</details>


### [192] [AIGen: Automating AI Bill of Materials Generation Through Hybrid MLOps Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26652)
*Federica Pepe,Daniele Bifolco,Costantino Martignetti,Aureliano D'Amici,Fabiano Izzo,Damian A. Tamburri,Massimiliano Di Penta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AIGen is a modular AI Bill of Materials (AIBoM) generator that produces SPDX 3.0 AI-profile compliant inventories of AI system components, leveraging MLflow, mining heuristics, and LLMs, with a plugin interface for domain collectors across Hugging Face, PyTorch, and TensorFlow, to support compliance with EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, and ISO/IEC 42001.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for rigorous, machine-readable documentation of AI artifacts (datasets, weights, pipelines, dependencies) to support governance and regulatory compliance. Existing tooling for SPDX 3.0 AI profiles is limited, hindering scalable, auditable supply-chain transparency.

Method: AIGen is a modular generator built on top of the MLflow MLOps framework. It blends mining heuristics with Large Language Models to extract, assemble, and structure AI artifacts into AIBoMs that conform to the SPDX 3.0 AI profile. A plugin interface enables domain-specific collectors to be added without changing core code, supporting frameworks such as Hugging Face, PyTorch, and TensorFlow.

Result: The approach yields machine-readable, interoperable AIBoMs that facilitate regulatory compliance and governance. AIGen provides a concrete, reusable foundation for transparent AI supply-chain governance and helps align practices with EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, and ISO/IEC 42001.

Conclusion: AIGen offers a modular, extensible solution for automated AI BOM generation that can scale across ecosystems and support regulatory, risk-management, and standards-alignment objectives.

Abstract: The responsible development and deployment of artificial intelligence (AI) systems requires rigorous documentation of their constituent artifacts, e.g., datasets, model weights, training pipelines, and runtime dependencies. Although the Software Package Data Exchange (SPDX) 3.0 standard introduced native support for AI and dataset profiles, practical tooling capable of generating standards-compliant AI Bills of Materials (AIBoMs) in an automated and extensible manner remains scarce. This paper presents AIGen, a modular AIBoM generator that produces machine-readable, interoperable inventories of AI system components that comply with the SPDX 3.0 AI profile. AIGen works on top of the MLflow MLOps framework and combines mining heuristics with Large Language Models to generate AIBoMs. A plugin interface allows practitioners to extend the tool with domain-specific collectors without modifying the core codebase, supporting heterogeneous AI frameworks such as Hugging Face, PyTorch, and TensorFlow. AIGen is designed to facilitate compliance with the European Union AI Act, the NIST AI Risk Management Framework, and ISO/IEC 42001, providing a concrete, reusable foundation for transparent, accountable AI supply chain governance. Tool URL: https://github.com/danielebifolco/AIGen Tool Video: https://youtu.be/\_nAbXDWfVL4

</details>


### [193] [Efficient Heteroscedastic Bayesian Optimization for Risk-Aware AutoRL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26680)
*Mingxuan Che,Tsung-Yuan Tseng,Theresa Eimer,Marius Lindauer,Alexander von Rohr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ERAHBO is a heteroscedastic Bayesian optimization method for RL hyperparameters that jointly models mean and variance of outcomes to optimize for high average return with low variability, using adaptive re-sampling to improve sample efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL outcomes are highly stochastic and both their mean and variability depend on hyperparameters; traditional HP optimization is sample-inefficient and may yield fragile policy performance.

Method: ERAHBO builds a Bayesian optimizer that models both the mean and variance of learning outcomes as functions of HP configurations (heteroscedastic modeling). It uses adaptive re-sampling to allocate evaluations across HPs rather than a fixed budget per configuration, aiming to maximize expected return while controlling risk.

Result: Empirical evaluations across diverse RL algorithms and environments show ERAHBO generally outperforms risk-neutral and risk-averse baselines, improving sample efficiency for optimizing risk-averse returns.

Conclusion: ERAHBO provides a more sample-efficient, robust HP optimization framework for RL by explicitly modeling heteroscedasticity and employing adaptive re-sampling to balance high mean performance with reduced variability.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown remarkable success across a wide range of complex tasks. However, RL outcomes can be highly stochastic, and both expected performance and variability often depend on hyperparameter (HP) configurations. We propose efficient and risk-averse heteroscedastic Bayesian Optimization (ERAHBO), a Bayesian optimization method that models both the mean and variance of learning outcomes as functions of the HP configurations. ERAHBO aims to identify HP configurations that achieve high average return while reducing variability across training runs, and it improves the sample efficiency of the HP optimization via adaptive re-sampling rather than a fixed budget per HP. Empirical evaluations across diverse RL algorithms and environments demonstrate that ERAHBO generally outperforms both risk-neutral and risk-averse baselines, delivering improved sample efficiency for risk-averse returns.

</details>


### [194] [Universality and Approximation Rates of Graph Neural Networks with Random Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26699)
*Lukas Gonon,Thilo Meyer-Brandis,Niklas Weber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PENNs with partially random node features can universally approximate any measurable permutation-invariant or permutation-equivariant function on directed graphs of fixed size, with probabilistic guarantees. For k-times continuously differentiable functions (k≥2), the paper provides upper bounds on approximation rates tied to the depth and sparsity (nonzero weights) of the feedforward components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify the expressiveness of permutation-equivariant neural networks (PENNs) built from feedforward components when using random node features, and to establish universal approximation capabilities for graph functions.

Method: Proves universality results for PENNs with partially random node features, showing they can approximate any measurable invariant/equivariant function on fixed-size directed graphs. For functions with k≥2 continuous derivatives, derives upper bounds on approximation rates, linking network complexity (depth, sparsity) to accuracy.

Result: Establishes a probabilistic universal approximation property for PENNs with partial randomness in node features, applicable to both permutation-invariant and -equivariant graph functions. Provides quantitative, depth- and sparsity-dependent bounds on approximation rates for smooth targets.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that incorporating partial randomness into PENNs suffices for universal expressive power on directed graphs of fixed size and offers rate bounds that relate architecture complexity to approximation accuracy, contributing to a theoretical foundation for GNN expressiveness with random features.

Abstract: We investigate message-passing graph neural networks with random node features. Random node features are known to enhance the expressiveness of graph neural networks (GNNs) both theoretically and empirically.
