Here, we establish a novel universality result focusing on permutation-equivariant neural networks (PENNs), a class of GNNs built from feedforward neural network components that subsumes many prominent GNN architectures. We show that PENNs, combined with partially random node features, can approximate arbitrarily well in probability any measurable permutation-invariant or permutation-equivariant function on directed graphs of fixed size with multidimensional node and edge features. For $k$-times continuously differentiable functions, $k\geq 2$, we also derive upper bounds on the approximation rates, relating the complexity of the feedforward components of a PENN in terms of layer depth and number of nonzero weights to the desired approximation accuracy.

</details>


### [195] [Mixture-of-experts for handwriting trajectory reconstruction from IMU sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26708)
*Florent Imbert,Eric Anquetil,Yann Soullard,Romain Tavenard*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A MOE-based framework for reconstructing online handwriting trajectories from a sensor-equipped digital pen, separating touch and hover dynamics, plus a new public dataset and improved accuracy over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve precision of online handwriting trajectory reconstruction on any surface and enhance learning aids by accurately modeling both pen-down touches and in-air hovering to guide subsequent strokes.

Method: Employ a Mixture-of-Experts with two specialized models: a touching expert for pen-down touches and a hovering expert for in-air trajectories. Each expert is enhanced with context-specific data. Introduce a public benchmark dataset to standardize evaluation and enable comparisons.

Result: Reported significant accuracy improvements over primary competitors, aided by the two-expert decomposition and the new dataset that facilitates future research.

Conclusion: A dual-expert MOE approach effectively models touch and hover phases for precise handwriting reconstruction, with a public dataset supporting reproducibility and comparative evaluation.

Abstract: The use of digital pens for online handwriting trajectory reconstruction is a prevalent method for human-computer interaction. In this study, we focus on a digital pen equipped with sensors where we aim at reconstructing the online handwriting trajectory. This pen enables writing on any surface and preserving the digital trace of handwriting. This type of pen could be used as an aid to learning to write in classroom. In this paper, we propose a new approach learning to finely reconstruct the touching trajectories while precisely analyzing the hovering part in order to position the next touching trace correctly. This relies on a Mixture-Of-Experts (MOE) approach. The first expert is dedicated for the pencil touch, and is named touching expert model. The second one is dedicated for the hovering pen trajectory, and is named hovering expert model. We improve on the learning of each of these experts based on additional context or specific examples. In addition we introduce a novel public benchmark dataset, to enable future research and comparisons in the field of handwriting reconstruction. The results demonstrates a significant enhancement compared to its primary competitors.

</details>


### [196] [PowerAtlas: Towards Electricity-Computing Co-Scheduling for Power Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26710)
*Kaiwen Jiang,Siya Xu,Ziyue Zhu,Chao Yang,Anh Tuan Luu,Haoran Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PowerAtlas is an LLM-driven co-scheduling framework that jointly optimizes electricity and computing tasks under grid constraints, validated with real data and a new ECBench benchmark, showing feasible, cost-reducing solutions across multiple LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data centers create volatile, grid-sensitive loads; general-purpose LLM scheduling can violate grid limits. There is a need for coordinated electricity-computing scheduling that respects both SLAs and grid rules.

Method: Integrates historical instances, domain knowledge, and physical constraints into an LLM-agent framework. Builds a real electricity–computing testbed with a Chinese provincial utility and derives ECBench, a benchmark of 2,000 scheduling instances with oracle-optimal solutions. Evaluates 11 LLMs and 3 open-weight backbones on the real data with PowerAtlas.

Result: PowerAtlas yields feasible schedules and cost gains under realistic physical operating conditions, with consistent improvements across eleven LLMs and three backbones. The authors provide public code at the project repository.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted electricity–computing co-scheduling is viable and scalable under grid constraints; the ECBench benchmark enables standardized evaluation; real-world deployment requires integration with utility systems and consideration of regulatory and operational factors.

Abstract: The rapid growth of AI workloads is turning data centers into large-scale, volatile, yet spatiotemporally flexible grid loads, creating an urgent need for coordinated electricity-computing scheduling. Under stringent grid constraints, schedules from general-purpose large language models (LLMs) are often infeasible, causing line-flow violations and unserved load. We present PowerAtlas, an LLM-agent framework for electricity-computing co-scheduling that integrates historical instances, domain knowledge, and physical constraints to produce joint decisions satisfying both grid operational rules and the service-level agreements (SLAs) of computing tasks. Working with a provincial power utility in China, we built an experimental electricity-computing network and validated the decision loop on real data-center data; from de-identified operational data we further constructed ECBench, a benchmark of 2,000 scheduling instances with oracle-optimal solutions. Experiments across eleven LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of PowerAtlas under realistic physical operating conditions, with consistent feasibility and cost gains across three open-weight backbones. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/JAVA-Jiang/PowerAtlas.

</details>


### [197] [Domain adaptation for handwriting trajectory reconstruction from IMU sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26736)
*Florent Imbert,Romain Tavenard,Yann Soullard,Eric Anquetil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A digital pen with kinematic sensors captures handwriting on any surface. The study addresses age-related signal differences (adults vs. children) by applying domain adaptation to create a unified feature representation for trajectory reconstruction, comparing against training-from-scratch and fine-tuning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accurate handwriting trajectory reconstruction across populations (adults vs. children) despite differing sensor signals due to speed and confidence, enhancing educational use of digital pens.

Method: Propose a domain adaptation approach to learn a unified intermediate feature representation for trajectory reconstruction; compare with two baselines: training from scratch and fine-tuning on the target domain.

Result: Domain adaptation methods are shown to be advantageous for leveraging existing knowledge to apply handwriting trajectory reconstruction in new contexts, outperforming the baseline strategies in cross-domain applicability.

Conclusion: Domain adaptation is a promising strategy for cross-context handwriting analytics with digital pens and can facilitate educational applications by accommodating variability between user groups (e.g., children and adults).

Abstract: Digital pens are commonly used to write on digital devices, providing the handwriting trace and enhancing human-computer interation. This study focuses on a digital pen equipped with kinematic sensors, allowing users to write on any surface while simultaneously preserving a digital trajectory of handwriting. This technology holds significant potential as a valuable educational tool, particularly in classrooms where it can facilitate the process of learning to write. A major issue is based on the difference in captured signals between adults and children. For similar handwriting trace, we have large differences in sensor signals due to differences in speed and confidence in the handwriting gesture of children. To address this, we investigate a domain adaptation approach to build a unified intermediate feature representation aimed at facilitating the trajectory reconstruction. We demonstrate the interest of domain adaptation methods in leveraging existing knowledge for application in different contexts. Specifically, we compare our domain adaptation approach with two other methods: training the model from scratch and fine-tuning the model.

</details>


### [198] [CalTwin: Towards Calibrated, Shift-Robust Medical World Models via Fisher-Information Regularisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26752)
*Behraj Khan,Shabir Ahmad,Syed Ahmad Chan Bukhari,Tahir Qasim Syed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CalTwin unifies covariate-shift regularization and confidence calibration for GRU-based medical world models, reducing OOD latent-state prediction error and modestly improving calibration in a PhysioNet Sepsis setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two reliability failures in medical world models: covariate shift across institutions and confidence misalignment in multi-step forecasts, which limit deployment.

Method: Introduce CalTwin: a single objective combining Fisher Information-based shift penalty (adapted from fragmented covariate-shift remediation) and a Confidence Misalignment Penalty (adapted from calibrated vision-language classification). Applied to a GRU latent-transition predictor; derive the objective, identify transferable proof steps from prior work and required adaptations; train on PhysioNet 2019 Sepsis Challenge with two hospital systems as sequential training fragments; test on unseen system.

Result: CalTwin reduces next-step latent-state MSE under OOD by 9.1% vs baseline; FIM penalty alone yields 7.0% of that improvement; ECE reduction is 0.7% for CalTwin and 1.3% for CMP alone in this setting.

Conclusion: A unified, lightweight regularizer improves robustness of medical world models to covariate shift and miscalibrated confidence; the approach is effective in a sepsis forecasting context and offers a pathway for deployment with reduced risk of misprediction.

Abstract: Medical world models aim to learn a latent state of patient or organ physiology and a transition function that forecasts how that state evolves under interventions, supporting downstream tasks from imaging-based diagnosis to digital-twin treatment planning. Two failure modes threaten the reliability of such models in clinical deployment: (i)~\emph{covariate shift}, because training data are fragmented across hospitals, scanners, and time, so the feature distribution seen by the latent-dynamics predictor differs across fragments and from the distribution at deployment; and (ii)~\emph{confidence misalignment}, because multi-step forecasts are often overconfident exactly where clinical risk is highest. We argue that both problems admit a unified treatment via a single lightweight regularisation objective, \textbf{CalTwin}, which combines a Fisher-Information-based shift penalty adapted from our prior work on fragmented covariate-shift remediation~\cite{khan2025mitigating,khan2025causal} with a Confidence Misalignment Penalty adapted from our prior work on calibrated vision-language classification~\cite{khan2025confidence}, applied here to a GRU-based medical world model's latent transition predictor. We derive the combined objective, establish which proof steps transfer from the classification setting without modification and which require adaptation, and evaluate it on the PhysioNet 2019 Sepsis Challenge, treating the two hospital systems as sequential training fragments and the unseen system as an out-of-distribution test. CalTwin reduces OOD next-step latent-state MSE by 9.1\% relative to the no-penalty baseline (FIM penalty alone accounts for 7.0\%); the ECE reduction from the Confidence Misalignment Penalty is real but small (0.7\% for CalTwin, 1.3\% for CMP alone).

</details>


### [199] [Journey Operators for Structured Multi-Axis Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26775)
*Mahesh Godavarti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified geometric framework for multi-axis data where per-axis transformations govern composition and relative positioning; path-independence iff axis transforms commute; under symmetry and bilinear/norm constraints, pairwise scores reduce to block-wise rotations explaining RoPE-like methods; leads to JoFormer and links to attention/SSMs; preliminary cross-domain evidence suggests practical inductive biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To model data with structure along multiple axes where order matters within axes but not across axes. The aim is to define a path-operator that composes transformations per axis to describe data composition and relative positioning, unify RoPE and its variants, and learn data-dependent, content-adaptive positional biases.

Method: Define a path-connecting two positions with a product of per-axis transformations. Analyze when composition and movement are path-independent (commutativity of axis transforms). Prove under toral-frame symmetry, cocycle, bilinearity, and norm preservation that the induced pairwise scoring rule must be block-wise rotations. Use this theory to design JoFormer for value aggregation and relate it to attention and state-space models. Provide initial experiments across vision, language, and length generalization to test inductive biases.

Result: Path-independence holds exactly when axis transformations commute. Under the stated symmetry and algebraic assumptions, the scoring rule is forced to be block-wise rotations, which explains the natural emergence of RoPE-like methods. The framework yields JoFormer as a model for value aggregation and clarifies connections to attention and SSMs. Early experiments across multiple modalities indicate observable consequences of the proposed inductive biases in practice.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a principled basis for RoPE-like positional encodings, supports content-adaptive biases, and offers a concrete instantiation (JoFormer) with theoretical links to attention mechanisms and SSMs. Preliminary cross-domain experiments are encouraging, suggesting practical relevance of the multi-axis inductive biases.

Abstract: Many kinds of data have structure along one or more axes: words in a sentence, pixels in an image, nodes in a tree, frames in audio, or cells in a 3D volume. Along one axis, order matters: "the dog bit the man" is different from "the man bit the dog." Across independent axes, however, neither composition nor movement should depend on the order of axes: in an image, composing right then down should give the same result as composing down then right, and moving right then down should describe the same relative position as moving down then right.
  We develop a framework for modeling this kind of multi-axis structure. Each data item carries its content together with a small transformation for each axis. A path connecting two positions defines a journey; the journey operator is the product of per-axis transformations along that path, governing both how data composes along the path and how relative position is described. When the transformations are fixed, our framework recovers Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) and its multi-dimensional variants. When they depend on the data, the model gains a content-adaptive positional inductive bias.
  We show exactly when these paths are well-defined: both composition and movement across axes are path-independent precisely when the axis transformations commute. We also prove that, under the stated toral-frame symmetry, cocycle, bilinearity, and norm-preservation assumptions, the resulting pairwise scoring rule must take the form of block-wise rotations, explaining why RoPE-like methods arise naturally.
  Finally, we use this theory to design JoFormer, a model for value aggregation, and relate it to attention and state-space models (SSMs).
  Initial experiments across vision, language, and length generalization suggest that these inductive biases can have observable consequences in
  practice.

</details>


### [200] [SkillRise: Agentic Reinforcement Learning for Cross-Task Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26784)
*Zhiyuan Yao,Yuxin Chen,Zhengxi Lu,Zishan Xu,Yueqing Sun,Yifu Guo,Yuquan Lu,Zhengzhou Cai,Kangning Zhang,Zhuowen Han,Zi-Han Wang,Ziang Ye,Qi Gu,Xunliang Cai,Weiwen Liu,Yongliang Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SkillRise unifies RL across related tasks by solving tasks while curating a transferable skill document passed to the next task; a single policy handles solving and curation with decoupled credit assignment. It achieves strong Pass@1 gains across ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld and scales with longer task sequences, while reducing multi-stage pipeline overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tasks in LLM agent settings often share reusable solution patterns but are treated as independent episodes. SkillRise aims to capture and reuse these patterns by evolving a shared skill representation and applying it across a sequence of related tasks, improving sample efficiency and generalization.

Method: Create progressively challenging task sequences. Use a single policy that alternates between solving the current task and curating an evolving skill document. Decouple credit assignment: current-task outcomes supervise solving; discounted downstream outcomes supervise curation. Train across multiple domains (ALFWorld, WebShop, ScienceWorld).

Result: SkillRise achieves the strongest Pass@1 among baselines, with gains of 2.3–8.5 percentage points. The learned curation policy also helps repeated attempts on the same task. Test-time performance improves with longer sequences of related tasks, even when each task is attempted only once, indicating reusable transferable skills. The approach also reduces runtime overhead compared to multi-stage skill-learning pipelines.

Conclusion: SkillRise provides a simple, efficient training paradigm for extracting, refining, and reusing transferable skills across tasks, enabling better generalization and reduced infra costs for LLM agents.

Abstract: Large language model agents often encounter related yet distinct tasks that share reusable solution patterns. Yet standard agentic reinforcement learning treats tasks as independent episodes, while existing approaches to skill learning either focus on repeated attempts of one task or use pipelines with multiple stages that entangle extraction, retrieval, and execution. We introduce SkillRise, a unified reinforcement learning framework for learning skills across tasks. SkillRise organizes related instances into progressively challenging sequences and uses a single policy to alternate between task solving and curating an evolving skill document passed directly to the next task. Decoupled credit assignment across tasks supervises solving with the current task outcome and curation with discounted downstream outcomes. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld show that SkillRise achieves the strongest Pass@1 performance among the compared methods, with gains over the strongest baseline ranging from 2.3 to 8.5 percentage points. Although trained across distinct tasks, its learned curation policy remains effective for repeated attempts on the same task. Further analysis reveals scaling at test time across tasks: performance improves with longer sequences of related tasks even when each task is attempted only once. This trend suggests that SkillRise reuses transferable skills across tasks rather than benefiting from repeated sampling of the same task. SkillRise further retains strong performance while substantially reducing the runtime overhead of skill learning pipelines with multiple stages. Together, these results provide a simple and efficient training paradigm for LLM agents to extract, refine, and reuse transferable skills across tasks.

</details>


### [201] [FedTopo: Relation-Level Topology Sharing for Model-Heterogeneous Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26801)
*Zhaoyang Ma,Zhihao Wu,Xin Gao,Lipo Wang,Youfang Lin,Jing Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedTopo introduces a topology-based knowledge transfer mechanism for federated learning with heterogeneous backbones, encoding global knowledge as class relation topology rather than absolute feature representations, yielding robust improvements with low communication and no inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous local architectures create misaligned representation spaces, making cross-silo knowledge transfer unreliable when encoded in absolute spaces (parameters, predictions, prototypes). A backbone-agnostic representation is needed.

Method: Each client constructs a local class relation topology from prototypes and class statistics. The server aggregates these topologies using a reliability-aware weighting to down-weight poorly supported relations and broadcasts a global topology. Local training uses the global topology to emphasize topology-similar negative classes, reducing reliance on exact feature-space alignment. Communication remains low, and no extra inference overhead is introduced.

Result: Empirical evaluation on three datasets with eight heterogeneous backbones shows FedTopo outperforms parameter-sharing, distillation, and prototype-sharing baselines, with consistent gains and low communication costs, and without additional inference burden.

Conclusion: Encoding global knowledge as class relation topology provides a backbone-agnostic, reliable transfer mechanism in federated learning, effectively mitigating heterogeneity-induced misalignment and improving performance across diverse backbones with efficient communication.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative learning over decentralized data silos without centralizing raw data. However, heterogeneous local architectures often induce non-aligned representation spaces, making it difficult to transfer global knowledge across silos. Existing paradigms share this knowledge as model parameters, distilled predictions, or class prototypes, yet all encode it in an absolute space that must be aligned across clients. Heterogeneous backbones break this alignment, so the shared knowledge becomes unreliable and misleads local training. We propose FedTopo, a relation-level framework that encodes global knowledge as class relation topology, capturing how classes relate within each client rather than where they lie in feature space. Each client builds its relation topology from local prototypes and uploads it with class statistics. The server then aggregates these relations in a reliability-aware manner that down-weights weakly supported ones, and broadcasts the global topology to clients. The global topology guides local training by emphasizing topology-similar negative classes. Experiments on three datasets under eight heterogeneous backbones show that FedTopo consistently outperforms parameter-, distillation-, and prototype-sharing baselines, with low communication and no inference overhead. Our code is available at https://github.com/Zhaoyang-Ma/FedTopo.

</details>


### [202] [Forecasting Trajectory-Level Safety Risks in Black-Box Multi-Turn Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26820)
*Shi Lin,Peng Qian,Dinghao Liu,Renjie Sun,Sifan Wu,Dezhang Kong,Chenpei Wang,Xun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Recast, a trajectory-level safety risk forecasting framework for LLMs that predicts future safety violations by modeling how risk evolves over dialogue trajectories, using dual-scale evidence, a compositional risk dynamics model, and a causal temporal encoder; achieves 88.3% detection of future failures with 2.41-turn lead time and 12.3% false alarm across 7 risk categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Move beyond turn-level violation detection to long-horizon risk forecasting to enable proactive safeguards for autonomous LLM agents.

Method: 1) Dual-scale trajectory view: retrieve risk-relevant evidence from short-term dialogue progression and long-term historical context. 2) Compositional risk evolution: model current risk configuration and its temporal dynamics. 3) Causal temporal encoder: learn latent risk evolution patterns and predict the distribution of future risk emergence turns.

Result: Extensive experiments across 7 risk categories show Recast predicts 88.3% of future safety failures with an average lead time of 2.41 turns and a 12.3% false alarm rate.

Conclusion: Trajectory-level risk forecasting provides proactive safeguards for LLMs, demonstrating effectiveness across multiple risk categories; future work may focus on broader risk coverage, retrieval efficiency, and reducing false alarms.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) evolve from standalone assistants into autonomous agents, ensuring their safety requires shifting beyond pointwise risk assessment to understand how risks emerge and unfold over long-horizon trajectories. In multi-turn interactions, malicious intent can be decomposed across seemingly harmless turns and gradually reconstructed through interaction trajectories, eventually resulting in safety failures. Existing safeguards remain largely reactive, detecting manifested violations while lacking the ability to predict latent risk evolution and enable preemptive prevention. To address this limitation, we propose Recast, a safety risk forecasting framework that advances LLM safeguarding beyond turn-level violation detection to trajectory-level risk prediction. Recast first retrieves risk-relevant evidence from both short-term dialogue progression and long-term historical context via a dual-scale trajectory view. It then models compositional risk evolution by capturing the current risk configuration and its temporal dynamics. Finally, a causal temporal encoder learns latent risk evolution patterns and predicts the distribution of future risk emergence turns. Extensive experiments across 7 risk categories show that Recast predicts 88.3% of future safety failures with an average lead time of 2.41 turns, while maintaining a false alarm rate of 12.3%, showcasing the effectiveness of trajectory-level forecasting in identifying emerging risks before safety violations occur.

</details>


### [203] [Budget-Aware LLM Discovery via Cost-Calibrated Frontier Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26828)
*Yansen Zhang,Yilu Liu,Tianyu Liu,Jiamin Chen,Xiaokun Zhang,Kai Xie,Xue Liu,Chen Ma,Yiyan Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CostAda: a cost-calibrated adaptive controller for LLM frontier search that accounts for token costs, improving efficiency and final quality under fixed budgets across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompt costs (tokens, retries, and guidance calls) introduce non-uniform search costs; credit-based progress is misleading when exploration cost varies, leading to inefficient use of a fixed search budget.

Method: Define cost-calibrated frontier utility that measures frontier progress relative to realized action cost and conditions credit on remaining budget; use this signal to control local exploration, frontier allocation, and budgeted tactics; integrate into inference-time search and evaluate on multiple backbone pairs across GLM-5 and GPT-5.4.

Result: CostAda achieves the strongest baseline quality with at most half the budget on 12 of 16 backbone pairs and the strongest mean final quality on all eight benchmarks under GLM-5 and GPT-5.4.

Conclusion: Incorporating cost into frontier utility makes the search process cost-aware, enabling better allocation of limited token budgets and improved final quality without increasing total resources; cost becomes a shaping constraint rather than mere accounting.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly support scientific and algorithmic discovery through inference-time search over evaluated candidates. Existing adaptive discovery controllers assign credit based only on score progress, even though prompt length, retries, and guidance calls cause search actions to incur different token costs. We prove that cost-blind credit can forfeit all but a vanishing fraction of attainable quality as frontiers multiply and costs diverge. Under a fixed search-side token budget, the controller must decide which frontier is improving and whether its gain justifies the realized cost before the budget is exhausted. We introduce \textbf{CostAda}, a cost-calibrated adaptive controller built around \emph{cost-calibrated frontier utility}. The utility values frontier progress relative to realized action cost and conditions that credit on the remaining budget. CostAda uses this signal to control local exploration intensity, frontier allocation, and budgeted tactic intervention. Cost and remaining budget therefore shape the search rather than serving only as accounting variables or a stopping rule. CostAda reaches the strongest baseline's full-budget quality with at most half the budget on twelve of sixteen benchmark--backbone pairs while achieving the strongest mean final quality on all eight benchmarks under GLM-5 and GPT-5.4.

</details>


### [204] [Kairos: Numerically Robust News Recommendation under Item Cold-Start via Cholesky-based LinUCB](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26832)
*Finn Hertsch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kairos uses contextual online learning (LinUCB) with rank-1 Cholesky updates and Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) to enable efficient, latency-conscious news personalization in data-scarce, TTL-constrained regional markets; reports a 4.85x efficiency gain with Tagesschau API and preserved ranking accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data scarcity and latency in algorithmic news personalization due to short article TTLs and limited interaction data, which hampers collaborative filtering and robust modeling.

Method: Contextual online learning with LinUCB; direct rank-1 updates of Cholesky factors to maintain positive definiteness; Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) to reduce inference latency; empirical validation on Tagesschau API showing semantic redundancy exploitation yields efficiency gains.

Result: 4.85-fold efficiency improvement with minimal degradation in ranking precision; numerical stability under ill-conditioned data through direct Cholesky updates; scalable for resource- and data-constrained settings.

Conclusion: Kairos offers a blueprint for high-performance, resource- and data-constrained recommender systems in regional markets and TTL-limited news domains.

Abstract: Algorithmic news personalization in regional markets often fails because modern deep learning models require massive interaction data while real-world news has a short Time-to-Live (TTL < 48 h) and shallow article pools. This structural item cold-start deprives collaborative filtering of the data needed for robust modeling. This paper presents Project Kairos, a framework that bridges this data scarcity through a contextual online learning approach (LinUCB). To ensure numerical integrity for continuous operation, Kairos replaces error-prone Sherman-Morrison inversions with direct rank-1 updates of Cholesky factors. This preserves the positive definiteness of the covariance matrix even under ill-conditioned data scenarios. Simultaneously, Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) integration addresses inference latency. Empirical evaluations based on the Tagesschau API demonstrate that exploiting semantic redundancy in the feature space achieves a 4.85-fold efficiency gain without significantly compromising ranking precision. Kairos thus provides a blueprint for high-performance recommendation systems in resource- and data-constrained environments.

</details>


### [205] [Tight Generalization Bound for AdaBoost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26838)
*Mikael Møller Høgsgaard*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaBoost generalization error is Theta(d log(n γ^2 / d) /(n γ^2) + log(1/δ)/n); upper bound proven via a margin-based analysis of the voting classifier, with a matching lower bound from prior work.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide tighter, margin-aware generalization bounds for AdaBoost that depend on the weak learner's advantage γ, the VC-dimension d, the sample size n, and the confidence δ.

Method: Derive an upper bound by leveraging AdaBoost's voting classifier with zero empirical γ/2-margin loss and applying a new margin-based generalization bound for voting classifiers; use known lower bound to establish tightness.

Result: A margin-based generalization bound for AdaBoost: Θ(d log(n γ^2 / d) /(n γ^2) + log(1/δ)/n); the upper bound is established, matching a prior lower bound.

Conclusion: The paper provides a tight (up to constants) margin-based analysis for AdaBoost generalization, highlighting the role of margin, γ, and VC dimension in sample complexity; the approach could extend to other voting classifiers.

Abstract: In this paper we show that the generalization error of AdaBoost is $Θ\big(\tfrac{d\ln(nγ^{2}/d)}{nγ^2}+\tfrac{\ln(1/δ)}{n}\big)$, where $γ$ is the advantage guaranteed by the weak learner, $d$ is the VC-dimension of the class containing the weak hypotheses, $n$ is the sample size, and $δ$ is the confidence parameter. The contribution of this paper is the upper bound; the matching lower bound follows from prior work. The upper bound proof follows by combining the known fact that AdaBoost outputs a voting classifier whose voting function has zero empirical $γ/2$-margin loss with what is, to the best of our knowledge, a new margin-based generalization bound for voting classifiers.

</details>


### [206] [Thinking Under Uncertainty: Evidence Use and Information-Seeking in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26845)
*Hua-Dong Xiong,Xinyuan Yan,Ji-An Li,Jingming Xue,Marcelo G. Mattar,Robert C. Wilson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Thinking at inference time improves current evidence use in decision tasks but does not promote information-seeking exploration; patterns in thinking length and confidence align with metacognition without definitive evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To distinguish whether models use available evidence to improve decisions versus seeking information that could improve future decisions, and to identify signatures of exploration and metacognition under uncertainty.

Method: Ten open-weight models performed matched horizon-style two-armed bandit tasks in thinking and non-thinking modes. A cognitive model separated value-guided action from noise, and identified two exploration signatures (UCB-like bias toward less-known arm; Thompson-like variability increasing with total uncertainty). Measured action preference, thinking length, and reported confidence; manipulated uncertainty via matched vs information-imbalanced history; decoder sweeps (e.g., temperature) examined effects on outputs.

Result: Thinking strengthened value-guided action and reduced uncertainty-independent choice noise, with no emergence of UCB-like exploration or strengthened Thompson-like exploration. Thinking length increased under information-imbalanced history. Reported confidence became more sensitive to decision difficulty and more aligned with chosen-task evidence. Decoder manipulations affected noise and thinking length but did not recreate the joint cross-output pattern.

Conclusion: In this controlled setting, inference-time thinking improved action on current evidence without inducing a shift toward information-seeking policies; patterns in thinking length and confidence suggest metacognitive control and monitoring, though these processes are not conclusively established.

Abstract: Inference-time thinking improves the performance of large language models, but aggregate outcomes do not reveal whether models use available evidence more effectively or seek information that could improve future decisions. We distinguish these responses by measuring action preference, thinking length, and reported confidence under matched uncertainty. Ten open-weight models completed matched horizon-style two-armed bandit trials in thinking and non-thinking modes. A cognitive model separated value-guided action and uncertainty-independent choice noise from two behavioral signatures of exploration: a UCB-like preference for the less-known arm and Thompson-like choice variability that increases with total uncertainty. On average, thinking strengthened value-guided action and reduced uncertainty-independent choice noise, without producing UCB-like exploration or strengthening Thompson-like exploration. Outside action, the information-imbalanced history condition, which also displayed more observations than the matched balanced condition, was associated with greater thinking length. Reported confidence became more sensitive to decision difficulty and more strongly associated with chosen task evidence. We interpret these thinking-length and reported-confidence patterns as consistent with metacognitive control and metacognitive monitoring, respectively, without establishing either process. Decoder sweeps, especially temperature, altered choice noise and thinking length but did not reproduce the joint cross-output pattern. In this controlled decision setting, thinking improved how models acted on current evidence, while neither measured signature supported a shift toward a more information-seeking policy.

</details>


### [207] [TREA-Net: A Transferable Residual Epidemiological Adaptation Network for Dengue Incidence Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26854)
*Inesh Shukla,Madhurima Panja,Tanujit Chakraborty,Chittaranjan Hens*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TREA-Net: a transferable neural-epidemiological adaptation framework for dengue forecasting with limited target data, combining environmental TSIR projections with a light gated residual, enabling cross-country transfer (Colombia/Nicaragua -> Mexico/Malaysia) and improved accuracy; supports node-invariant designs and uses two global target parameters; with TiRex it yields best results and conformal prediction improves interval efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data scarcity and non-stationarity in emerging surveillance systems limit reliable neural forecasting; cross-domain pretrained models may miss local epidemiological dynamics; need transferable adaptation with minimal target data.

Method: Proposes TREA-Net: augment neural backbones with Environmental Time-Series Susceptible-Infected-Recovered projections; learn a lightweight gated residual correction transferable from data-rich to data-scarce regions; node-invariant design for varying location counts; target adaptation requires learning only two global parameters; transfer from long-running dengue surveillance in Colombia and Nicaragua to 8-week-ahead forecasting in Mexico and Malaysia using 78 or 104 weeks of target data; evaluation across five neural backbones and ten transfer settings; integration with TiRex; conformal prediction.

Result: Improves backbone performance in 9 out of 10 transfer settings with statistical significance; with TiRex, achieves the lowest mean absolute error across all target datasets; conformal prediction maintains empirical coverage while reducing 8-week prediction-interval width by 29.6% in Mexico.

Conclusion: Demonstrates TREA-Net as a lightweight, portable early-warning framework for health agencies with limited surveillance data, enabling cross-region dengue forecasting with calibrated uncertainty.

Abstract: Accurate multi-week dengue forecasting supports timely vector-control interventions, outbreak preparedness, and healthcare resource allocation. However, newly established surveillance systems often lack the historical data needed to train reliable neural forecasting models. Although pretrained time-series models offer promising zero-shot forecasts, their cross-domain training may not capture local epidemiological dynamics. We propose TREA-Net, a Transferable Residual Epidemiological Adaptation Network for dengue forecasting under limited data. TREA-Net augments neural forecasting backbones with projections from an Environmental Time-Series Susceptible-Infected-Recovered model and learns a lightweight gated residual correction transferable from data-rich to data-scarce regions. Its node-invariant design accommodates surveillance systems with different numbers of locations, while target adaptation requires learning only two global parameters. We transfer knowledge from long-running dengue surveillance in Colombia and Nicaragua to 8-week-ahead forecasting in Mexico and Malaysia using only 78 or 104 weeks of target data. Across five neural backbones and ten transfer settings, TREA-Net improves the corresponding backbone in 9 out of 10 settings, with statistically significant gains. When integrated with TiRex, a foundation model for forecasting, it achieves the lowest mean absolute error across all target datasets. Conformal prediction further maintains empirical coverage while reducing 8-week prediction-interval width by 29.6% in Mexico. These results demonstrate TREA-Net's potential as a lightweight and portable early-warning framework for health agencies with limited surveillance data.

</details>


### [208] [Amortized Moment Matching for Visual Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26860)
*Wenze Liu,Xintao Wang,Pengfei Wan,Xiangyu Yue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Amortized moment matching learns data moments with neural networks to guide diffusion denoisers, introduces AMFD loss via polynomial projections, enabling dynamic conditional moment learning and scalable, matrix-free optimization; yields robust training dynamics and strong generation performance (ImageNet one-step, GenEval), scalable to text-to-image and instruction-following.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the challenge of explicit marginal moment calculations in distribution matching for diffusion models. Leverage neural networks to learn data moments and conditional moments, enabling scalable, flexible moment matching in high-dimensional spaces.

Method: Cast diffusion denoisers through polynomial projections; show that an n-th degree projection identifies data moments up to order n+1. Derive the Amortized Fréchet Distance (AMFD) loss from a tractable affine setting. Use an alternating, matrix-free optimization to learn conditional moments, enabling a post-training objective on global representations and a flexible generative-space exploration.

Result: AMFD achieves stronger performance than FD-baselines on FDr^6 and ImageNet one-step generation. It enables direct exploration in native generative spaces, with the first two moments sufficing in semantically rich spaces. In text-to-image tasks, condition-aware AMFD improves instruction-following, outperforming 4B FLUX teachers on GenEval and matching PickScore.

Conclusion: AMFD provides a scalable, neuralized framework for moment matching that improves training dynamics and generation quality over explicit FD-based methods, with broad applicability from image to text-to-image generation; code is available.

Abstract: We propose amortized moment matching, utilizing neural networks to learn data moments as distributional training signals. By casting diffusion denoisers through polynomial projections, we establish a general framework for moment amortization, revealing that an $n$-th degree projection explicitly identifies data moments up to order $n+1$. Derived from the tractable affine case, we instantiate the Amortized Fréchet Distance (AMFD) loss. Unlike FD-loss which relies on explicit marginal moment calculations, AMFD is able to dynamically learn conditional moments via an alternating, matrix-free optimization pipeline that effortlessly scales to high-dimensional data. When operating on global representation features, AMFD serves as a powerful post-training objective; empirically, its neural formulation yields more robust training dynamics than exact statistical matching, substantially surpassing the FD baseline on the FDr$^6$ metric and achieving superior one-step generation on ImageNet. Furthermore, it unlocks direct exploration within native generative spaces, suggesting that the first two moments can identify target distributions only in spaces with strong semantics. Finally, when scaled to text-to-image generation, the condition-aware nature of AMFD unlocks massive gains in instruction-following capabilities, enabling our one-step models to outperform their multi-step FLUX.2 [klein] 4B teachers on the GenEval benchmark while achieving on-par performance on PickScore. Code and checkpoints are available at https://github.com/poppuppy/amfd.

</details>


### [209] [ReCo: Reweighting GRPO Against Distributional Concentration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26862)
*Junoh Park,Junseo Hwang,Wonguk Cho,Taesup Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCo reweights GRPO updates to counteract the bias toward high-probability responses and tokens, improving large-k Pass@k on math reasoning benchmarks while keeping small-k performance close to GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO reduces reasoning coverage by concentrating updates on responses the base model already favors and by amplifying high-probability tokens, limiting exploration of alternative reasoning paths.

Method: Introduce ReCo: a reweighting scheme that (1) normalizes response contributions by their expected occurrence in the rollout group, and (2) uses a variance-based token-level ratio instead of the standard importance ratio to amplify updates at non-saturated decision points.

Result: Across Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B/7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on five math benchmarks, ReCo yields improved Pass@k for large k and remains comparable to GRPO for small k.

Conclusion: ReCo mitigates GRPO’s coverage collapse by encouraging exploration of diverse reasoning paths, achieving better large-k performance without sacrificing small-k behavior.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has become a standard reinforcement learning method for post-training language models. Recent work shows that GRPO can reduce the base model's reasoning capacity and underperform it in Pass@k when k is large, indicating reduced coverage of reasoning paths. We find that this reduction is associated with GRPO concentrating on responses that the base model already generates with high probability. We trace this concentration to two mechanisms in the GRPO update. At the response level, high-probability responses dominate the group gradient through repeated occurrence. At the token level, GRPO's importance ratio scales gradients, further reinforcing tokens that become more likely under the current policy. We propose ReCo, a reweighting method that addresses both effects. Response contributions are normalized by their expected occurrence within the rollout group, and the token-level importance ratio is replaced with a variance-based ratio that gives larger update scale to non-saturated decision points where alternative token choices remain plausible. Across Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B/7B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, ReCo improves Pass@k for large values of k and is comparable to GRPO for small values of k.

</details>


### [210] [Actions Have Consequences: Detecting Outcome Performativity using Intervention Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26908)
*Brandon Gower-Winter,Georg Krempl*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OPAB detects Outcome Performativity by comparing outcome distributions across prediction-driven interventions; provides sample complexity bounds; validated on Open Bandits dataset; identifies indistinguishability regions; practical implications for scarce data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prediction-driven outcomes occur in many domains; need robust methods to detect performativity, assess data requirements and ethical constraints.

Method: Formalize a prediction-intervention framework; quantify distributional dissimilarity between outcomes under different prediction groups; derive sample complexity bounds for various performativity classes; empirical validation; case study.

Result: OPAB detection is feasible in many settings; significant dissimilarity indicates performativity; indistinguishability regions exist where required interventions are insufficient; practical implications for limited data scenarios; case study confirms efficacy on Open Bandits dataset.

Conclusion: OPAB provides a viable detection tool for Outcome Performativity with clear sample-efficiency considerations; future work to refine bounds, examine additional domains, address ethical constraints, and extend to more datasets.

Abstract: In many domains such as Palliative Care, Credit Assignment and Recommender Systems, predictions may causally influence the outcomes they predict. This phenomena is known as Outcome Performativity. This paper formalises an approach for detecting Outcome Performativity using prediction intervention called Outcome Performativity A/B Detection (OPAB). OPAB enables the detection of Outcome Performativity by assessing the dissimilarity in outcome distributions produced by different predictions groups (interventions). If that dissimilarity is significant, Outcome Performativity is detected. We derive sample complexity bounds for OPAB under various Outcome Performative assumption classes which we empirically validate. Results show that detecting Outcome Performativity using OPAB is achievable in numerous cases. Results also show the presence of regions of indistinguishability which describe settings where the allotted number of interventions are insufficient for detecting Outcome Performativity. The results of which have broader practical implications for the detectability of Outcome Performativity in settings where samples are scarce, cost-prohibitive or potentially unethical to obtain. The paper concludes with a case study on the efficacy of OPAB on the Open Bandits dataset, and provides directions for future work.

</details>


### [211] [Two Calls Beat Five Agents: Evaluating Multi-Agent Pipelines Against Self-Refinement for Local Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26922)
*Ashish Prajapati,Om Mohite*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM pipelines on a 7B local model (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct) show limited gains and even declines depending on data format and task; a plaintext input often mitigates JSON error accumulation, and a task-aware reform can preserve accuracy; simpler approaches can rival or outperform complex multi-agent systems for small-model deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether structured multi-agent pipelines provide benefits for small local LLMs and under what conditions the gains hold, focusing on data formats, self-refinement strategies, and task-aware redesign.

Method: Deploy Parishad (five roles) on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct; evaluate on GSM8K (500 Qs) and HumanEval (164 Qs); compare with direct prompting and two-call self-refinement (V1, V2); analyze JSON vs plaintext data formats; measure accuracy and token usage.

Result: JSON data format caused error accumulation, dropping GSM8K accuracy from 75.0% to 45.0%; plaintext format restored accuracy to 82.0%. Two-call self-refinement V1 yielded 86.2% on GSM8K with 7.4x fewer tokens, but reduced HumanEval accuracy from 96.3% to 66.5%. A task-aware V2 design on HumanEval preserved 95.1% accuracy. Overall, communication format and implementation details outweighed architectural complexity; simpler methods can match or exceed multi-agent pipelines for local 7B models.

Conclusion: Format and design choices dominate performance in small-model multi-agent systems; deploying simpler, well-tuned approaches may outperform more complex multi-agent pipelines for local 7B LLMs; code and data released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM pipeline systems break down the task among multiple roles for better reasoning, but are benchmarked mainly with large-scale commercial models. In this study, we investigate Parishad, a structured multi-agent system involving five roles, by deploying it on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, a local model, on two datasets: GSM8K (500 questions) and HumanEval (164 questions), compared with prompting directly and two-call self-refinement. The multi-agent system drops GSM8K accuracy from 75.0\% to 45.0\% with JSON data format due to the error accumulation problem. With plaintext format, the accuracy is restored to 82.0\%. A two-call self-refinement strategy (V1) can achieve 86.2\% accuracy on GSM8K, with 7.4$\times$ lower token usage. However, the same V1 implementation on HumanEval---where direct accuracy is already 96.3\%---actively destroys performance (66.5\%). A task-aware gated redesign (V2) applied to HumanEval preserves accuracy at 95.1\%. Our results demonstrate that communication format and implementation details determine outcomes more than architectural complexity, and that simpler approaches match or outperform multi-agent pipelines for local 7B model deployment. All code and data are released.

</details>


### [212] [Surrogate assisted diversity estimation in neural ensemble search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26940)
*Alexandr Udeneev,Petr Babkin,Oleg Bakhteev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dual-objective surrogate-guided NES jointly optimizes architecture and ensemble diversity via two DAG-based surrogates, yielding competitive ensembles on standard datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NAS is computationally expensive and extending to NES causes an exponential search space; a efficient method is needed to produce accurate and diverse ensembles without prohibitive cost.

Method: Represent candidate architectures as DAGs. Train two independent surrogate models to estimate per-architecture accuracy and diversity potential. Use combined estimates to guide NES, selecting architectures that are strong individually and diverse collectively.

Result: The resulting ensemble achieves competitive or superior performance compared with Deep Ensembles and Random Search on FashionMNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100.

Conclusion: A dual-objective surrogate-guided NES can efficiently identify strong and diverse ensembles, mitigating the computational burden of joint NAS/NES and delivering robust performance.

Abstract: Ensembles are a standard way to improve the performance and robustness of deep neural networks, but their effectiveness crucially depends on both the quality and the diversity of individual models. Most neural architecture search (NAS) methods are computationally expensive. Extending them to neural ensemble search (NES), which requires joint optimization of individual architectures and their ensemble composition, leads to an exponential growth of the search space and makes the problem computationally intractable. To address this, we introduce a dual-objective surrogate-guided ensemble search: candidate architectures are represented as directed acyclic graphs, and two surrogate models are trained independently to estimate predictive accuracy and diversity potential. Their combined estimates guide an NES framework that efficiently identifies architectures that are both individually strong and collectively diverse. Our final ensemble achieves competitive or superior performance compared to standard baselines such as Deep Ensembles and Random Search on FashionMNIST, CIFAR-10, and CIFAR-100.

</details>


### [213] [Foundation Models for Face Presentation Attack Detection: A Unified Linear-Probing Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26993)
*Peter Lorenz,Anjith George,Sébastien Marcel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Frozen foundation-model representations provide strong intra-dataset PAD performance with a linear probe but do not reliably generalize cross-dataset; model scale helps inconsistently across architectures; InternViT-6B excels intra-dataset while CLIP ViT-B/32 offers the best cross-dataset-transfer–compute trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether general-purpose vision foundation models encode PAD-relevant information that can be exploited with minimal task-specific training, addressing data scarcity and cross-dataset domain shifts.

Method: Systematic evaluation of 24 frozen encoders (self-supervised ViTs, vision-language encoders, and supervised CNNs) using a unified linear-probing protocol on the MCIO PAD benchmark (MSU-MFSD, CASIA-FASD, Replay-Attack, OULU-NPU); backbone fixed, train only a lightweight linear head to isolate existing PAD cues in pretrained representations.

Result: Strong intra-dataset PAD performance with linear heads across frozen encoders; cross-dataset transfer is unreliable; larger models help within several families but not monotonically; InternViT-6B achieves the lowest mean intra-dataset error; CLIP ViT-B/32 provides the most favorable cross-dataset transfer–compute trade-off among examined probes.

Conclusion: Pretrained representations contain PAD-relevant information, but domain shift requires explicit adaptation; linear probing on frozen encoders suffices for in-domain performance yet is insufficient for cross-domain generalization, highlighting the need for task-specific adaptation or fine-tuning to address dataset shift.

Abstract: Face presentation attack detection (PAD) remains challenging under cross-dataset evaluation, where domain shift degrades models trained on a single dataset. The scarcity of large-scale labeled data motivates adapting pretrained vision models rather than training task-specific architectures from scratch, raising a fundamental question: do general-purpose vision foundation models encode PAD-relevant information accessible with minimal task-specific training? To investigate, we systematically evaluate 24 frozen encoders, including self-supervised vision transformers, vision-language encoders, and supervised CNNs, using a unified linear-probing protocol on the MCIO benchmark (MSU-MFSD, CASIA-FASD, Replay-Attack, OULU-NPU). The backbone remains fixed, and only a lightweight linear head is trained to isolate the PAD information already present in the pretrained representation. %We report intra- and cross-dataset performance, along with accuracy-compute trade-offs, relative to two specialist PAD baselines. Results show that frozen foundation-model representations can support strong intra-dataset PAD performance with only a linear classifier, but this performance does not reliably transfer across datasets. Model scale is beneficial within several families, although the effect is not monotonic and is strongly mediated by architecture and pretraining. InternViT-6B achieves the lowest mean intra-dataset error, whereas CLIP ViT-B/32 offers the most favorable cross-dataset transfer-compute trade-off among the evaluated probes. These findings suggest that while pretrained representations contain PAD-relevant information, explicit adaptation remains necessary to address domain shift.

</details>


### [214] [BayesAME: Bayesian Active Model Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27023)
*Paula Cordero Encinar,Taylan Cemgil,Arnaud Doucet,Virginia Aglietti,Silvia Chiappa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BayesAME is a Bayesian, sequential framework that automatically determines the coreset size needed to estimate full benchmark performance for large generative models. It uses latent item-group abilities with priors from historical models, updates with Bayesian inference, and selects items via an information-gain criterion, iterating until predefined stability and uncertainty thresholds. It extends to multiple target models and benefits from non-random coreset selection and continuous log-likelihoods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating large generative models on full benchmarks is computationally expensive; when reliable performance estimation is priority, automatically adapt the coreset size to reflect this priority rather than rely on a fixed, user-specified size.

Method: Models performance as a latent ability per item-group, with a joint prior encoding similarity to historical models. Bayesian posterior over abilities yields estimators and uncertainty. Items are chosen to maximize information gain; the coreset is expanded iteratively until fluctuation and uncertainty fall below user-defined thresholds. Includes a multi-target extension to capture cross-model correlations between multiple target models.

Result: Empirical results show BayesAME consistently outperforms sequential baselines across diverse benchmarks. Non-random (informed) coreset selection is advantageous. Continuous response log-likelihoods outperform binary scores, improving estimation accuracy.

Conclusion: BayesAME provides automatic, uncertainty-aware coreset sizing and item selection for efficient and accurate benchmark estimation of large generative models, with demonstrated gains over existing methods and robust handling across multiple target models.

Abstract: Evaluating large generative models across benchmarks is time-consuming and computationally expensive. This drives the need for methods that can estimate full benchmark performance by evaluating models on only a subset of items, known as a coreset. Current literature mostly requires the practitioner to input a coreset size. However, when reliable performance estimation takes priority over efficiency, an evaluation method should also be capable of automatically determining a coreset size that reflects this priority. We introduce BayesAME, a sequential Bayesian framework specifically targeting automatic determination of the coreset size. BayesAME models performance as a random variable by defining a latent ability for each group of items sharing the same historical model performances, with a joint prior distribution encoding the belief that the target model behaves similarly to these historical models. The posterior distribution over these abilities is used to derive performance estimators, quantify performance uncertainty, and select items to add to the coreset via an information-gain criterion. The coreset is iteratively augmented until the performance estimate fluctuation and the performance uncertainty fall below their respective user-defined thresholds. We propose a multi-target extension that captures performance correlations across multiple target models to further reduce the coreset size. Through extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks, we demonstrate that BayesAME consistently outperforms sequential adaptations of existing methods. Crucially, our comprehensive analysis addresses recent skepticism in the literature, establishing that non-random coreset selection is advantageous over random selection. Finally, we highlight that leveraging continuous response log-likelihoods over traditional binary scores significantly enhances estimation accuracy.

</details>


### [215] [TreeCCA: Canonical Correlation Analysis via Gradient-Boosted Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27027)
*James Chapman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TreeCCA combines gradient-boosted trees with an Eckart–Young loss to train end-to-end CCA encoders, delivering nonlinear cross-view correlation with native interpretability and strong tabular-data performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CCA traditionally uses linear or neural encoders; there is a need for nonlinear, interpretable cross-view representations that can be trained in a plug-and-play way within standard GBT libraries for tabular data.

Method: Introduce the Eckart–Young loss to obtain closed-form per-sample gradients, enabling a custom objective that can be plugged into XGBoost/LightGBM. Each tree split uses a single feature, yielding natural interpretability via gain importances for cross-view drivers. The approach is extended to multi-view settings (TreeMCCA).

Result: Empirically, TreeCCA matches or outperforms Deep CCA on synthetic benchmarks (Signed Power, Hermite). It excels in a sparse setting by recovering the true support with Precision@S=1.00 at p=50. On UCI HAR, it achieves comparable accuracy to Deep CCA at about 5× lower cost, with gain importances confirming a physics-driven hypothesis. Across five tabular multi-view datasets, TreeMCCA consistently matches or exceeds linear CCA in nonlinear correlation extraction and downstream classification.

Conclusion: TreeCCA provides a practical, end-to-end, interpretable CCA encoder for tabular data by leveraging gradient-boosted trees with an EY-based objective, yielding competitive accuracy, efficiency, and interpretability relative to neural encoders.

Abstract: Gradient-boosted trees dominate tabular machine learning, yet canonical correlation analysis has always relied on linear or neural encoders. We propose \textbf{TreeCCA}, the first method to train gradient-boosted tree ensembles end-to-end as CCA encoders, inheriting their plug-and-play reliability: no architecture design, familiar hyperparameters, and strong performance with defaults. The technical enabler is the Eckart-Young (EY) loss, which supplies closed-form per-sample gradients that slot directly into any standard GBT library (XGBoost, LightGBM) as a custom objective.
  TreeCCA is the first CCA method to combine nonlinear accuracy with native interpretability: every tree split selects one feature, so gain importances reveal which inputs drive cross-view correlation at no extra cost. We demonstrate these properties on synthetic benchmarks, where TreeCCA matches or exceeds Deep CCA (2.61 vs.\ 2.43 on Signed Power; 2.93 vs.\ 2.89 on Hermite), and on a sparse benchmark with zero linear cross-view covariance, where TreeCCA recovers the true support with $\text{Precision@}S = 1.00$ at $p=50$ while PMD finds no signal. On the UCI HAR sensor-fusion benchmark, TreeCCA achieves comparable accuracy to Deep CCA at $5\times$ lower cost, while XGBoost gain importances directly validate a physics-motivated hypothesis about the data --- an interpretation not readily available with neural encoders. Across five popular tabular multi-view datasets, TreeMCCA consistently matches or exceeds linear CCA in both nonlinear correlation extraction and downstream classification accuracy.

</details>


### [216] [Lottery Tickets Are Not Deployment Tickets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27031)
*Bum Jun Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper questions whether accuracy-equivalent sparse models (e.g., lottery tickets) can replace a dense incumbent without changing downstream decision logic. It finds that exact accuracy parity does not imply behavioral compatibility under fixed deployment conditions, with 7–10% decision churn in fixed-threshold settings and limited reliability improvements from sparsity; clean-accuracy certification alone is insufficient for safe drop-in replacement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess real-world deployment viability of sparse model replacements by evaluating their effect on deployment-relevant behaviors when downstream logic is fixed, addressing a gap where prior work reports mixed effects of sparsification.

Method: Audit a broad, protocol-specific set of deployment-relevant behaviors (calibration, out-of-distribution response, class-level reliability, representations, downstream policy decisions) for accuracy-matched sparse candidates vs. dense incumbents. Introduce a behavioral-compatibility distance to quantify deviations beyond clean accuracy. Evaluate under fixed-threshold policy diagnostics across multiple study settings.

Result: Sparse candidates can match dense-accuracy but exhibit different behavior. Lottery tickets show lower corruption accuracy in some bands. In small-gap, fixed-threshold setups, replacement changes 7–10% of accept–review decisions, creating downstream reconfiguration churn. These results illustrate limits of clean-accuracy certification and show that compatibility with a fixed incumbent is distinct from merely achieving high accuracy.

Conclusion: Compatibility with a fixed incumbent cannot be inferred from clean accuracy alone. Small confidence shifts near decision boundaries can drive first-order routing churn, even with exact top-1 agreement. The study provides a framework (behavioral-compatibility distance) to assess real deployment viability and highlights the need to consider deployment conditions when evaluating sparsified models for replacement.

Abstract: Reports on how sparsification, compression, and lottery tickets change model behavior have been mixed in the prior literature, with beneficial effects observed in some studies and adverse effects in others. Moreover, prior work has not considered actual deployment conditions, where decision logic is already fixed for the incumbent. To assess these mixed findings from a practical standpoint, we study the production-replacement question at the deployment level, namely whether an accuracy-matched lottery ticket or another sparse challenger can replace an incumbent dense model without reconfiguring downstream decision logic. We therefore audit a broad, protocol-specific panel of deployment-relevant behaviors spanning calibration, OOD response, class-level reliability, representations, and downstream policy decisions, and summarize clean-accuracy-excluded deviations with a behavioral-compatibility distance. Across extensive experiments, sparse candidates repeatedly recover dense-reference accuracy yet remain behaviorally different; in several study-band-matched settings, LTs also show lower corruption accuracy. In small-gap settings with fixed-threshold policy diagnostics, lottery-ticket replacement changes 7% to 10% of accept--review decisions. This churn creates precisely the burden that drop-in replacement is meant to avoid: reconfiguring and revalidating downstream decision logic. These findings establish the limits of clean-accuracy certification: Establishing compatibility with a fixed incumbent is distinct from attributing churn uniquely to sparsity or treating every measured deviation as harmful. Our theory explains the routing result: Even exact pointwise top-1 agreement cannot bound fixed-threshold decision changes, and small confidence shifts near the operating boundary can generate first-order routing churn.

</details>


### [217] [CoCaRS: Correlation Calibration-Based Redundancy Suppression for Heterogeneous Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27054)
*Fengming Yu,Haiwei Pan,Kejia Zhang,Chunling Chen,Jian Guan,Baoying Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoCaRS enhances heterogeneous knowledge distillation by calibrating redundancy suppression through Correlation Calibration (CEE) and Strength Allocation Control (SAC), with Adaptive Coefficient Regulation (ACR) to maintain structure and reduce sensitivity to coefficient settings across teacher–student pairs and training stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large architectural differences between teacher and student cause representation gaps that hinder direct knowledge transfer. Uniform decorrelation can erase useful structure, and fixed suppression coefficients are brittle across models and training phases.

Method: Propose CoCaRS with (1) Confusion Evidence Estimation (CEE) to identify reliable semantic relations for correlation estimation, (2) Strength Allocation Control (SAC) to preserve discriminative structure during decorrelation, and (3) Adaptive Coefficient Regulation (ACR) to scale the suppression term by relative loss, reducing coefficient sensitivity.

Result: Experiments on CIFAR-100 and ImageNet-1K show improved distillation performance and reduced sensitivity to coefficient settings. Code will be released soon.

Conclusion: CoCaRS effectively retains structural information while suppressing redundancy in heterogeneous KD and demonstrates robustness to hyperparameter choices across teacher–student pairs and stages.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation (KD) enables a compact student model to learn from a powerful teacher and has become an effective paradigm for model compression. The emergence of diverse model architectures has extended KD from homogeneous to heterogeneous settings. However, differences in architectural inductive biases between the teacher and student models often result in substantial representation discrepancies, limiting the effectiveness of direct knowledge transfer. Recently, redundancy suppression has offered a new perspective on heterogeneous KD by preserving cross-architecture invariance and reducing feature redundancy through decorrelation of teacher-student feature correlations. Nevertheless, this formulation may weaken useful structural information through uniform decorrelation, while a fixed coefficient may make the effective contribution of redundancy suppression sensitive to teacher-student pairs and training stages. To address these problems, Correlation Calibration-based Redundancy Suppression (CoCaRS) is proposed to better retain structural information while suppressing redundancy and reduce sensitivity to coefficient settings across teacher-student pairs and training stages. Specifically, CoCaRS calibrates feature decorrelation through Confusion Evidence Estimation (CEE) and Strength Allocation Control (SAC), which respectively capture reliable semantic relations for correlation estimation and preserve discriminative structure during decorrelation. Adaptive Coefficient Regulation (ACR) further regulates the contribution of the calibrated redundancy suppression objective according to its relative loss scale, reducing sensitivity to coefficient settings. Extensive experiments on CIFAR-100 and ImageNet-1K validate the effectiveness of CoCaRS in improving distillation performance and reducing sensitivity to coefficient settings. Code will be released soon.

</details>


### [218] [Parameter-Free Dynamic Regret for Online Convex Optimization under Heavy-Tailed Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27073)
*Vaneet Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces HT-PAder, a parameter-free OCO algorithm for non-stationary environments with heavy-tailed noise (finite p-th moment). It achieves universal dynamic regret without prior knowledge of problem parameters, using restarted AdaGrad experts over a geometric pool of block lengths plus a pathwise meta-algorithm AdaGrad-Hedge, with a matching lower bound establishing optimal path-length dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-stationary online convex optimization with heavy-tailed noise; existing results often require knowledge of problem parameters or stronger (sub-Gaussian) noise assumptions. There is a demand for parameter-free, universal dynamic regret guarantees that adapt to unknown path length of the comparator and unknown problem constants.

Method: HT-PAder combines restarted AdaGrad experts arranged over a geometric grid of block lengths, controlled by a pathwise meta-algorithm AdaGrad-Hedge that aggregates meta-losses without assuming moment conditions. The approach yields a parameter-free, universal dynamic regret bound that adapts to the comparator path length P_T and the noise level without requiring prior knowledge of D, G, σ, or P_T.

Result: For domain diameter D, Lipschitz constant G, noise level σ, and comparator path length P_T, HT-PAder achieves an expected universal dynamic regret of 
\tilde{O}\left( G D \sqrt{T\left(1+\frac{P_T}{D}\right)} + σ D T^{1/p}\left(1+\frac{P_T}{D}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}} \right). The bound holds universally with respect to problem parameters and covers p ∈ (1,2], including p=2 as a special case. A matching lower bound is proven, establishing optimality of the path-length exponent.

Conclusion: The work delivers the first parameter-free minimax universal dynamic regret guarantee for p=2 and extends to heavy-tailed noise with finite p-th moment (p≤2). It confirms the optimality of the path-length dependence and provides a practical, parameter-free method for non-stationary OCO under realistic noise assumptions.

Abstract: We study online convex optimization (OCO) in non-stationary environments under heavy-tailed noise, where the stochastic gradient oracle admits only a finite $p$-th central moment for some $p \in (1, 2]$. While static regret is well-understood, achieving universal dynamic regret in a parameter-free manner remains an open challenge. We resolve this by proposing \textbf{HT-PAder}, a parameter-free algorithm combining restarted AdaGrad experts over a geometric pool of block lengths with a pathwise meta-algorithm, \textbf{AdaGrad-Hedge}, which requires no moment conditions on meta-losses. For a domain of diameter $D$, Lipschitz constant $G$, noise level $σ$, and comparator path length $P_T$, HT-PAder achieves an expected universal dynamic regret of \[ \widetilde O\left( GD\sqrt{T(1+P_T/D)} + σD T^{1/p}(1+P_T/D)^{(p-1)/p} \right). \] The algorithm does not require prior knowledge of any of these problem parameters. Even in the special case of finite variance ($p=2$), HT-PAder provides the first parameter-free minimax universal dynamic regret guarantee. We also prove a matching lower bound, establishing the optimality of the path-length exponent.

</details>


### [219] [Single-Beat Cuffless Blood Pressure Estimation Using Ear-PPG and ECG with a Lightweight Hybrid Learning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27076)
*Kindeep K. Dhatt,Tengyue Wu,Hanbang Hua,Yayun Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Beat-wise, lightweight multi-modal BP estimation using ECG, ear PPG, and IMUs with a 1D CNN embedding and LightGBM; achieves MAE ~4.02/1.79 mmHg and 28% MAE reduction vs baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address cuffless BP estimation challenges under motion and physiological variability; avoid multi-second windows; preserve beat-level information for wearable deployment.

Method: Synchronized chest ECG and ear-clip reflectance PPG with 6-axis IMUs; 1D CNN extracts a 64-dimensional embedding from individual PPG beats; fusion with 30 physiology-grounded features (including PTT statistics and HRV); LightGBM regression. Evaluated on a multi-phase stress protocol (n=10) and PulseDB with subject-disjoint validation; 30 independent runs.

Result: MAE_Sys = 4.02 ± 0.21 mmHg; MAE_Dia = 1.79 ± 0.05 mmHg; 28.2% reduction in combined MAE relative to baseline models.

Conclusion: Beat-wise estimation without long temporal context enables computationally efficient cuffless BP monitoring suitable for wearable deployment under practical resource constraints; source code available at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: Continuous cuffless blood pressure (BP) monitoring remains challenging due to motion artifacts, physiological variability, and the limited robustness of conventional pulse transit time (PTT) models under dynamic conditions. Many prior approaches rely on multi-second windows to stabilize estimation, an assumption that is frequently violated during real-world monitoring with intermittent signal corruption. Here, we show that discriminative BP-related information is preserved at the single-beat level and present a lightweight multi-modal wearable framework for continuous BP estimation.
