The system integrates synchronized chest electrocardiography (ECG) and ear-clip reflectance photoplethysmography, each co-located with a 6-axis inertial measurement unit to provide motion context. We introduce a hybrid learning architecture in which a one-dimensional convolutional neural network extracts a 64-dimensional embedding from individual PPG beats and fuses it with 30 physiology-grounded features, including PTT statistics and heart rate variability, followed by LightGBM regression. The method was evaluated using a multi-phase stress protocol ($n=10$) and the PulseDB public dataset with subject-disjoint validation. Across 30 independent runs, the model achieved mean absolute errors of $4.02 \pm 0.21$~mmHg for systolic BP and $1.79 \pm 0.05$~mmHg for diastolic BP, corresponding to a 28.2\% reduction in combined MAE relative to baseline models. By enabling beat-wise estimation without long temporal context, this framework supports computationally efficient cuffless BP monitoring suitable for wearable deployment under practical resource constraints. The source code for this work is available at https://github.com/SYMBIOX-Lab/BP-wireless.

</details>


### [220] [Equilibrium Training of Energy-Based Models with Parallel Trajectory Tempering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27077)
*Nicolas Béreux,Aurélien Decelle,Cyril Furtlehner,Beatriz Seoane*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PTT enables stable, efficient equilibrium training of EBMs, improving performance on multimodal and data-scarce datasets and yielding practical log-likelihood estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EBMs offer interpretable generative models but suffer from poor MCMC mixing; a training algorithm that preserves equilibrium sampling during learning could improve reliability, sample quality, and log-likelihood estimates.

Method: Parallel Trajectory Tempering (PTT) that leverages optimization-path continuity to maintain equilibrium sampling; augmented with reservoir sampling and adaptive optimization; computationally comparable to Persistent Contrastive Divergence; provides thermalization times and equilibrium samples.

Result: PTT outperforms existing EBM training methods on Restricted Boltzmann Machines; on discrete tabular data, it surpasses state-of-the-art deep generative models in sample quality and robustness to limited data.

Conclusion: Equilibrium maximum-likelihood training of EBMs becomes practical and computationally efficient with PTT, offering direct thermalization estimates and high-quality samples at modest cost.

Abstract: Energy-Based Models (EBMs) provide an interpretable framework for generative modeling of scientific data, but poor Markov Chain Monte Carlo mixing often limits their reliability. We introduce a training algorithm based on Parallel Trajectory Tempering (PTT), which exploits the continuity of the optimization path to maintain equilibrium sampling throughout learning. This enables stable and fast training on highly multimodal and data-scarce scientific datasets. Combined with reservoir sampling and adaptive optimization, PTT has a computational cost comparable to Persistent Contrastive Divergence, making it a practical replacement for standard training methods. It also provides direct estimates of thermalization times, equilibrium samples from trained models, and accurate log-likelihoods at essentially no additional cost. Experiments on Restricted Boltzmann Machines show that PTT consistently outperforms existing EBM training approaches. On discrete tabular data, it also surpasses state-of-the-art deep generative models, yielding higher-quality samples and greater robustness to overfitting and limited data. Our results make equilibrium maximum-likelihood training of EBMs practical and computationally efficient.

</details>


### [221] [Scores Are Not Decisions: Cost-Aware Stopping for Tool Acquisition in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27083)
*Yicheng Feng,Yan Zhang,Yan Cheng,Wei Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cost-aware marginal decision-focused stopping method (CAM-DF) selects how many external tools an LLM agent should acquire, balancing information gain against heterogeneous costs. A compact CAM-DF-lite variant is introduced; training is done on the offline gap between stopping now and the best continuation, with Bayes-aligned objectives and evidence that score-only rules are suboptimal under cost heterogeneity. Evaluations show state-of-the-art performance across diverse tool domains, with substantial reductions in tool usage in live execution while maintaining task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents rely on diverse external tools, there is a fundamental trade-off: using too few tools leads to under-information, while using too many increases cost, context load, and privacy exposure. Existing rankings do not address optimal stopping under heterogeneous tool acquisition costs; there is a need for a principled, cost-aware stopping criterion.

Method: Formulate tool acquisition as cost-aware marginal decision-focused stopping (CAM-DF) over ranked tool prefixes. Introduce CAM-DF-lite as a compact interpretable variant. Train directly on the offline gap between stopping now and the best continuation, using sign labels for the decision and magnitude weights for the payoff at stake. Prove the objective is Bayes-aligned with the stopping target; demonstrate that score-only rules are suboptimal under heterogeneous costs. Evaluate on 1,343 tasks across five tool-use domains, comparing against multiple ranking sources and cost regimes. CAM-DF acts as a lightweight pre-execution plugin that converts existing tool rankings into lower-cost acquisition decisions without LLM fine-tuning.

Result: CAM-DF achieves the highest payoff among deployable methods on τ-bench Retail, with gains over a predict-then-threshold baseline across all five ranking sources and two cost regimes. It is state-of-the-art under heterogeneous costs and high cost pressure, with larger gains when rankings are weaker. In live execution, CAM-DF reduces tool access by 37% while maintaining comparable task success. The CAM-DF family functions as a lightweight pre-execution plugin.

Conclusion: The CAM-DF family provides a practical, low-overhead solution for cost-aware tool acquisition in LLM agents. It successfully converts tool rankings into efficient stopping decisions without requiring LLM fine-tuning, delivering strong performance across diverse domains and cost conditions.

Abstract: As LLM agents increasingly depend on diverse external services such as search engines, databases, and connectors, agent harnesses face a fundamental tool-selection challenge: acquiring too few tools leaves the task under-informed, while too many adds cost, context load, and privacy exposure. Routers and retrievers can rank candidate tools by relevance, but a ranking alone does not determine how many are worth selecting. Existing approaches leave acquisition under heterogeneous costs unaddressed. We formulate this decision as cost-aware marginal decision-focused stopping (CAM-DF) over ranked tool prefixes, with CAM-DF-lite as a compact interpretable variant. We train directly on the offline gap between stopping now and the best continuation: its sign labels the decision, its magnitude weights each error by the payoff at stake. We prove this objective is Bayes-aligned with the stopping target and that score-only rules are suboptimal under heterogeneous costs. We evaluate on 1,343 tasks across five tool-use domains. On $τ$-bench Retail, CAM-DF attains the highest payoff among deployable methods, with gains over a predict-then-threshold baseline across all five ranking sources and two cost regimes. Our approach is state-of-the-art under heterogeneous costs and high cost pressure, with larger gains under weaker rankings. In live execution, CAM-DF exposes the agent to 37\% fewer tools than full access while maintaining comparable task success. The CAM-DF family is a lightweight pre-execution plugin that turns existing tool rankings into lower-cost acquisition decisions without fine-tuning the underlying LLM.

</details>


### [222] [Sky sphere representation in language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27092)
*Aleksandr Berdnikov,Yevgeny Liokumovich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Open-source ~100B language models encode a decodable, curved, high-dimensional night-sky feature representation in their residual streams; LOO tests show substantial variance explained (65–85%) with median angular error 12–21° on prompts connecting objects to night-sky neighbors; not a simple leak from flat representations; authors present the first example of such a curved irreducible manifold and publish code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether large language models store and surface domain-relevant, geometrically structured representations (here, a night-sky map) in their internal activations, and characterize the geometry of these representations.

Method: Analyze the residual stream of several ~100B open-source LMs. Use principal component analysis to identify dominant components, test decodability with leave-one-out (LOO) regressions, evaluate variance explained (R^2) and predictive angular error on prompts about proximity in the night sky, and verify against potential flat-leakage by perturbation controls; compare across models; confirm the representation is a curved, high-dimensional manifold.

Result: Most models exhibit a decodable night-sky representation in the residual stream. It emerges as top principal components on relevant prompts and yields significant LOO scores, explaining 65–85% of variance and achieving median angular errors of 12°–21°. The representation is not a simple leak from a flat correlated feature space. The authors claim this as the first example of a curved high-dimensional irreducible feature manifold. Code is released.

Conclusion: The findings indicate that large-scale language models encode nontrivial, geometrically structured latent representations for spatial-domain knowledge in their hidden states. The representation’s curvature and irreducibility suggest a robust, intrinsic latent structure, not reducible to flat correlations. This has implications for understanding how LMs store domain knowledge and for interpretability studies; replication and extension across architectures and tasks are warranted.

Abstract: We analyze whether language models of size ~100B have a representation of the night sky map that is decodable from their residual stream. We find that most of the considered open-source models do have such a representation, and it often even surfaces to the top principal components on prompts that ask questions like ``what is close to this object in the night sky''. In all but one model this representation showed significant scores in LOO testing, containing up to 65-85% of variance ($R^2$-score) and having median angular error down to $12^\circ-21^\circ$. We verify that our representation is not a simple leak from a correlated flat representation. To our knowledge, this representation is the first example of a curved high-dimensional irreducible feature manifold.
  Codes used in the paper are published at https://github.com/l3erdnik/Decodable-sky

</details>


### [223] [Hierarchical Spatio-Temporal Transformer for Coherent Emergency Department Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27106)
*Filipa Lino,Bárbara Tavares,Carlos Santiago,Cláudia Soares,Manuel Marques*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HierSTT is a hierarchical Transformer-based framework for coherent multi-level emergency department demand forecasting (hospital, regional, national). It uses a national Temporal Fusion Transformer and region/hospital spatio-temporal encoder-decoders conditioned on higher-level forecasts, plus a coherence-aware loss. It is validated on a nationwide Portuguese ED dataset (81 hospitals, 5 regions) and reports a 32% WAPE improvement over the best non-hierarchical DL baseline and superior performance vs classical reconciliation methods, with near-coherent cross-level predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting ED demand requires consistent, multi-level forecasts that align hospital, regional, and national aggregates for staffing, bed management, and capacity planning. Existing work often forecasts each level independently, causing incoherence across levels and suboptimal decision support.

Method: A hierarchical Transformer architecture (HierSTT) combining a national Temporal Fusion Transformer with slotted spatio-temporal encoder-decoder modules for regional and hospital levels. Higher-level forecasts condition lower-level models. Training optimizes a coherence-aware loss to penalize cross-level inconsistencies. The model uses heterogeneous covariates per level and is trained end-to-end. A real-world Portuguese ED dataset (81 hospitals, 5 regional administrations) is used.

Result: HierSTT reduces average WAPE by 32% relative to the best non-hierarchical deep-learning baseline and outperforms classical hierarchical reconciliation methods, while producing near-coherent predictions across levels.

Conclusion: HierSTT demonstrates that end-to-end hierarchical forecasting with coherence-aware training improves accuracy and coherence for multi-level ED demand, enabling better staffing, bed management, and capacity planning. Validated on a large real-world dataset, the approach shows promise for wider adoption and adaptation to other health systems.

Abstract: Emergency Departments (EDs) are critical access points in healthcare systems, yet they face persistent pressure from unpredictable patient demand, seasonal surges, and non-urgent visits. Effective ED planning requires forecasts at multiple decision-making levels: hospitals need local demand estimates for staffing and bed management, regions require forecasts to coordinate healthcare units, and national authorities need system-wide projections for capacity planning. However, most existing approaches forecast ED demand independently at a single level, ignoring the hierarchy linking hospitals, regions, and national systems. This can produce incoherent predictions, where hospital-level forecasts do not aggregate consistently to regional or national demand. We propose HierSTT, a hierarchical Transformer-based framework for coherent multi-level ED forecasting. HierSTT jointly predicts hospital, regional, and national level demand in a single end-to-end model. A Temporal Fusion Transformer captures national dynamics, while spatio-temporal Transformer encoder-decoder modules model regional and hospital demand conditioned on higher-level forecasts. A coherence-aware loss penalizes cross-level inconsistencies during training. We further introduce a nationwide Portuguese ED dataset covering 81 hospitals across 5 regional health administrations, with heterogeneous covariates at each level. Experiments show that HierSTT reduces average WAPE by 32\% relative to the best non-hierarchical deep learning baseline and outperforms all classical hierarchical reconciliation methods, while producing near-coherent predictions across levels. Additional resources associated with this work are available at https://github.com/FilipaLino/HierSTT.

</details>


### [224] [Voronoi Histograms for Adaptive Vectorization of Expected Persistence Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27126)
*Kaifeng Zhang,Kai Ming Ting*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Voronoi diagram-based histogram vectorization for Expected Persistence Diagrams (EPD): an adaptive, partition-based discretization that foregoes explicit smoothing, with stability guarantees and conditions for preserving Wasserstein-scale variation; validated on real datasets for classification and dimensionality reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EPD accelerates PD-based topological analysis by sampling topological features across subsets, but existing vectorizations rely on predefined smooth transformations (e.g., Gaussian, landscape). A discretization that adapts to data via Voronoi partitions may offer computational efficiency and flexibility.

Method: Construct a Voronoi diagram over a subset of the ambient space; compute histograms of EPD features by counting features falling into Voronoi cells, forming a vectorization without an explicit smooth feature function. Derive stability bounds under stated separation and normalization conditions and characterize when the histogram preserves Wasserstein-scale variation. Validate on real-world datasets with meaningful topological structure for classification and dimensionality reduction.

Result: The paper provides theoretical stability guarantees for the Voronoi-histogram EPD representation and identifies conditions under which the representation preserves Wasserstein-scale variation. Empirical results on real datasets show the approach effectively captures topological information for classification and dimensionality reduction tasks.

Conclusion: Voronoi diagram-based histograms offer a viable, adaptive vectorization of EPD that avoids fixed smooth transformations, provides theoretical guarantees, and demonstrates practical utility in topology-driven learning tasks.

Abstract: Persistence Diagram (PD) is known to capture point cloud topology effectively, but its computation has high time complexity. Expected Persistence Diagram (EPD) has been developed to reduce the time cost by studying the topology of multiple subsets of a point cloud and it serves as a distribution of topological features. Existing EPD vectorizations often rely on predefined point transformations, such as Gaussian or landscape functions. We study an alternative discretization based on Voronoi histograms, which trades smooth functional approximation for adaptive partition-based counting. We propose to use Voronoi Diagram-based histogram as the vectorization of EPD, without imposing an explicit smooth point transformation model. Under stated separation and normalization conditions, we establish stability bounds and characterize when the histogram representation preserves Wasserstein-scale variation. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed representation on real-world datasets which have significant topological features for classification and dimensionality reduction tasks.

</details>


### [225] [Minimal Markovization via Stable Quotients in Holonomy-Cover Decision Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27132)
*Zuyuan Zhang,Yongshan Chen,Mahdi Imani,Tian Lan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces the stable quotient as the minimal observation-wise abstraction for holonomy-cover POMDPs, yielding an exact finite Markov state when paired with the current observation; enables Holonomy Memory RL with provable memory-optimal properties and practical performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Partial observability in sequential decision making requires a recursively updateable statistic that restores the Markov property. For holonomy-cover POMDPs, the smallest such statistic is unknown; the work seeks a principled, memory-efficient representation.

Method: Define the stable quotient as the coarsest observation-wise abstraction that preserves one-step rewards and quotient successors. Prove that (current observation, stable class) forms an exact finite Markov state. Establish optimal memory bounds under reachability and pairwise decision separation. Introduce resettable diagnostics and nearest-prototype inference to enable accurate class estimation, and a calibrate-then-restart reduction to transfer finite-MDP guarantees. Propose Holonomy Memory Reinforcement Learning, where memory is the stable class updated via ordered edge transports, with local coordinates identified when diagnostics permit, followed by a standard finite-MDP RL backbone after synchronization.

Result: The current class, when initialized correctly, attains exact minimal memory symbols needed for exact class tracking. Under resettable diagnostics, nearest-prototype inference yields exponentially decaying error. Empirically, exact compression from raw states to quotient states is recovered, achieving perfect paired-order accuracy with three decision-time memory states, matching the quotient oracle and outperforming non-oracle baselines.

Conclusion: The framework delivers a principled, provably memory-efficient RL approach for holonomy-cover POMDPs. It provides exact state representation via the stable quotient, supports scalable RL through a finite-MDP backbone, and demonstrates practical gains in compression accuracy and decision performance. This enables the broader adoption of Holonomy Memory RL in structured POMDP settings.

Abstract: An agent acting under partial observability must retain a recursively updateable statistic of history that restores the Markov property, but the smallest such statistic is generally unknown. We characterize this minimal Markov sufficient statistic for holonomy-cover decision processes, a structured POMDP class in which the visible dynamics are Markov and every realized visible transition applies a fixed permutation to a hidden mode. In particular, we construct the stable quotient, the coarsest observation-wise abstraction preserving one-step rewards and quotient successors, and prove that the pair of the current observation and stable class forms an exact finite Markov state. When the current class is correctly initialized, exact class tracking requires exactly the minimal memory symbols, in the sense that under reachability and pairwise decision separation at a maximizing observation, no arbitrary finite-memory controller can use fewer. Under resettable diagnostics, nearest-prototype class inference has exponentially decaying error, and a calibrate-then-restart reduction transfers finite-MDP guarantees to the recovered state. The results enable \emph{Holonomy Memory Reinforcement Learning}. It represents memory by the current stable class, updates it through ordered edge transports, identifies local class coordinates when diagnostics are available, and applies a standard finite-MDP RL backbone after synchronization. Experiments recover an exact compression from raw states to quotient states and achieve perfect paired-order accuracy with three decision-time memory states, matching the quotient oracle and outperforming the non-oracle baselines.

</details>


### [226] [Cost-Sensitive Conformal Prediction and Human-in-the-Loop Abstention for Imbalanced High-Stakes Decision Support: A Multi-Domain Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27143)
*Manpreet Singh,Akshatha Srikantha,Shyamal Lakhanpal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mondrian (class-conditional) CP dramatically improves minority-class coverage in imbalanced settings and, with cost-controlled abstention, lowers expected decision costs; validated by a large-scale benchmark across diverse datasets and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard marginal CP under-covers rare, costly minority classes in high-stakes domains; need distribution-free, cost-aware uncertainty quantification with reliable minority guarantees.

Method: Benchmark across 15 real-world imbalanced tabular datasets, 7 models, 3 probability calibration methods, 10 seeds; compare marginal CP, Mondrian CP, and cost-controlled abstention; evaluate minority coverage, cost metrics, and break-even thresholds; 3,150 runs.

Result: Mondrian CP yields valid minority-class coverage with an average improvement of 61.7 percentage points over marginal CP (p < 1e-80). Combining Mondrian CP with cost-controlled abstention reduces expected decision costs more than standard decision boundaries and various rejectors; dataset-specific break-even thresholds for human deferral are quantified.

Conclusion: Provides practical, distribution-free, cost-aware uncertainty quantification guidance for high-stakes decision support; Mondrian CP plus abstention offers robust minority coverage and cost savings in imbalanced tasks.

Abstract: High-stakes decision systems in credit scoring, fraud detection, healthcare, and industrial safety require reliable uncertainty quantification under severe class imbalance and asymmetric error costs. Standard marginal conformal prediction (CP) provides valid overall coverage guarantees; however, we show that it severely under-covers rare, costly minority classes, with minority-class coverage dropping to as low as 0.5% on certain datasets. To characterize and address this limitation, we conduct a comprehensive benchmark comparing marginal CP, class-conditional (Mondrian) CP, and cost-controlled abstention mechanisms across 15 real-world imbalanced tabular datasets, 7 classification models, 3 probability calibration techniques, and 10 random seeds, resulting in 3,150 experimental runs. Our results show that Mondrian CP restores valid minority-class coverage, achieving an average minority-coverage improvement of 61.7 percentage points over marginal CP (p < 1e-80). Furthermore, combining Mondrian CP with cost-controlled abstention significantly reduces expected decision cost compared with standard decision boundaries, confidence-based rejectors, and risk-controlled rejectors under realistic human review budgets. We further quantify dataset-specific break-even thresholds at which deferring ambiguous instances to human experts becomes cost-effective. These findings provide practical guidance for deploying distribution-free, cost-aware uncertainty quantification in high-stakes decision support systems.

</details>


### [227] [Skillful forecasting of offshore winds from satellite scatterometer constellations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27152)
*Francesco Pinto,Luca Lanzilao,Paco Lopez Dekker,Angela Meyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A satellite-based nowcasting framework for offshore wind (WindCastNet) uses a partial convolutional LSTM to forecast wind fields from irregular, multi-constellation scatterometer data, achieving notable short-term accuracy gains over NWP and persistence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate intraday offshore wind forecasts are essential for power system operation and renewable integration. NWP is not optimized for minutes-to-hours lead times, and satellite scatterometer data have not been directly used for forecasting.

Method: WindCastNet employs a partial convolutional long short-term memory network that ingests microwave radar observations from European, Chinese, and Indian scatterometers, handling irregular spatial coverage, asynchronous sampling, and variable revisit times. Spatial observation masks and inter-observation intervals are encoded; a continuous temporal representation enables forecasts at arbitrary lead times.

Result: In the North Sea evaluation, WindCastNet reduces RMSE by 23% at 1 h and 7% at 2 h relative to the HARMONIE MEPS model, and outperforms persistence by 9–15% during the first three forecast hours. Forecast skill declines under strong-wind conditions and spatially non-uniform flow.

Conclusion: Satellite scatterometer constellations can provide an independent, competitive source of short-term offshore wind forecasts, enabling improved renewable-energy forecasting and broader marine weather applications, including tropical cyclone nowcasting.

Abstract: Accurate intraday forecasts of offshore wind are becoming increasingly important for power system operation and the integration of growing shares of offshore wind energy. Operational forecasts rely predominantly on numerical weather prediction (NWP), which is not optimized for lead times of minutes to hours, where initial-condition accuracy dominates forecast skill. Although satellite scatterometer observations are routinely assimilated into NWP, they have not previously been used directly for forecasting. Here we present WindCastNet, the first satellite-based nowcasting framework for offshore wind speed and direction, introducing a new paradigm for intraday forecasting that learns from spatiotemporally irregular satellite observations. WindCastNet predicts offshore wind fields from observations acquired by satellite scatterometer constellations. WindCastNet employs a partial convolutional long short-term memory network that exploits microwave radar observations from the European, Chinese, and Indian scatterometers despite their irregular spatial coverage, asynchronous sampling, and variable revisit times. Spatial observation masks and inter-observation intervals are encoded, while a continuous temporal representation enables forecasts at arbitrary lead times. Evaluated over the North Sea, WindCastNet reduces the root-mean-square error by 23% and 7% relative to the HARMONIE MEPS model at lead times of 1 and 2 h, respectively, and outperforms persistence by 9-15% during the first three forecast hours. Forecast skill decreases under strong-wind conditions and spatially non-uniform flow. These results demonstrate that satellite scatterometer constellations can provide an independent and competitive source of short-term offshore wind forecasts, opening new opportunities for renewable energy forecasting but also broader marine weather applications, including tropical cyclone nowcasting.

</details>


### [228] [When Do Learned Diffusion Proposals Help Constraint Solving? A Controlled Study on Continuous Algebraic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27169)
*Quang Bui,Sparsh Roy,Akash Gundimeda,Davin Yin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph-based learned proposal framework for continuous algebraic constraint solving (MARC) shows regime-limited gains over random multi-start; learned proposals can help but primarily in restricted, high-dimensional uncoupled regimes; in many real-world cases, classical multi-start suffices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-decision nature of solving continuous algebraic constraints and the inefficiency of augmentation selection; aim to guide solver design with data-driven proposals.

Method: MARC converts the problem to a factor graph; a graph-neural diffusion denoiser proposes assignments; a descent on an exact computer-algebra energy refines them; an exact symbolic checker certifies solutions; introduces a candidate-conditioned repair ranker selecting among K augmentations; compares against budget-matched random multi-start; evaluates on synthetic regimes and eight real-world systems.

Result: The repair-ranker approach nearly reaches exhaustive-search performance with far fewer calls (e.g., 0.997 vs 0.236 accuracy for random, p<1e-70; 0.982±0.006 across seeds); learning offers improvements but mostly in specific regimes. Across trapped low-dimensional families it ties with random restart; dominates in high dimension where random search fails; once variables couple, the advantage vanishes. Best-of-K random multi-start has success probability 1−(1−q(n))^K, reproducing the curve with a single constant; eight real-world systems are solved by classical multi-start; learning gains are not universal and map regime boundaries.

Conclusion: Learned proposals improve solvers only in restricted regimes; a robust combination of randomized multi-start, polish, and symbolic checking remains strong; this work provides a regime map indicating where learning is beneficial.

Abstract: Solving a continuous algebraic constraint system requires two decisions: which values satisfy the constraints, and which structural augmentation renders an unsolvable system solvable. Classical solvers answer the first well and the second only by enumeration. On that discrete decision, a candidate-conditioned repair ranker choosing among K augmentations reaches the exhaustive-search ceiling at a fraction of the calls, outperforming random (0.997 vs 0.236 balanced nonlinear menu accuracy; p < 10^-70; 0.982 +/- 0.006 across seeds) and beating a budget-matched per-candidate probe on accuracy and cost. MARC turns such a system into a factor graph, over which a graph-neural diffusion denoiser proposes assignments, descent on an exact computer-algebra energy polishes them, and an exact symbolic checker certifies solutions. Evaluations of diffusion-based proposals rarely include one control: random multi-start under the same refinement budget. Applied to our system, it sharply curtails what the learned proposal contributes on the value decision. Does it beat random multi-start at choosing satisfying assignments? Only narrowly, in a predictable regime. Across trapped low-dimensional families it ties with random restart, but dominates in high dimension, where random search fails. Once variables couple, the advantage is gone. Since all methods share one polish and one checker, best-of-K random multi-start succeeds with probability exactly 1 - (1 - q(n))^K, where q(n) is single-start reachability; one measured constant, with no free parameters, reproduces the entire curve (mean absolute error 0.012). The favorable regime is not specific to our synthetic families: across eight real-world systems in robotics, positioning, optimization, and algebra, classical multi-start solved all eight, none in the learning-favorable regime. We map the regimes in which learned proposals improve solvers.

</details>


### [229] [Inverse Learning of Latent Risk-Neutral Densities from Irregular Option Quotes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27188)
*Lennon J. Shikhman,Michael Galarnyk,Aadi Dash,Nicholas A. Welsh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Pricing accuracy does not guarantee latent risk-neutral density accuracy; benchmarks reveal trade-offs and target-dependent inductive biases rather than a universal winner.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenge the assumption that price fitting implies accurate density recovery in option markets; quantify this gap using controlled and historical benchmarks.

Method: Two benchmarks: (i) controlled benchmark exposing simulator-truth latent densities for evaluation; (ii) chronological NIFTY benchmark with only held-out market prices. Models: a two-component lognormal mixture; DeepONet (neural operator); quote transformer; Merton-family mis-specification for structural tests; numerical conditioning analysis enforcing mass and forward constraints; hold-out evaluation on 524 NIFTY calls with test-time adaptation.

Result: Two-component lognormal mixture yields the lowest aggregate price errors (L1, Wasserstein, fixed-tail). DeepONet reduces 1% quantile and variance errors by 39.0% and 34.6% relative to the mixture; quote transformer reduces L1 by 16.4% under Merton misspecification. Conditioning analysis shows 95 of 126 pricing directions are numerically null; densities separated by L1=0.061 can produce identical prices on covered strikes. On held-out NIFTY calls, adaptation lowers DeepONet RMSE by 28.3%; per-expiry mixture and SVI remain more accurate. Overall, evidence supports target-dependent inductive bias rather than a universal winner.

Conclusion: Price accuracy and latent-density recoverability are decoupled; model performance is task-dependent. Practitioners should align model choice with objective (density recovery vs price accuracy) and incorporate domain constraints (mass/forward) to avoid ill-posed density identifiability issues.

Abstract: Accurate option prices do not imply accurate recovery of the latent risk-neutral density. We study this distinction with two complementary benchmarks. A controlled benchmark exposes simulator-truth densities for latent evaluation, while a chronological NIFTY benchmark tests only held-out market prices. A two-component lognormal mixture has the lowest aggregate price, $L^1$, Wasserstein, and fixed-tail errors on the synthetic benchmark. Learned operators retain narrower strengths: DeepONet reduces 1% quantile and variance error by 39.0% and 34.6% relative to the mixture, and a quote transformer reduces $L^1$ by 16.4% on the structurally misspecified Merton family. A numerical conditioning analysis explains why these rankings can differ: after enforcing mass and forward constraints, 95 of 126 pricing directions are numerically null, and two densities separated by $L^1 = 0.061$ produce identical prices on the covered strikes. On 524 held-out NIFTY calls, validation-selected test-time adaptation reduces DeepONet RMSE by 28.3%, but per-expiry mixture and SVI fits remain much more accurate. The evidence supports target-dependent inductive bias, not a universal winner.

</details>


### [230] [From Classification to Regression: Using a Fruitfly to Solve Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27196)
*Shady E. Ahmed,Panos Stinis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A local-pattern, similarity-based regression framework inspired by fruitfly sensing that replaces global surrogates with a finite library of patterns, enabling offline training and online lightweight inference with tunable accuracy/storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of global surrogate models in nonlinear regression by exploiting recurring local input-space regions and efficient similarity-based reconstruction, inspired by biological sensing mechanisms.

Method: Build a finite library of representative local patterns. For a query, measure similarities to stored patterns and combine their responses via weighted reconstruction. An offline phase extracts patterns from data or governing equations; the online phase uses only similarity evaluations and aggregation. Applicable with suitable embeddings and similarity measures.

Result: Demonstrates applicability to nonlinear dynamical systems, data-driven regression, and physics-informed learning. The approach reduces computational and memory demands and provides explicit control over the trade-off among accuracy, storage, and inference cost.

Conclusion: A modular, scalable framework that blends classification-inspired pattern catalogs with regression, enabling efficient online inference in resource-constrained settings and tunable accuracy/storage characteristics through embeddings and similarity metrics.

Abstract: We present a novel approach to regression tasks using classification which is motivated by the mechanism used by fruitflies to sense their environment. Specifically, we formulate a general framework for learning nonlinear input-output relationships by replacing complex global surrogate models with a finite library of representative local patterns. Since scientific data often occupy limited and recurring regions of the input space, we generate predictions by measuring similarities between a query and stored patterns, then combining their associated responses through weighted reconstruction. We apply this approach to nonlinear dynamical systems, data-driven regression, and physics-informed learning using suitable embeddings and similarity measures. For dynamical systems, our offline-online workflow extracts patterns from data or governing equations during the offline phase, while online prediction requires only similarity evaluation and response aggregation. This structure helps us reduce computational and memory demands while providing explicit control over the trade-off among accuracy, storage, and inference cost.

</details>


### [231] [Do You Really Need to Pretrain Q-Functions for Online RL Fine-Tuning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27203)
*Perry Dong,Ron Polonsky,Dorsa Sadigh,Chelsea Fin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Q-function pretraining offers limited benefit for fine-tuning a pretrained policy due to mismatch between offline Q targets and online fine-tuning Q-function; introduces Initialization via Policy Ensemble (IPE) which improves fine-tuning by leveraging diverse policy rollouts; observed average 1.26x improvement on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether offline Q-function pretraining is useful when fine-tuning a pretrained policy; address the observed success of online RL with randomly initialized Q-functions and the unclear value of Q-pretraining.

Method: Systematic empirical study across continuous control tasks to assess Q-function pretraining; analyze the source of any benefits or failures; identify mismatch between pretrained Q targets and online finetuning; propose IPE; evaluate on standard benchmarks.

Result: Naive Q-function pretraining provides little benefit over random initialization; mismatch between offline-pretrained Q and online-target Q persists even after offline value maximization; Initialization via Policy Ensemble (IPE) yields ~1.26x improvement in fine-tuning performance over naive Q-pretraining.

Conclusion: Pretraining the Q-function is not universally beneficial for fine-tuning a pretrained base policy; the key mismatch explains the lack of gains; a simple alternative, Initialization via Policy Ensemble (IPE), using diverse policy rollouts to bootstrap Q-learning, improves performance and should be preferred in such settings.

Abstract: Pre-training followed by fine-tuning has become the dominant recipe for learning performant policies, and in value-based reinforcement learning (RL) this raises a natural question: given a pretrained policy, should the Q-function be pretrained on offline data too? Conventional wisdom suggests it should, but recent results show that online RL with a randomly-initialized Q-function can result in highly performant and reliable policies without needing to pretrain the Q-function. In this paper, we systematically study whether pretraining the Q-function actually helps when fine-tuning on top of a pretrained base policy. We find, surprisingly, that naive Q-function pretraining often provides little benefit over random initialization. We show this stems from a fundamental mismatch: the Q-function learned during pretraining targets the pretrained policy's Q-function, not the Q-function that online fine-tuning converges to, and this gap persists even after offline value maximization. Motivated by this finding, we propose Initialization via Policy Ensemble (IPE), a simple method that trains multiple diverse policies and uses their pooled rollouts to bootstrap the Q-function learning in online RL. Across a suite of challenging continuous control benchmarks, IPE yields an average 1.26x improvement in fine-tuning performance over naive Q-function pre-training.

</details>
